can equivalently be defined by expanding
E
as a power series and mapping
p^{m}x^{n}\mapsto 2^{-n}\sum_{r=0}^{n}\binom{n}{r}\hat{x}^{r}\hat{p}^{m}\hat{x}^{n-r}=2^{-m}\sum_{s=0}^{m}\binom{m}{s}\hat{p}^{s}\hat{x}^{n}\hat{p}^{m-s}

[
89
,
65
]
. In particular,
\OpX_{\hbar}[x]=\hat{x}
and
\OpX_{\hbar}[p]=\hat{p}
.

of a
symbol

E

[
2
]
:

\displaystyle\OpX_{\hbar}[E]:=\,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{(2\pi\hbar)^{2d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}\!\mathrm{d}\chi\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}\!\mathrm{d}\alpha\,E(\alpha)e^{i\chi_{a}(\hat{r}-\alpha)^{a}/\hbar}

(4.9)

This defines an invertible mapping between complex-valued functions on phase space
\mathbb{R}^{2d}
and operators on the Hilbert space
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
of complex-valued wavefunctions on configuration space
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
When it is unambiguous from context, we will for compactness use a hat
32
32

32

In the special case of Gaussian
states
(see Section
5.3
), we will in this paper also use hats slightly differently to distinguish the quantum Gaussian state
\hat{\tau}_{\alpha,\sigma}
from its Wigner function, the corresponding classical Gaussian state
\tau_{\alpha,\sigma}:=\mathcal{W}_{\hbar}[\hat{\tau}_{\alpha,\sigma}]=\OpX_{\hbar}^{-1}[\hat{\tau}_{\alpha,\sigma}]/(2\pi\hbar)^{d}
.
to denote the quantum operator corresponding to a classical function:
\hat{E}=\OpX_{\hbar}[E]
.
Weyl quantization obeys
\OpX_{\hbar}[E^{*}]=\OpX_{\hbar}[E]^{\dagger}

[
85
,
2
,
86
]
and the trace identity
33
33

33

This does not extend to the trace of a product of three or more operators. For that, one must deploy the Moyal product described in the next subsection.

\displaystyle\Tr[\hat{E}_{1}\hat{E}_{2}]=\frac{1}{(2\pi\hbar)^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}\!\mathrm{d}\alpha\,E_{1}(\alpha)E_{2}(\alpha)

(4.10)

when
\hat{E}_{1}
,
\hat{E}_{2}
are Hilbert-Schmidt operators
34
34

34

We expect that (
4.10
) also holds when
\hat{E}_{1}
is a polynomially bounded operator and
\hat{E}_{2}
is a Schwartz operator as defined by Keyl et al
[
90
]
. (A operator, such as a density matrix, is a Schwartz operator if and only if its Wigner function is rapidly decaying, in which case its Wigner function must be a Schwartz function
[
91
]
.) The special case where
\hat{E}_{2}
is a mixture (convex combination) of Gaussian states is demonstrated in Lemma
5.1
.
(Proposition 155 of Ref.
[
86
]
.) In particular,
\Tr[\hat{E}]=(2\pi\hbar)^{-d}\int\!\mathrm{d}\alpha\,E(\alpha)
when
\hat{E}
is trace-class.
The action of a Weyl operator on a vector
\psi\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is
[
2
]

\displaystyle(\OpX_{\hbar}[E]\psi)(x)=(\hat{E}\psi)(x)=\frac{1}{(2\pi\hbar)^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\!\mathrm{d}y\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\!\mathrm{d}p\,e^{i(x-y)\cdot p/\hbar}E\Big(\frac{x+y}{2},p\Big)\psi(y).

(4.11)

This form does not respect the symplectic covariance, but it is common and often useful for calculations.

The inverse of Weyl quantization is the
Wigner(-Weyl) transform
of

\hat{E}
, producing the symbol
E
, a scalar function on phase space:

\displaystyle\OpX_{\hbar}^{-1}[\hat{E}](\alpha)=

\displaystyle\,\frac{1}{(2\pi\hbar)^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}\!\mathrm{d}\chi\,\Tr\left[e^{i\chi_{a}(\hat{r}-\alpha)^{a}/\hbar}\hat{E}\right].

(4.12)

A slightly different object is the
Wigner function

\mathcal{W}_{\hbar}[\rho]
of a density matrix (positive semidefinite trace-class operator)
\rho
. In order to obtain relations like
\Tr[\hat{x}^{n}\rho]=\int x^{n}\mathcal{W}_{\hbar}[\rho](x,p)\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}p
and
\Tr[\hat{p}^{m}\rho]=\int p^{m}\mathcal{W}_{\hbar}[\rho](x,p)\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}p
, we must define the Wigner function to differ
35
35

35

Although this seems a bit unusual, the normalization factor
in (
4.9
)
is fixed by the desideratum that

\OpX_{\hbar}[1]=\hat{I}
(the identity operator)
while the normalization factor in (
4.13
) is fixed by the desideratum that
\Tr[\rho]=\int\mathcal{W}_{\hbar}[\rho](\alpha)\,\mathrm{d}\alpha
. Indeed,
\OpX_{\hbar}
preserves the physical units (e.g., meters for
\hat{x}=\OpX_{\hbar}[x]
), while for
\mathcal{W}_{\hbar}[\rho]
to be a probability distribution over phase space it needs to have the same units as
\hbar^{-d}
even though the operator
\rho
has no units.
from the Wigner transform of
\rho
by a factor of
(2\pi\hbar)^{-d}
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}_{\hbar}[\rho](\alpha):=\frac{\OpX_{\hbar}^{-1}[\rho](\alpha)}{(2\pi\hbar)^{d}}=\frac{1}{(2\pi\hbar)^{2d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}\!\mathrm{d}\chi\,\Tr\left[e^{i\chi_{a}(\hat{r}-\alpha)^{a}/\hbar}\rho\right]

(4.13)

For compactness we will sometimes use the notation
W_{\rho}:=\mathcal{W}_{\hbar}[\rho]
when there is no chance of ambiguity.

For a quantum state
\rho
with Schwartz kernel
K_{\rho}(x,y)=\langle x|\rho|y\rangle
,

\displaystyle(\rho\psi)(x)=\int\!\mathrm{d}y\,K_{\rho}(x,y)\psi(y),

(4.14)

an alternative and maybe more recognizable expression for the Wigner function is
36
36

36

Note that
K_{\rho}
need only be distribution valued in order
to make sense of
\mathcal{W}_{\hbar}[\rho]
as a distribution, since the oscillatory integral can be considered as a distributional Fourier
transform.

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}_{\hbar}[\rho](x,p)=\frac{1}{(2\pi\hbar)^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}e^{iy\cdot p/\hbar}K_{\rho}(x+y/2,x-y/2)\mathrm{d}y.

(4.15)

This expression is more amenable to direct computation than the equivalent expression (
4.13
), but breaks symplectic covariance by treating position and momentum differently.

4.5
Moyal star product

On the phase-space side, the

Moyal star product

\star
implements the equivalent of matrix multiplication,
i.e.,
\OpX_{\hbar}[A\star B]=\OpX_{\hbar}[A]\OpX_{\hbar}[B]
for
the symbols
A
and
B
.
The general definition is

\displaystyle A\star B(\alpha)=\frac{1}{(2\pi\hbar)^{d}}\int e^{i\beta_{a}\xi^{a}/(2\hbar)}A(\alpha+\beta/2)B(\alpha+\xi/2)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\beta\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi.

(4.16)

When
A
and
B
are analytic, it can alternatively be expressed as

\displaystyle A\star B=

\displaystyle A\exp\left[(i\hbar/2)\reflectbox{$\vec{\reflectbox{$\partial$}}$}{}_{a}\vec{\partial}{}^{a}\right]B

(4.17)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{(i\hbar/2)^{n}}{n!}(\partial_{a_{1}}\cdots\partial_{a_{n}}A)(\partial^{a_{1}}\cdots\partial^{a_{n}}B)

(4.18)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle AB+\frac{i\hbar}{2}\left\{\!\!\left\{A,B\right\}\!\!\right\}_{\text{PB}}+O(\hbar^{2})

(4.19)

where
\left\{\!\!\left\{A,B\right\}\!\!\right\}_{\text{PB}}:=(\partial_{a}A)(\partial^{a}B)=(\partial_{x}A)(\partial_{p}B)-(\partial_{p}A)(\partial_{x}B)
is the Poisson bracket and
\omega^{ab}
is the antisymmetric Levi-Civita symbol.
When one of the functions (say
A
) is a polynomial of degree
n
, then the summation in (
4.18
) is naturally understood to terminate after the
n
-th term, and one can check that it agrees with the integral definition (
4.16
) so long as the other function (
B
) has derivatives defined through order
n
, even if
B
is not analytic.

Likewise, the
Moyal bracket
is

\displaystyle\left\{\!\!\left\{A,B\right\}\!\!\right\}_{\text{MB}}:=

\displaystyle\frac{-i}{\hbar}(A\star B-B\star A)

(4.20)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\{\!\!\left\{A,B\right\}\!\!\right\}_{\text{PB}}+O(\hbar^{2})

(4.21)

which reduces to the Poisson bracket for small
\hbar
as expected.

5
Technical preliminaries

This section collects previously known results in a common notation that will be used in our proof. It also introduces the “local harmonic approximation” for quantum and classical Markovian dynamics which, in the quantum case, we were unable to find explicitly in the literature in full generality. Some readers may wish to only skim this section before reading the proof in Section
6
, returning here as necessary for clarification.

5.1
Classical limit of Lindblad dynamics: Fokker-Planck equation

We assume our system follows Markovian dynamics so the density matrix
\rho
of the system obeys a Lindblad equation
\partial_{t}\rho=\hat{\mathcal{L}}[\rho]
with

\displaystyle\hat{\mathcal{L}}[\rho]

\displaystyle=-\frac{i}{\hbar}[\hat{H},\rho]+\frac{1}{\hbar}\sum_{k}\left(\hat{L}_{k}\rho\hat{L}_{k}^{\dagger}-\frac{1}{2}\{\hat{L}_{k}^{\dagger}\hat{L}_{k},\rho\}\right)

(5.1)

\displaystyle=-\frac{i}{\hbar}[\hat{H},\rho]+\frac{1}{2\hbar}\sum_{k}\left([\hat{L}_{k}\rho,\hat{L}_{k}^{\dagger}]+[\hat{L}_{k},\rho\hat{L}_{k}^{\dagger}]\right).

(5.2)

where
\hat{H}
is the Hamiltonian and
\{\hat{L}_{k}\}
some set of Lindblad operators.

In this section we recall how to heuristically identify the classical Liouville equation (for the dynamics of a probability distribution over phase space) that is associated with a Markovian quantum system in the limit
\hbar\to 0
. We will do so by considering the quantum dynamics in the Wigner phase-space representation. Note that this is not a formal limit. Indeed even when the quantum and classical Liouvillians are close according to an appropriate metric, the evolving states will often diverge exponentially fast in time, so that similar dynamics on an identical initial state can produce very different states at later times, including flagrantly non-classical states.

The Lindblad equation (
5.1
) is
transformed to the Wigner representation as
\partial_{t}W_{\rho}=\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{q}}[W_{\rho}]
by applying
\mathcal{W}_{\hbar}
to both sides
[
56
,
76
,
75
]
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{q}}[W_{\rho}]:=

\displaystyle\,\mathcal{W}_{\hbar}[\hat{\mathcal{L}}[\rho]]

(5.3)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,-\frac{i}{\hbar}(H\star W_{\rho}-W_{\rho}\star H)+\frac{1}{\hbar}\sum_{k}\left(L_{k}\star W_{\rho}\star L_{k}^{*}-\frac{1}{2}L_{k}^{*}\star L_{k}\star W_{\rho}-\frac{1}{2}W_{\rho}\star L_{k}^{*}\star L_{k}\right)

(5.4)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,\left\{\!\!\left\{H,W_{\rho}\right\}\!\!\right\}_{\text{MB}}+\frac{i}{2}\sum_{k}\left(\left\{\!\!\left\{L_{k}\star W_{\rho},L_{k}^{*}\right\}\!\!\right\}_{\text{MB}}+\left\{\!\!\left\{L_{k},W_{\rho}\star L_{k}^{*}\right\}\!\!\right\}_{\text{MB}}\right)

(5.5)

where
W_{\rho}=\mathcal{W}_{\hbar}[\rho]
is the Wigner function of
\rho
.
We emphasize that
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{q}}=\mathcal{W}_{\hbar}\circ\hat{\mathcal{L}}\circ\mathcal{W}_{\hbar}^{-1}
is
37
37

37

As usual, “
\circ
” denotes function composition.
just a different representation of the exact quantum dynamics generated by
\hat{\mathcal{L}}
.
Using the series expression for the Moyal star product (
4.18
) to expand in powers of
\hbar
, and making use of
\partial_{a}\partial^{a}=\omega^{ab}\partial_{a}\partial_{b}=0
(by symmetry), we have
[
74
,
75
,
76
,
77
]
(see also
[
92
,
93
,
79
,
84
]
)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{q}}[W]=

\displaystyle\,(\partial_{a}H)(\partial^{a}W)+\partial^{a}\left[W\Imag\sum_{k}L_{k}\partial_{a}L_{k}^{*}\right]+\frac{\hbar}{2}\partial_{a}\left[(\partial_{b}W)\Real\sum_{k}(\partial^{a}L_{k})(\partial^{b}L_{k}^{*})\right]+O(\hbar^{2})

(5.6)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\partial_{a}\left[\left(\partial^{a}H+G^{a}\right)W\right]+\frac{\hbar}{2}\partial_{a}\left(\Omega^{ab}\partial_{b}W\right)+O(\hbar^{2})

(5.7)

where
the friction vector
G^{a}:=\Imag\sum_{k}L_{k}\partial^{a}L_{k}^{*}
and the
scaled diffusion matrix
38
38

38

Instead of
\Omega_{ab}
, many authors (e.g., Ref.
[
59
]
) have traditionally used a “localization matrix”
\Lambda_{ab}=\hbar^{-1}\Omega_{ab}=\hbar^{-2}D_{ab}=\hbar^{-1}\Real\sum_{k}\ell_{k,a}^{*}\ell_{k,b}
(or maybe with a factor of 2). Some intuition for the physical meaning of these matrices can come from noting that a superposition of two wavepackets widely separated in phase space by the vector
\alpha
decoheres at a rate
\alpha^{a}\Lambda_{ab}\alpha^{b}=\hbar^{-1}\alpha^{a}\Omega_{ab}\alpha^{b}
, i.e., the off-diagonal components of the density matrix decay like
\sim\exp(-t\alpha^{a}\Lambda_{ab}\alpha^{b})
. We choose to work with
\Omega_{ab}
rather than
\Lambda_{ab}
because
\Omega_{ab}
has no
\hbar
dependence (as we consider
L_{k}
and
H
to be independent of
\hbar
) when, as we have done, Lindblad operators are defined so products of pairs of them have the same units as the Hamltonian. This makes it easier to read off the classical limit
\hbar\to 0
.

\displaystyle\Omega^{ab}:=\Real\sum_{k}(\partial^{a}L_{k})(\partial^{b}L_{k}^{*})

(5.8)

are functions on phase space.
This shows that if we identify the
diffusion matrix
39
39

39

There have long been competing
[
46
]
conventions
[
47
]
on whether to include the factor of
1/2
in front of the diffusion term in the Fokker-Planck equation, and there is no uniformity even within authors studying quantum Brownian motion specifically. Our convention for the matrix
D_{ab}
agrees with, e.g., Diósi & Kiefer
[
94
,
78
]
and Graefe et al.
[
56
]
, but differs by a factor of 2 from, e.g., Isar et al.
[
95
]
and Dekker & Valsakumar
[
96
]
.
as
D^{ab}:=\hbar\Omega^{ab}
then the quantum dynamics in the Wigner representation take on the general form
40
40

40

Strictly speaking, one can consider the Kramer-Moyal expansion, a partial differential equation for
f
with derivatives of arbitrary power. However, by the Pawula theorem, if the expansion does not terminate by second order then it must contain an infinite number of terms in order that
f
remain positive
[
97
]
. See Ref.
[
98
]
and Sections 1.2.7, 3.3.2, and 4.1 of Ref.
[
46
]
for further discussion. In the case of a classical stochastic system that arises as the limit of a Lindblad equation, we see that the additional terms will correspond to higher powers of
\hbar
, which get small in the classical limit. We have kept track of the
O(\hbar^{1})
terms because these are the necessary ones to produce the classical state
f
that
\tilde{\rho}
will well approximate. That is, adding higher-order terms would define different
f
, but they would all be close to
\tilde{\rho}
, while dropping the
O(\hbar^{1})
terms would give non-diffusive (though generically still dissipative) dynamics that produce a
f
that is
not
well approximated by
\tilde{\rho}
.
of the Fokker-Planck equation
[
46
,
47
]

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}[f]\,

\displaystyle=-\partial_{a}[(\partial^{a}H+G^{a})f]+\frac{1}{2}\partial_{a}(D^{ab}\partial_{b}f)

(5.9)

\displaystyle=-\partial_{a}(U^{a}f)+\frac{1}{2}\partial_{a}(D^{ab}\partial_{b}f)

(5.10)

up to terms of order
O(\hbar^{2})
, where we have introduced the
deterministic drift

U^{a}:=\partial^{a}H+G^{a}
. This justifies our Definition
3.1
for corresponding classical dynamics.

It’s worth briefly noting that the Fokker-Planck equation is often written as

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}[f]\,

\displaystyle=-\partial_{a}[(\partial^{a}H+G^{a}+\partial_{b}D^{ab}/2)f]+\frac{1}{2}\partial_{a}\partial_{b}(D^{ab}f)

(5.11)

\displaystyle=-\partial_{a}(\tilde{U}^{a}f)+\frac{1}{2}\partial_{a}\partial_{b}(D^{ab}f)

(5.12)

which has the advantage
41
41

41

On the other hand, the form (
5.11
) has the advantage of taking the explicit divergence form
\partial_{a}(D^{ab}\partial_{b}f)
which is a self-adjoint operator with respect to the
L^{2}
norm. The form (
5.11
) is associated with the Itô stochastic calculus, while (
5.9
) is associated with the alternative formalism of Stratonovich.
of isolating the
mean drift
vector

\tilde{U}^{a}:=\partial^{a}H+G^{a}+\partial_{b}D^{ab}/2
(usually called simply the drift). The mean drift points in the direction of the
mean
probability flow, i.e., the direction that a strongly localized distribution will move when averaging over the diffusion:

\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\langle r^{a}\rangle_{f}=\int\!\mathrm{d}\alpha\,\tilde{U}^{a}(\alpha)f(\alpha)
.
The mean drift and the deterministic drift differ by the
spurious drift
vector
\partial_{b}D^{ab}/2=\tilde{U}^{a}-U^{a}
(also known as the noise-induced drift). For the important case of harmonic dynamics, discussed in Section
5.2
, the diffusion matrix
D
is constant over phase space, so
\tilde{U}=U
, the spurious drift vanishes, and the two forms (
5.9
) and (
5.11
) coincide. For non-harmonic dynamics, we will be most interested in the deterministic drift
U^{a}
because, as discussed in Section
5.3
, it is the direction in which Gaussians states flow under the local harmonic approximation introduced in Section
5.4
.

We emphasize that even though the diffusion term

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\partial_{a}\left(D^{ab}\partial_{b}f\right)=\frac{\hbar}{2}\partial_{a}\left(\Omega^{ab}\partial_{b}f\right)=\frac{\hbar}{2}\partial_{a}\left(\Imag\sum_{k}(\partial^{a}L_{k})(\partial^{b}L_{k}^{*})\partial_{b}f\right)

(5.13)

in the Fokker-Planck equation (
5.9
) vanishes as
\hbar\to 0
with the classical function
L_{k}
fixed, we do not generically recover closed-system dynamics in this limit: the friction vector
G^{a}=\Imag\sum_{k}L_{k}\partial^{a}L_{k}^{*}
survives.
However, when the system is closed (
L_{k}=0
), both the friction and the diffusion vanish and we recover the Liouville equation:
\partial_{t}f=(\partial_{a}H)(\partial^{a}f)=\left\{\!\!\left\{H,f\right\}\!\!\right\}_{\text{PB}}
.

5.2
Harmonic Markovian dynamics: quadratic Lindblad equation

It’s widely known that when the Hamiltonian of a closed classical or quantum system is
quadratic
in the phase space variables
x
and
p
(so
H=F_{ab}r^{a}r^{b}/2
after an appropriate choice of the origin), the dynamics can be solved exactly for all time. Such dynamics are often called ‘‘linear’’ because when the system is perturbed its response is proportional to the size of the perturbation.
42
42

42

More precisely, Hamilton’s equation of motion
\partial_{t}r^{a}=\partial^{a}H=F^{a}_{\phantom{a}b}r^{b}
is linear in the variable
r(t)=(x(t),p(t))
, so solutions (trajectories) are closed under linear combinations. Equivalently, when eliminating
p
, the second order equation
[\partial_{t}^{2}-F^{a}_{\phantom{a}a}\partial_{t}+F^{ab}F_{ab}/2]x(t)=0
for
x
is linear.
To avoid confusion between the quadratic variables and the resulting linear response, we will call these “harmonic” dynamics.

It is less often appreciated that exact solutions also exist in the more general case of a Lindbladian open systems when, in addition to a
quadratic
Hamiltonian, the Lindblad operators are
linear
in
\hat{x}
and
\hat{p}

[
99
,
95
,
100
]
. (Introducing linear Lindblad operators, rather than quadratic ones, is the natural way to generalize a quadratic Hamiltonian since the Lindblad operators appear together in pairs in the Lindblad equation.) We will call this
harmonic
(
Markovian
)
dynamics
,
43
43

43

This is often called “quantum Brownian motion” (QBM), but that terminology is sometimes also applied to dynamics that feature non-quadratic Hamiltonians or that do not strictly obey the Markov property.
where the Hamiltonian and Lindblad operators take the form
44
44

44

Or, more explicitly,
\hat{H}=F_{0}+F_{\mathrm{x}}\hat{x}+F_{\mathrm{p}}\hat{p}+\frac{1}{2}F_{\mathrm{x}\mathrm{x}}\hat{x}^{2}+F_{\mathrm{x}\mathrm{p}}(\hat{x}\hat{p}+\hat{p}\hat{x})+\frac{1}{2}F_{\mathrm{p}\mathrm{p}}\hat{p}^{2}
and
\hat{L}_{k}=\ell_{k,0}+\ell_{k,\mathrm{x}}\hat{x}+\ell_{k,\mathrm{p}}\hat{p}
.

\displaystyle\hat{H}

\displaystyle=F_{0}\hat{I}+F_{a}\hat{r}^{a}+\frac{1}{2}F_{ab}\hat{r}^{a}\hat{r}^{b},

(5.14)

\displaystyle\hat{L}_{k}

\displaystyle=\ell_{k,0}\hat{I}+\ell_{k,a}\hat{r}^{a}

(5.15)

for real number
F_{0}
,
F_{a}
, and
F_{ab}=F_{ba}
and complex numbers
\ell_{k,0}
and
\ell_{k,a}
.

The Lindblad equation (
3.3
) becomes
45
45

45

One way to simplify the manipulation is to make the Lindblad gauge transformation
\hat{H}\to\hat{H}+\Imag\sum_{k}\ell_{k,0}\hat{L}_{k}^{\dagger}
and
\hat{L}_{k}\to\hat{L}_{k}-\ell_{k,0}\hat{I}
(which has effect
F_{a}\to F_{a}+\Imag\sum_{k}\ell_{k,0}\ell_{k,a}^{*}
and
\ell_{k,0}\to 0
).

[
101
,
95
]

\displaystyle\hat{\mathcal{L}}^{\mathrm{har}}[\rho]

\displaystyle=-\frac{i}{\hbar}\bigg[F_{a}\hat{r}^{a}+\frac{1}{2}F_{ab}\hat{r}^{a}\hat{r}^{b}+\Imag\sum_{k}\ell_{k,0}\ell_{k,a}^{*}\hat{r}^{a},\rho\bigg]+\frac{1}{\hbar}\sum_{k}\ell_{k,a}\ell_{k,b}^{*}\left(\hat{r}^{a}\rho\hat{r}^{b}-\frac{1}{2}\left\{\hat{r}^{b}\hat{r}^{a},\rho\right\}\right)

(5.16)

\displaystyle=-\frac{i}{\hbar}\left[\hat{r}^{a},\frac{1}{2}\left\{(F_{a}+\Gamma_{a})+(F_{ab}+\Gamma_{ab})\hat{r}^{b},\rho\right\}\right]-\frac{1}{\hbar}\frac{\Omega_{ab}}{2}\left[\hat{r}^{a},\left[\hat{r}^{b},\rho\right]\right],

(5.17)

where
46
46

46

Note that while both the real and imaginary parts of
\sum_{k}\ell_{k,a}^{*}\ell_{k,b}
(
\Omega_{ab}
and
\Gamma_{ab}
) appear in the harmonic dynamics, only the imaginary part of
\sum_{k}\ell_{k,0}\ell_{k,a}^{*}
appears. The real part does not contribute due to the form of the Lindblad equation, and the same is true for
\sum_{k}\ell_{k,0}\ell_{k,0}^{*}
(which is real by construction).

\displaystyle\Gamma_{a}=\Imag\sum_{k}\ell_{k,0}\ell_{k,a}^{*},\qquad\Omega_{ab}=\Real\sum_{k}\ell_{k,a}^{*}\ell_{k,b},\qquad\Gamma_{ab}=\Imag\sum_{k}\ell_{k,a}^{*}\ell_{k,b}

(5.18)

are real-valued parameters. Note that the scaled diffusion matrix
\Omega_{ab}
and the
friction gradient

\Gamma_{ab}
are the real and imaginary parts of the positive semidefinite matrix
\sum_{k}\ell_{k,a}^{*}\ell_{k,b}
, so they are respectively symmetric and antisymmetric, and
\Omega_{ab}
is furthermore positive semidefinite itself.
Eq. (
5.17
) is the most general possible open-system quantum dynamics of a single degree of freedom when the Hamiltonian is constrained to be no more than quadratic in
\hat{x}
and
\hat{p}
and the Lindblad operators are constrained to be no more than linear.

The dynamical equation for the Wigner function
W_{\rho}=\mathcal{W}_{\hbar}[\rho]=(2\pi\hbar)^{-d}\OpX_{\hbar}^{-1}[\rho]
equivalent to Eq. (
5.17
) is
47
47

47

As discussed in Sec.
4.1
, the arrow on the partial derivative
\vec{\partial}
indicates that it acts on everything to the right, including
W(\alpha)
.

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}_{\hbar}[\hat{\mathcal{L}}^{\mathrm{har}}[\rho]](\alpha)=

\displaystyle\,\left[-\vec{\partial}{}_{a}\left(F^{a}+\Gamma^{a}+(F^{a}_{\phantom{a}b}+\Gamma^{a}_{\phantom{a}b})\alpha^{b}\right)+\frac{\hbar}{2}\Omega^{ab}\vec{\partial}{}_{a}\vec{\partial}{}_{b}\right]\mathcal{W}_{\hbar}[\rho]

(5.19)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,\left[-\vec{\partial}{}_{a}(\partial^{a}H+G^{a})+\frac{1}{2}D^{ab}\vec{\partial}{}_{a}\vec{\partial}{}_{b}\right]\mathcal{W}_{\hbar}[\rho]

(5.20)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,\mathcal{L}^{\mathrm{har}}[\mathcal{W}_{\hbar}[\rho]](\alpha)

(5.21)

where we have evaluated our correspondence definitions in this case of harmonic dynamics:
48
48

48

Since
F_{ab}
and
\Gamma_{ab}
are respectively symmetric and antisymmetric by construction, their index-raised forms
F^{a}_{\phantom{a}b}=\omega^{ac}F_{cb}
and
\Gamma^{a}_{\phantom{a}b}=\omega^{ac}\Gamma_{cb}
are the Hamiltonian and skew-Hamiltonian components of
F^{a}_{\phantom{a}b}+\Gamma^{a}_{\phantom{a}b}
. As will be seen in Section
5.3
,
F^{a}_{\phantom{a}b}+\Gamma^{a}_{\phantom{a}b}
controls the non-diffusive component of the dynamics for the covariance matrix of harmonically evolving Gaussian states.

\displaystyle\partial^{a}H(\alpha)=\,

\displaystyle F^{a}+F^{a}_{\phantom{a}b}\alpha^{b}

(5.22)

\displaystyle G^{a}(\alpha)=\,

\displaystyle\Imag\sum_{k}L_{k}(\alpha)\partial^{a}L_{k}^{*}(\alpha)=\Gamma^{a}+\Gamma^{a}_{\phantom{a}b}\alpha^{b}

(5.23)

\displaystyle D^{ab}(\alpha)=\,

\displaystyle\hbar\Omega^{ab}(\alpha)

(5.24)

Note in particular that the Hamiltonian drift
\partial^{a}H
and the friction
G^{a}
(and hence the deterministic drift
U^{a}
) are all linear on phase space. Furthermore there is no spurious drift
\partial_{b}D^{ab}/2
because the diffusion
D^{ab}
is constant, so the deterministic drift and mean drift coincide:
U^{a}=\tilde{U}^{a}
.

From (
5.19
) we see that this dynamical equation for the Wigner function in quantum harmonic dynamics takes the
exact
same form as a Fokker-Planck equation for classical harmonic dynamics, i.e.,

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{\mathrm{har}}\circ\mathcal{W}_{\hbar}=\mathcal{W}_{\hbar}\circ\hat{\mathcal{L}}^{\mathrm{har}}.

(5.25)

This is because, unlike the general anharmonic case discussed in Section
5.1
, there are no terms of order
O(\hbar^{2})
or higher.

5.3
Gaussian states and their harmonic evolution

We recall that the
covariance matrix
of a pure quantum state
\psi
with zero mean position and momentum (
\langle\psi|\hat{x}|\psi\rangle=0
,
\langle\psi|\hat{p}|\psi\rangle=0
) is defined as

\displaystyle\sigma^{ab}\,

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}\sigma^{\mathrm{x}\mathrm{x}}&\sigma^{\mathrm{x}\mathrm{p}}\\
\sigma^{\mathrm{p}\mathrm{x}}&\sigma^{\mathrm{p}\mathrm{p}}\end{pmatrix}=\left\langle\psi\middle|\frac{\{\hat{r}^{a},\hat{r}^{b}\}}{2}\middle|\psi\right\rangle=\left\langle\psi\middle|\begin{pmatrix}\hat{x}^{2}&(\hat{x}\hat{p}+\hat{p}\hat{x})/2\\
(\hat{x}\hat{p}+\hat{p}\hat{x})/2&\hat{p}^{2}\end{pmatrix}\middle|\psi\right\rangle

(5.26)

\displaystyle=\left\langle r^{a}r^{b}\right\rangle_{W}=\begin{pmatrix}\langle x^{2}\rangle_{W}&\langle xp\rangle_{W}\\
\langle xp\rangle_{W}&\langle p^{2}\rangle_{W}\end{pmatrix}

(5.27)

Here,
r^{a}(\alpha)=\alpha^{a}
is the phase-space coordinate function,
W
is the Wigner function of
\psi
, and expectation values are
\langle f(\alpha)\rangle_{W}:=\int\!\mathrm{d}\alpha\,W(\alpha)f(\alpha)
. More generally, when the state
\rho
is mixed and the means

\bar{r}^{a}:=(\bar{x},\bar{p})^{a}:=\Tr[\rho\hat{r}^{a}]=\langle r^{a}\rangle_{W}
are non-zero,
the covariance matrix is

\displaystyle\sigma^{ab}:=\Tr[\rho\{(\hat{r}-\bar{r})^{a},(\hat{r}-\bar{r})^{b}\}/2]=\langle(r-\bar{r})^{a}(r-\bar{r})^{b}\rangle_{W}.

(5.28)

A
Gaussian distribution
over phase space takes the form

\displaystyle\tau_{\alpha,\sigma}(\alpha+\beta)=\frac{\exp(-\beta^{a}\sigma^{-1}_{ab}\beta^{b}/2)}{(2\pi)^{d}\sqrt{\det\sigma}}

(5.29)

for a positive semidefinite covariance matrix
\sigma^{ab}
and mean
\alpha^{a}
. It is the Wigner function of the quantum state

\displaystyle\hat{\tau}_{\alpha,\sigma}=\mathcal{W}_{\hbar}^{-1}[\tau_{\alpha,\sigma}]=(2\pi\hbar)^{d}\OpX_{\hbar}[\tau_{\alpha,\sigma}]

(5.30)

that generally takes the form
\hat{\tau}_{\alpha,\sigma}\propto e^{-\hat{r}^{a}A^{ab}\hat{r}^{b}}
where
A
is a matrix determined by
\sigma
; when
\sigma
corresponds to a pure state
\hat{\tau}_{\alpha,\sigma}
(see below), the corresponding
A
diverges, and one would instead write
\hat{\tau}_{\alpha,\sigma}=|\alpha,\sigma\rangle\langle\alpha,\sigma|
where
|\alpha,\sigma\rangle
has a Gaussian wavefunction.
The Gaussian distribution obeys the mixing relation
\tau_{\alpha_{1},\sigma_{1}}\ast\tau_{\alpha_{2},\sigma_{2}}=\tau_{\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2},\sigma_{1}+\sigma_{2}}
, where “
\ast
” denotes the convolution,
f\ast g(\alpha)=\int\!\mathrm{d}\alpha\,f(\alpha-\beta)g(\beta)=g\ast f(\alpha)
. This can be extended to Gaussian states through linearity of the Wigner function:
\hat{\tau}_{\alpha_{1},\sigma_{1}}\ast\tau_{\alpha_{2},\sigma_{2}}=\hat{\tau}_{\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2},\sigma_{1}+\sigma_{2}}=\tau_{\alpha_{1},\sigma_{1}}\ast\hat{\tau}_{\alpha_{2},\sigma_{2}}
, which preserves the normalization and positive semidefinite conditions.

For our main result we will need an simple extension of the Weyl trace identity, (
4.10
), to Gaussian states:

Lemma 5.1

(Trace formula for mixtures of Gaussians)
.

If
E(\alpha)
is bounded by a polynomial and
\rho=\int\hat{\tau}_{\alpha,\sigma}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu(\alpha,\sigma)
is a mixture of Gaussians,

\displaystyle\Tr[\hat{E}\rho]=\Tr[\rho\hat{E}]=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}\!\mathrm{d}\alpha\,E(\alpha)W_{\rho}(\alpha)

(5.31)

where
W_{\rho}:=\mathcal{W}_{\hbar}[\rho]
is the Wigner function of
\rho
.

Proof.

One can directly compute

\begin{split}\Tr[\hat{E}\rho]&=\int\langle\alpha,\sigma|\hat{E}|\alpha,\sigma\rangle\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu(\alpha,\sigma)\\
&=\int\left(\int E(\beta)\tau_{\alpha,\sigma}(\beta)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\beta\right)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu(\alpha,\sigma)\\
&=\int E(\beta)\left(\int\tau_{\alpha,\sigma}(\beta)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu(\alpha,\sigma)\right)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\beta\\
&=\int E(\beta)W_{\rho}(\beta)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\beta.\end{split}

(5.32)

The second line follows from an explicit calculation of
the inner product against a Gaussian state. The key
point is that if
E
is bounded by a polynomial,
then in particular it is a tempered distribution so

\hat{E}
is well-defined as a map from Schwartz class
functions to tempered distributions, and thus

\langle\alpha,\sigma|\hat{E}|\alpha,\sigma\rangle

is well-defined.
∎

The distribution
\tau_{\alpha,\sigma}
and
\hat{\tau}_{\alpha,\sigma}
are always normalized,
\int\!\mathrm{d}\alpha\,\tau_{\alpha,\sigma}(\alpha)=\Tr[\hat{\tau}_{\alpha,\sigma}]=1
, but
\hat{\tau}_{\alpha,\sigma}
is only a pure quantum state state (
\hat{\tau}_{\alpha,\sigma}\geq 0
,
\Tr[\hat{\tau}_{\alpha,\sigma}]=\Tr[\hat{\tau}_{\alpha,\sigma}^{2}]=1
) when
\frac{\sigma}{\hbar/2}
is additionally a symplectic matrix (i.e.,
\frac{\sigma}{\hbar/2}\omega\frac{\sigma}{\hbar/2}=\omega
). More generally, these equivalent conditions on a positive semidefinite matrix
\sigma
ensure that
\hat{\tau}_{\alpha,\sigma}
is a (possibly mixed) quantum state
[
102
,
103
]
:

•

\hat{\tau}_{\alpha,\sigma}\geq 0
.

•

\sigma\geq\tilde{\sigma}
for some
\tilde{\sigma}
such that
\frac{\tilde{\sigma}}{\hbar/2}
is symplectic and positive semidefinite, i.e.,
\hat{\tau}_{\alpha,\sigma}
can be expressed as a Gaussian mixture of pure Gaussian states
\hat{\tau}_{\alpha,\tilde{\sigma}}
.

•

\frac{\sigma}{\hbar/2}+i\omega\geq 0
.

•

\nu_{i}\geq 1
, where
\{\nu_{i}\}_{i=1}^{2d}
are the Williamson symplectic eigenvalues
[
104
,
105
]
of
\frac{\sigma}{\hbar/2}
.

When
\text{rank}(\hat{\tau}_{\alpha,\sigma})=1
, the state
\hat{\tau}_{\alpha,\sigma}
is more specifically a
pure Gaussian state
(also called a “squeezed coherent state”), in which case the inequalities above are saturated.

The above demonstrates why some authors in Gaussian quantum information set
\hbar=2
, although we will not do so in this paper.
Instead, we will occasionally work with the rescaled matrix
\bar{\sigma}:=\frac{\sigma}{\hbar/2}
for convenience.

A powerful fact about harmonic dynamics is that, in both classical and quantum systems, Gaussians remain Gaussian for all time, with the centroids following the classical equations of motion and the covariance matrices obeying linear dynamics
[
95
,
79
,
56
]
(see also
[
76
,
106
]
):
49
49

49

The deterministic drift (
5.33
) describes the movement of the center of a Gaussian wavepacket; it includes the symplectic flow
\partial^{a}H
from the Hamiltonian and the friction
G^{a}=\Imag\sum_{k}L_{k}\partial^{a}L_{k}^{*}
from the Lindblad terms. Using traditional matrix multiplication, the change in the covariance matrix (
5.34
) can be written more concisely as

S=K\sigma+\sigma K^{\top}+D

where
K
denotes the asymmetric matrix
K^{a}_{\phantom{a}c}=\partial_{c}U^{a}=\partial_{c}\partial^{a}H+\partial_{c}G^{a}
. This non-diffusive (
D\to 0
) component of
\dot{\sigma}=S
arises from the (uncertainty-area-preserving) local stretching and skewing, and can be derived intuitively by looking directly at the change in the covariance matrix
\sigma
under the linear flow
\partial U
of the probability mass. The diffusive component
D
arises of course from the (uncertainty-expanding) noise.

Lemma 5.2

(Gaussian harmonic evolution)
.

Assume a Gaussian classical initial state
f(0)=\tau_{\alpha_{0},\sigma_{0}}
, centered at
\alpha_{0}
in phase space with covariance matrix
\sigma_{0}
, and let
f(t)
be a solution to classical harmonic dynamics, i.e., the Fokker-Planck equation (
3.4
) with a deterministic drift
U^{a}=\partial^{a}H+G^{a}
and diffusion matrix
D^{ab}
that are respectively linear and constant functions of the phase-space coordinates
r=(x,p)
. Then

f(t)=\tau_{\alpha(t),\sigma(t)}
where

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}\alpha^{a}(t)}{\mathrm{d}t}=

\displaystyle\,U^{a}(\alpha)

(5.33)

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}\sigma^{ab}(t)}{\mathrm{d}t}=

\displaystyle\,S^{ab}(\alpha,\sigma):=\partial_{c}U^{a}(\alpha)\sigma^{cb}+\sigma^{ac}\partial_{c}U^{b}(\alpha)+D^{ab}(\alpha)

(5.34)

with
\alpha(0)=\alpha_{0}
,
\sigma(0)=\sigma_{0}
. Likewise, for Gaussian quantum initial state
\rho(0)=\hat{\tau}_{\alpha_{0},\sigma_{0}}=\mathcal{W}_{\hbar}^{-1}[\tau_{\alpha_{0},\sigma_{0}}]
and
\rho(t)
a solution to quantum harmonic dynamics (the Lindblad equation (
3.3
) with
\hat{H}
and
\hat{L}_{k}
respectively quadratic and linear in
\hat{r}=(\hat{x},\hat{p})
), we have

\rho(t)=\hat{\tau}_{\alpha(t),\sigma(t)}=\mathcal{W}_{\hbar}^{-1}[\tau_{\alpha(t),\sigma(t)}]
.

Proof.

The classical case can be checked by direct computation.
50
50

50

Another approach is to first observe that the Fokker-Planck equation preserves the Gaussian property of distributions and then compute the time derivatives of the mean and covariance from their definition using integration by parts.

First, we recall the Gaussian derivatives reviewed in Appendix
B.1
,

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial\alpha^{a}}\tau_{\alpha,\sigma}(\beta)=\frac{\partial}{\partial\alpha^{a}}\tau_{0,\sigma}(\beta-\alpha)=-\frac{\partial}{\partial\beta^{a}}\tau_{0,\sigma}(\beta-\alpha)=-\frac{\partial}{\partial\beta^{a}}\tau_{\alpha,\sigma}(\beta)=\sigma^{-1}_{ab}\beta^{b}\tau_{\alpha,\sigma}(\beta),

(5.35)

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial\sigma^{ab}}\tau_{\alpha,\sigma}(\beta)=\frac{1}{2}(\sigma^{-1}_{ac}(\beta-\alpha)^{c}\sigma^{-1}_{bd}(\beta-\alpha)^{d}-\sigma^{-1}_{ab})\tau_{\alpha,\sigma}(\beta)=\frac{1}{2}\frac{\partial}{\partial\beta^{a}}\frac{\partial}{\partial\beta^{b}}\tau_{\alpha,\sigma}(\beta).

(5.36)

Then we evaluate the time derivative with the chain rule:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\tau_{\alpha(t),\sigma(t)}(\beta)\,

\displaystyle=\left[\frac{\mathrm{d}\alpha^{a}(t)}{\mathrm{d}t}\frac{\vec{\partial}}{\partial\alpha^{a}}+\frac{\mathrm{d}\sigma^{ab}(t)}{\mathrm{d}t}\frac{\vec{\partial}}{\partial\sigma^{ab}}\right]\tau_{\alpha(t),\sigma(t)}(\beta)

(5.37)

\displaystyle=\left[-U^{a}(\alpha)\frac{\vec{\partial}}{\partial\beta^{a}}+\Big(\partial_{c}U^{a}(\alpha)\sigma^{cb}+\sigma^{ac}\partial_{c}U^{b}(\alpha)+D^{ab}(\alpha)\Big)\frac{1}{2}\frac{\vec{\partial}}{\partial\beta^{a}}\frac{\vec{\partial}}{\partial\beta^{b}}\right]\tau_{\alpha(t),\sigma(t)}(\beta)

(5.38)

\displaystyle\,\begin{split}&=\bigg[-\frac{\vec{\partial}}{\partial\beta^{a}}U^{a}(\alpha)-\frac{1}{2}\frac{\vec{\partial}}{\partial\beta^{a}}\partial_{c}U^{a}(\alpha)\sigma^{cb}\sigma^{-1}_{bd}(\beta-\alpha)^{d}\\
&\hskip 75.0001pt-\frac{1}{2}\frac{\vec{\partial}}{\partial\beta^{b}}\sigma^{ac}\partial_{c}U^{b}(\alpha)\sigma^{-1}_{ad}(\beta-\alpha)^{d}+\frac{1}{2}D^{ab}(\alpha)\frac{\vec{\partial}}{\partial\beta^{a}}\frac{\vec{\partial}}{\partial\beta^{b}}\bigg]\tau_{\alpha(t),\sigma(t)}(\beta)\end{split}

(5.39)

\displaystyle=\left[-\frac{\vec{\partial}}{\partial\beta^{a}}U^{a}(\alpha)+\frac{\vec{\partial}}{\partial\beta^{a}}\partial_{c}U^{a}(\alpha)(\alpha-\beta)^{c}+\frac{1}{2}D^{ab}(\alpha)\frac{\vec{\partial}}{\partial\beta^{a}}\frac{\vec{\partial}}{\partial\beta^{b}}\right]\tau_{\alpha(t),\sigma(t)}(\beta)

(5.40)

\displaystyle=\left[-\frac{\vec{\partial}}{\partial\beta^{a}}U^{a}(\beta)+\frac{1}{2}\frac{\vec{\partial}}{\partial\beta^{a}}D^{ab}(\beta)\frac{\vec{\partial}}{\partial\beta^{b}}\right]\tau_{\alpha(t),\sigma(t)}(\beta)

(5.41)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{L}[\tau_{\alpha(t),\sigma(t)}](\beta).

(5.42)

To to get (
5.41
) we used the constancy of the diffusion,
D^{ab}(\beta)=D^{ab}(\alpha)
, and the linearity of the drift,
U^{a}(\beta)=U^{a}(\alpha)+\partial_{c}U^{a}(\alpha)(\beta-\alpha)^{c}
.
The quantum case follows from (
5.25
), i.e., the equivalence
\mathcal{W}_{\hbar}^{-1}\circ\mathcal{L}=\hat{\mathcal{L}}\circ\mathcal{W}_{\hbar}^{-1}
for harmonic dynamics:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\hat{\tau}_{\alpha(t),\sigma(t)}=\mathcal{W}_{\hbar}^{-1}[\partial_{t}\tau_{\alpha(t),\sigma(t)}]=\mathcal{W}_{\hbar}^{-1}\circ\mathcal{L}[\tau_{\alpha(t),\sigma(t)}]=\hat{\mathcal{L}}\circ\mathcal{W}_{\hbar}^{-1}[\tau_{\alpha(t),\sigma(t)}]=\hat{\mathcal{L}}[\hat{\tau}_{\alpha(t),\sigma(t)}]

(5.43)

∎

5.4
Local harmonic approximation

We will now define a local harmonic approximation to both quantum and classical dynamics about an arbitrary point
\alpha
in phase space. The quantum approximation is a natural extension of Heller’s semiclassical approximation for closed quantum systems
[
54
,
92
]
. In particular, see Ref.
[
92
]
for a discussion of the basic reason that expanding the Wigner function in powers of
\hbar
and truncating is often not well-behaved, while the present technique is: expand the dynamics
\hat{\mathcal{L}}
in powers of
\hbar
, truncate, and then evolve the Wigner function exactly with that. Vladimirov & Petersen considered a local harmonic approximation to Markovian open-system dynamics in (effectively) the special case of linear Lindblad operators
[
107
]
, although we are unsure if it is equivalent to our definition in that case.

In multi-index notation,
51
51

51

Recall:
\partial_{\alpha}^{n}E=\partial_{x_{1}}^{n_{1}}\cdots\partial_{x_{d}}^{n_{d}}\partial_{p_{1}}^{n_{d+1}}\cdots\partial_{p_{d}}^{n_{2d}}E
,
\alpha^{n}:=(x_{1})^{n_{1}}\cdots(x_{d})^{n_{d}}(p_{1})^{n_{d+1}}\cdots(p_{d})^{n_{2d}}
, and

n!=\prod_{j=1}^{2d}(n_{j})!
for
n:=(n_{1},n_{2},\cdots,n_{2d})\in(\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0})^{\times 2d}
.
the Taylor approximations about
\alpha
of a function
E
at an arbitrary order
m\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
:

\displaystyle E^{[\alpha,m]}(\alpha+\beta)

\displaystyle:=\sum_{|n|\leq m}\frac{(\partial_{\alpha}^{n}E)(\alpha)}{n!}\beta^{n}

(5.44)

with error

\displaystyle\delta E^{[\alpha,m]}

\displaystyle:=E(\alpha)-E^{[\alpha,m]}.

(5.45)

The Taylor remainder theorem gives the bound

\delta E^{[\alpha,m]}(\beta)\leq\frac{1}{m!}|\beta-\alpha|^{m+1}|E|_{C^{m+1}}
.

The Taylor approximation for the operator, and its error, are then naturally defined using Weyl quantization:

\displaystyle\hat{E}^{[\alpha,m]}:=\OpX_{\hbar}[E^{[\alpha,m]}],\qquad\qquad\delta\hat{E}^{[\alpha,m]}:=\hat{E}-\hat{E}^{[\alpha,m]}

(5.46)

We will in particular use the second-order approximation to the classical Hamiltonian,

\displaystyle\hat{H}^{[\alpha,2]}=

\displaystyle\OpX_{\hbar}[H^{[\alpha,2]}]

(5.47)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle F_{0}(\alpha)+F_{a}(\alpha)(\hat{r}-\alpha)^{a}+\frac{1}{2}F_{ab}(\alpha)(\hat{r}-\alpha)^{a}(\hat{r}-\alpha)^{b}

(5.48)

and the first- and second-order approximations to the classical Lindblad functions,

\displaystyle\hat{M}^{[\alpha,1]}_{k}=\OpX_{\hbar}[M^{[\alpha,1]}_{k}]

\displaystyle=L^{[\alpha,1]}_{k}(\hat{r})-L_{k}(\alpha)=\ell_{k,a}(\alpha)(\hat{r}-\alpha)^{a}

(5.49)

\displaystyle\hat{M}^{[\alpha,2]}_{k}=\OpX_{\hbar}[M^{[\alpha,2]}_{k}]

\displaystyle=L^{[\alpha,2]}_{k}(\hat{r})-L_{k}(\alpha)=\ell_{k,a}(\alpha)(\hat{r}-\alpha)^{a}+\frac{1}{2}\ell_{k,ab}(\alpha)(\hat{r}-\alpha)^{a}(\hat{r}-\alpha)^{b}

(5.50)

where we have defined the shorthand
\hat{M}_{k}:=\hat{L}_{k}-L_{k}(\alpha)
, which is just the Lindblad operator with its classical value at
\alpha
subtracted off. We have introduced
52
52

52

Eqs. (
5.51
), (
5.52
), and (
5.53
) reduce to (
5.14
), (
5.15
), and (
5.18
) in that special case where the dynamics are globally harmonic and the center of the approximation is set at the origin
\alpha=0
.

\displaystyle F_{0}(\alpha):=

\displaystyle\,H(\alpha),

\displaystyle\quad F_{a}(\alpha):=

\displaystyle\,\partial_{a}H(\alpha),

\displaystyle\quad F_{ab}(\alpha):=

\displaystyle\,\partial_{a}\partial_{b}H(\alpha),

(5.51)

\displaystyle\ell_{k,0}(\alpha):=

\displaystyle\,L_{k}(\alpha),

\displaystyle\quad\ell_{k,a}(\alpha):=

\displaystyle\,\partial_{a}L_{k}(\alpha),

\displaystyle\quad\ell_{k,ab}(\alpha):=

\displaystyle\,\partial_{a}\partial_{b}L_{k}(\alpha)

(5.52)

where in particular
F_{a}
and
F_{ab}
are the local gradient and Hessian of the classical Hamiltonian
H
.
In a closed (i.e., Hamiltonian) system,
F^{a}=\omega^{ab}F_{b}
is the classical flow and
F^{a}_{\phantom{a}b}=\omega^{ac}F_{bc}
is the Jacobian.
For later use we also define the shorthand

\displaystyle\Gamma^{a}_{\phantom{a}b}(\alpha):=\partial_{b}G^{a}(\alpha)=\Imag\sum_{k}\left(\partial_{b}L_{k}\partial^{a}L_{k}^{*}+L_{k}\partial_{b}\partial^{a}L_{k}^{*}\right).

(5.53)

It is tempting to simply start with the Lindblad equation and replace
\hat{H}
with its quadratic approximation
\hat{H}^{[\alpha,2]}:=\OpX_{\hbar}[H^{[\alpha,2]}]
and
\hat{L}_{k}
with its linear approximation
\hat{L}^{[\alpha,1]}_{k}:=\OpX_{\hbar}[L^{[\alpha,1]}_{k}]
, and indeed this would give harmonic dynamics, but it would not give the
correct
harmonic dynamics. The reason is that quadratic term in the Taylor approximation to
\hat{L}_{k}
can still contribute at the same order as the quadratic part of
\hat{H}
, to wit, the quadratic term in
\hat{L}_{k}
multiplied by the constant (zeroth order) term in
\hat{L}_{k}^{\dagger}
.

So instead, we re-write the
exact
Lindblad equation (
5.1
) as

\displaystyle\begin{split}\hat{\mathcal{L}}[\rho]&=-\frac{i}{\hbar}\left[\hat{H}+\Imag\sum_{k}L_{k}(\alpha)(\hat{L}_{k}-L_{k}(\alpha))^{\dagger},\rho\right]\\
&\qquad+\frac{1}{\hbar}\sum_{k}\left((\hat{L}_{k}-L_{k}(\alpha))\rho(\hat{L}_{k}-L_{k}(\alpha))^{\dagger}-\frac{1}{2}\left\{(\hat{L}_{k}-L_{k}(\alpha))^{\dagger}(\hat{L}_{k}-L_{k}(\alpha)),\rho\right\}\right)\end{split}

(5.54)

\displaystyle=-\frac{i}{\hbar}\left[\hat{H}+\Imag\sum_{k}L_{k}(\alpha)\hat{M}_{k}^{\dagger},\rho\right]+\frac{1}{\hbar}\sum_{k}\left(\hat{M}_{k}\rho\hat{M}_{k}^{\dagger}-\frac{1}{2}\left\{\hat{M}_{k}^{\dagger}\hat{M}_{k},\rho\right\}\right)

(5.55)

Here we are just observing the well-known fact that
\hat{\mathcal{L}}
is invariant under the replacements
\hat{H}\to\hat{H}+\Imag\sum_{k}L_{k}(\alpha)(\hat{L}_{k}-L_{k}(\alpha))^{\dagger}
and
\hat{L}_{k}\to\hat{L}_{k}-L_{k}(\alpha)
, where
\Imag\sum_{k}L_{k}(\alpha)\hat{M}_{k}^{\dagger}
is the contribution by the Lindblad operators to the Hamiltonian part of the dynamics. (Note that
\hat{M}_{k}
will be different for different choices of
\alpha
, although we do not denote this dependence explicitly; Eq. (
5.54
) holds for any choice of
\alpha
. We emphasize that no approximation has yet been made.)

With this form we can now identify a harmonic approximation
\hat{\mathcal{L}}^{(\alpha)}
to the Lindbladian
\hat{\mathcal{L}}
near the point
\alpha
, where all terms are at most second-order in the phase-space operators:

\displaystyle\hat{\mathcal{L}}^{(\alpha)}[\rho]\,:=

\displaystyle-\frac{i}{\hbar}\left[\hat{H}^{[\alpha,2]}+\Imag\sum_{k}L^{[\alpha,0]}_{k}\hat{M}^{[\alpha,2]}_{k}{}^{\dagger},\rho\right]+\frac{1}{\hbar}\sum_{k}\left(\hat{M}^{[\alpha,1]}_{k}\rho\hat{M}^{[\alpha,1]}_{k}{}^{\dagger}-\frac{1}{2}\left\{\hat{M}^{[\alpha,1]}_{k}{}^{\dagger}\hat{M}^{[\alpha,1]}_{k},\rho\right\}\right)

(5.56)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{i}{\hbar}\left[\hat{r}^{a},\frac{1}{2}\left\{\widehat{\partial_{a}H^{[\alpha,2]}}+\hat{G}^{[\alpha,1]}_{a},\rho\right\}\right]-\frac{1}{2\hbar^{2}}\left[\hat{r}^{a},\left[\hat{D}^{[\alpha,0]}_{ab}\hat{r}^{b},\rho\right]\right].

(5.57)

Our motivation to consider the second line comes from (
5.17
), and it should be compared to (
5.58
) below. It can be manipulated into this form directly
53
53

53

Recall that, per our notation,
\widehat{\partial_{a}H^{[\alpha,2]}}:=\OpX_{\hbar}[\partial_{a}H^{[\alpha,2]}]=\OpX_{\hbar}[(\partial_{a}H)^{[\alpha,1]}]=\OpX_{\hbar}[\partial_{a}H]^{[\alpha,1]}=\partial_{a}H(\alpha)+\partial_{a}\partial_{b}H(\alpha)(\hat{r}-\alpha)^{b}
.
One can expand
G^{[\alpha,1]}_{a}(\beta)=\Gamma_{a}+\Gamma_{ab}\beta^{b}
for some real-valued
\Gamma_{a}
and
\Gamma_{ab}
. One finds that
\Gamma_{a}=\Imag\sum_{k}\ell_{k,0}\ell_{k,a}^{*}
and
\Omega_{ab}=\Real\sum_{k}\ell_{k,a}^{*}\ell_{k,b}
, in agreement with (
5.18
), but that
\Gamma_{ab}=\Imag\sum_{k}(\ell_{k,0}\ell_{k,ab}^{*}+\ell_{k,a}^{*}\ell_{k,b})
, which contains the extra term
\ell_{k,0}\ell_{k,ab}^{*}
relative to (
5.18
) that vanishes in the (globally harmonic, so
\ell_{k,ab}=0
) case considered in Sec.
5.2
. This term produces a non-zero symmetric component of
\Gamma_{ab}
.
(albeit laboriously).

By construction, these dynamics are harmonic.
Note the appearance of both
\hat{M}^{[\alpha,1]}_{k}
and
\hat{M}^{[\alpha,2]}_{k}
, and also that
L^{[\alpha,0]}_{k}=L_{k}(\alpha)
is just a scalar. Unlike simply replacing the Lindblad operators in the Lindblad equation with their linear approximations at
\alpha
, this definition correctly captures the complete harmonic dynamics near
\alpha
.

On the classical side, we would like a similar approximation to the Fokker-Planck equation (
5.9
),

\mathcal{L}[f]=-\partial_{a}[(\partial^{a}H+G^{a})f]+\frac{1}{2}\partial_{a}[D^{ab}\partial_{b}f]
,
that best approximates
\mathcal{L}
in the vicinity of a point
\alpha
while preserving Guassianity in the distribution
f
.

The most general Gaussian-preserving Fokker-Planck equation is one where the drift vector
\partial^{a}H+G^{a}
and diffusion matrix
D^{ab}
are, respectively, linear and constant functions on phase space.

With maybe less initial motivation
54
54

54

Per Lemma
5.3
, a Gaussian centered on
\alpha
will, under
\mathcal{L}^{(\alpha)}
, flow in the direction of the deterministic drift
U^{a}(\alpha)
. Alternatively, one might consider flowing them along the mean drift
\tilde{U}^{a}(\alpha)
by including a linear approximation to the spurious drift
\tilde{U}^{a}(\alpha)-U^{a}(\alpha)=\partial_{b}D^{ab}/2
in (
5.61
). Although there are existing uses of the local harmonic approximation to the Fokker-Planck equation in the case of uniform diffusion (zero spurious drift), e.g., Ref.
[
108
]
, we were unable to find a clear definition in the case of non-zero spurious drift. The present definition, without the spurious drift, is ultimately justified by Lemma
5.3
.
than the quantum case, we will consider the harmonic approximation

\mathcal{L}^{(\alpha)}
to the classical dynamics
\mathcal{L}
near the point
\alpha
to be given by taking the linear approximation to the transport terms
\partial^{a}H+G^{a}
and the zero-th order approximation to the diffusion term
D^{ab}
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{(\alpha)}[f]=

\displaystyle-\partial_{a}\left[\left(\partial^{a}H^{[\alpha,2]}+G^{[\alpha,1]}{}^{a}\right)f\right]+\frac{1}{2}\partial_{a}[D^{[\alpha,0]}{}^{ab}\partial_{b}f]

(5.58)

\displaystyle\begin{split}:=\,&\left(\partial_{a}H^{[\alpha,2]}+\Imag\sum_{k}L^{[\alpha,0]}_{k}\partial_{a}M^{[\alpha,2]}_{k}{}^{*}\right)(\partial^{a}f)+\partial^{a}\left(f\Imag\sum_{k}M^{[\alpha,1]}_{k}\partial_{a}M^{[\alpha,1]}_{k}{}^{*}\right)\\
&\qquad+\frac{\hbar}{2}\partial_{a}\left[\Real\sum_{k}\big(\partial^{a}M^{[\alpha,1]}_{k}\big)\big(\partial^{b}M^{[\alpha,1]}_{k}{}^{*}\big)\partial_{b}f\right]\end{split}

(5.59)

We collect these approximations in the following definition:

Definition 5.1

(Harmonic Approximation)
.

Given the classical Markovian dynamics

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}[f]=

\displaystyle-\partial_{a}[f(\partial^{a}H+G^{a})]+\frac{1}{2}\partial_{a}(D^{ab}\partial_{b}f)

(5.60)

we define the
classical harmonic approximation
to the dynamics at
\alpha
as

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{(\alpha)}[f]:=

\displaystyle-\partial_{a}[f(\partial^{a}H^{[\alpha,2]}+G^{[\alpha,1]}{}^{a})]+\frac{1}{2}\partial_{a}(D^{[\alpha,0]ab}\partial_{b}f)

(5.61)

where
E^{[\alpha,m]}
denotes the
m
-th order Taylor approximation to the phase space function
E
at
\alpha
. Likewise, given quantum Markovian dynamics

\displaystyle\hat{\mathcal{L}}[\rho]

\displaystyle=-\frac{i}{\hbar}[\hat{H},\rho]+\frac{1}{\hbar}\sum_{k}\left(\hat{L}_{k}\rho\hat{L}_{k}^{\dagger}-\frac{1}{2}\{\hat{L}_{k}^{\dagger}\hat{L}_{k},\rho\}\right)

(5.62)

we define the
quantum harmonic approximation
to the dynamics at
\alpha
as

\displaystyle\hat{\mathcal{L}}^{(\alpha)}[\rho]

\displaystyle:=-\frac{i}{\hbar}\left[\hat{H}^{[\alpha,2]}+\Imag\sum_{k}\hat{L}^{[\alpha,0]}_{k}\hat{M}^{[\alpha,2]}_{k}{}^{\dagger},\rho\right]+\frac{1}{\hbar}\sum_{k}\left(\hat{M}^{[\alpha,1]}_{k}\rho\hat{M}^{[\alpha,1]}_{k}{}^{\dagger}-\frac{1}{2}\left\{\hat{M}^{[\alpha,1]}_{k}{}^{\dagger}\hat{M}^{[\alpha,1]}_{k},\rho\right\}\right)

(5.63)

where
M_{k}:=L_{k}-L^{[\alpha,0]}_{k}
and
\hat{E}^{[\alpha,m]}=\mathcal{W}_{\hbar}[E^{[\alpha,m]}]
.
The respective errors are denoted

\displaystyle\delta\mathcal{L}^{(\alpha)}:=\mathcal{L}-\mathcal{L}^{(\alpha)},\qquad\qquad\delta\hat{\mathcal{L}}^{(\alpha)}:=\hat{\mathcal{L}}-\hat{\mathcal{L}}^{(\alpha)}

(5.65)

Importantly, evolving
\tilde{\rho}
with
\hat{\mathcal{L}}^{(\alpha)}
is equivalent to evolving its Wigner function
W_{\rho}=\mathcal{W}_{\hbar}[\rho]
with
\mathcal{L}^{(\alpha)}
:

Lemma 5.3

(Quantum-classical harmonic equivalence)
.

Consider the exact classical dynamics (
5.60
) corresponding (in the sense of Definition
3.1
) to the exact quantum dynamics (
5.62
) with
H
and
L_{k}
twice differentiable. Then their respective harmonic approximations
\hat{\mathcal{L}}^{(\alpha)}
and
\mathcal{L}^{(\alpha)}
at any point
\alpha
are equivalent in the sense of being directly related by the Wigner transform:

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{(\alpha)}\circ\mathcal{W}_{\hbar}=\mathcal{W}_{\hbar}\circ\hat{\mathcal{L}}^{(\alpha)}

(5.66)

Proof.

By Definition
3.1
,
\mathcal{L}
is the classical limiting dynamics corresponding to
\hat{\mathcal{L}}
when
G^{a}=\Imag\sum_{k}L_{k}\partial^{a}L_{k}^{*}
and
D^{ab}=\hbar\Real\sum_{k}(\partial^{a}L_{k})(\partial^{b}L_{k}^{*})
, so

\displaystyle G^{[\alpha,1]}{}^{a}

\displaystyle=\Imag\sum_{k}(L^{[\alpha,0]}_{k}\partial^{a}M^{[\alpha,2]}_{k}{}^{*}+M^{[\alpha,1]}_{k}\partial^{a}M^{[\alpha,1]}_{k}{}^{*}),

(5.67)

\displaystyle D^{[\alpha,0]ab}

\displaystyle=\hbar\Real\sum_{k}(\partial^{a}L^{[\alpha,1]}_{k})(\partial^{b}L^{[\alpha,1]}_{k}{}^{*})

(5.68)

Then (
5.66
) can be checked through direct computation with the Moyal product (
4.17
)
using, for example,

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\mathcal{W}_{\hbar}\left[\hat{M}^{[\alpha,1]}_{k}\rho\hat{M}^{[\alpha,1]}_{k}{}^{\dagger}-\frac{1}{2}\left\{\hat{M}^{[\alpha,1]}_{k}{}^{\dagger}\hat{M}^{[\alpha,1]}_{k},\rho\right\}\right]\\
&\qquad=-\partial_{a}\left[\Imag\big(M^{[\alpha,1]}_{k}\partial^{a}M^{[\alpha,1]}_{k}{}^{*}\big)W_{\rho}\right]+\frac{\hbar}{2}\left[\Real\big(\partial^{a}M^{[\alpha,1]}_{k}\big)\big(\partial^{b}M^{[\alpha,1]}_{k}{}^{*}\big)\right](\partial_{a}\partial_{b}W_{\rho}).\end{split}

(5.69)

∎

6
Proof of Theorem
3.1

In this section we give a detailed outline of the proof of Theorem
3.1
, up to lemmas that are deferred to later sections.

6.1
Defining the Gaussian mixture
\tilde{\rho}(t)

We will define a quantum trajectory

\displaystyle\tilde{\rho}(t)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}}\hat{\tau}_{\alpha,\sigma}\mathrm{d}\mu_{t}(\alpha,\sigma)

(6.1)

for a probability measure
\mu_{t}
that we will construct to satisfy

\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\tilde{\rho}(t)=\iint\!\hat{\mathcal{L}}^{(\alpha)}[\hat{\tau}_{\alpha,\sigma}]\mathrm{d}\mu_{t}(\alpha,\sigma)

(6.2)

where
\hat{\mathcal{L}}^{(\alpha)}
is the harmonic approximation to the Lindbladian, as defined in Section
5.4
. The double integral sign is used to emphasize that the integral is taken over both phase space,
\mathbb{R}^{2d}
, and the space of all covariance matrices for pure Gaussian states, i.e., positive semidefinite
\sigma
where
\sigma/(\hbar/2)
is symplectic. We suppress the explicit integration domains in (
6.2
) and hereafter.

We now invoke our lemma from Section
5.3
, restated here for convenience, about the evolution of Gaussian states under harmonic dynamics:
See
5.2

By Definition
5.1
and Lemma
5.3
, the dynamics
\hat{\mathcal{L}}^{(\alpha)}
are harmonic and characterized by quadratic Hamiltonian
H^{[\alpha,2]}
, linear friction
G^{[\alpha,1]}
, and constant scaled diffusion
\Omega^{[\alpha,0]}
. So it follows from Lemma
5.2
and the chain rule that
55
55

55

The third equality in (
6.3
) is simply because
E^{[\alpha,m]}(\alpha)=E(\alpha)
for all
m\geq 0
.

\displaystyle\hat{\mathcal{L}}^{(\alpha)}[\hat{\tau}_{\alpha,\sigma}]=\partial_{t}\hat{\tau}_{\alpha,\sigma}=[U^{[\alpha,1]}(\alpha)\vec{\partial}{}_{\alpha}+S^{[\alpha,0]}(\alpha,\sigma)\vec{\partial}{}_{\sigma}]\hat{\tau}_{\alpha,\sigma}=[U(\alpha)\vec{\partial}{}_{\alpha}+S(\alpha,\sigma)\vec{\partial}{}_{\sigma}]\hat{\tau}_{\alpha,\sigma}

(6.3)

for all
\alpha
,
where we have introduced the abbreviations
A\partial_{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}:=A^{ab}\partial_{a}\partial_{b}=A^{ab}\partial^{2}/\partial\alpha^{a}\partial\alpha^{b}
and
A\partial_{\sigma}:=A^{ab}\partial/\partial\sigma^{ab}
.
Indeed, for the rest of this section and in Sec.
7
it will be simpler to work with implicit matrix multiplication rather than explicit indices, so we will use
U
,
S
,
F
, and
\Gamma
in place of
U^{a}
,
S^{ab}
,
F^{a}_{\phantom{a}b}=\partial_{b}\partial^{a}H
, and
\Gamma^{a}_{\phantom{a}b}=\partial_{b}G^{a}
.
Then our desired condition (
6.2
) becomes

\begin{split}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\iint\hat{\tau}_{\alpha,\sigma}\mathrm{d}\mu_{t}(\alpha,\sigma)=\iint\!\mathrm{d}\mu_{t}(\alpha,\sigma)\left[U(\alpha)\vec{\partial}{}_{\alpha}+S(\alpha,\sigma)\vec{\partial}{}_{\sigma}\right]\hat{\tau}_{\alpha,\sigma}\end{split}

(6.4)

We could integrate the right-hand side of (
6.4
) by parts in
\sigma
and
\alpha
to obtain a transport equation for
\mu_{t}
,
but we would quickly lose control of the covariance matrix
\sigma
, which can be strongly stretched by the evolution.
Instead, we observe that a component of the flow in the
\sigma
direction (toward increasing mixedness of the state) can also be interpreted
as diffusion in the
\alpha
direction.
56
56

56

When
\mu_{t}(\alpha,\sigma)
has support only on a single value of the covariance matrix
\sigma
, it is the measure associated with the Glauber-Sudarshan P function, and it has long been known that diffusive dynamics, which would increases the mixedness of a single Gaussian state, can often be re-cast as diffusion in the P function over pure states
[
109
,
110
,
111
,
112
,
113
,
94
]
. What makes the present approach distinct is that we are considering a more general distribution
\mu_{t}
supported on a large (but restricted) space of pure-state covariance matrices
\sigma
. Increasing this allowed space to include
\sigma
corresponding to mixed states may allow our main result to be generalized further, but we defer that to future work.
In particular, for
Gaussian states
\tau_{\alpha,\sigma}(\beta)=\exp[-(\beta-\alpha)^{\top}\sigma^{-1}(\beta-\alpha)/2]/((2\pi)^{d}\sqrt{\det\sigma})
,

\displaystyle\partial_{\sigma}\tau_{\alpha,\sigma}=\frac{1}{2}\partial_{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}\tau_{\alpha,\sigma},

(6.5)

as reviewed in Appendix
B.1
.
Therefore, for any decomposition
S=S_{\mathrm{D}}+S_{0}
, we have

\left[U\vec{\partial}_{\alpha}+S\vec{\partial}_{\sigma}\right]\hat{\tau}_{\alpha,\sigma}=\left[U\vec{\partial}{}_{\alpha}+S_{0}\vec{\partial}{}_{\sigma}+\frac{1}{2}S_{\mathrm{D}}\vec{\partial}{}_{\alpha}\vec{\partial}{}_{\alpha}\right]\hat{\tau}_{\alpha,\sigma}.

(6.6)

Plugging this into (
6.4
) and integrating by parts, we see that if
\mu_{t}
is a probability measure then (
6.2
) is satisfied so long as

\mu_{t}
solves
57
57

57

Recall that the arrow on the partial derivatives
\vec{\partial}
indicates that they act on everything to the right, including
\mu_{t}
.

\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\mu_{t}=\left[-\vec{\partial}{}_{\alpha}U-\vec{\partial}{}_{\sigma}S_{0}+\frac{1}{2}\vec{\partial}{}_{\alpha}\vec{\partial}{}_{\alpha}S_{\mathrm{D}}\right]\mu_{t}

(6.7)

The main question remaining is the definition of
S_{0}
and
S_{\mathrm{D}}
such that
\mu_{t}
remains a probability measure and supported on the pure NTS states.
This is deferred to Section
7
, but we state here the primary condition.

Lemma 6.1

(
\mathrm{NTS}
-preservation condition)
.

Suppose that an initial probability measure
\mu_{0}
is supported on the set

\mathbb{R}^{2d}\times\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{NTS}}(\xi)

and that

\displaystyle S(\alpha,\sigma)=[F(\alpha)+\Gamma(\alpha)]\sigma+\sigma[F(\alpha)+\Gamma(\alpha)]^{\top}+D(\alpha)

(6.8)

for matrix-valued function
F
,
\Gamma
, and
D
that are Hamiltonian, skew-Hamiltonian, and positive semidefinite, respectively, and which satisfy
D/\hbar+i\Gamma\omega\geq 0
(as is guaranteed for all Lindbladian dynamics). Suppose also that
\xi
satisfies

\displaystyle 2\hbar\xi\zeta_{\mathrm{max}}+\xi^{2}\chi_{\mathrm{max}}<1

(6.9)

where

\displaystyle\zeta_{\mathrm{max}}\,

\displaystyle:=\sup_{\alpha}\|F(\alpha)\|\|D^{-1}(\alpha)\|,

(6.10)

\displaystyle\chi_{\mathrm{max}}\,

\displaystyle:=\sup_{\alpha}\|D(\alpha)\|\|D^{-1}(\alpha)\|,

(6.11)

are the respective extremal ratios taken by

\|F\|
and
\|D\|
relative to the minimum eigenvalue
\lambda_{\mathrm{min}}[D]=\|D^{-1}\|^{-1}
. (The latter is just the maximum condition number taken by
D
over phase space.) It is also sufficient that
\Gamma=0
and
\hbar\mu\zeta_{\mathrm{max}}<1
.
Then there exists a decomposition
S=S_{0}+S_{\mathrm{D}}
such that when
\mu_{t}
is evolved according to
\partial_{t}\mu_{t}=\mathchoice{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.81778pt$\hss\resizebox{327682}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\displaystyle\mathcal{L}\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.81778pt$\hss\resizebox{327682}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\textstyle\mathcal{L}\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.81778pt$\hss\resizebox{259232}{204801}{\rotatebox[origin]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptstyle\mathcal{L}\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.81778pt$\hss\resizebox{217728}{177495}{\rotatebox[origin]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{L}\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}[\mu_{t}]
where

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.81778pt$\hss\resizebox{327682}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\displaystyle\mathcal{L}\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.81778pt$\hss\resizebox{327682}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\textstyle\mathcal{L}\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.81778pt$\hss\resizebox{259232}{204801}{\rotatebox[origin]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptstyle\mathcal{L}\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.81778pt$\hss\resizebox{217728}{177495}{\rotatebox[origin]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{L}\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}[\mu_{t}]:=\left[-\vec{\partial}{}_{\alpha}U-\vec{\partial}{}_{\sigma}S_{0}+\frac{1}{2}\vec{\partial}{}_{\alpha}\vec{\partial}{}_{\alpha}S_{\mathrm{D}}\right]\mu_{t},

\mu_{t}
remains a probability measure and supported on
\mathbb{R}^{2d}\times\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{NTS}}(\xi)
for all times
t\geq 0
.

The partial differential equation
\partial_{t}\mu_{t}=\mathchoice{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{327682}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\displaystyle\mathcal{L}\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{327682}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\textstyle\mathcal{L}\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{259232}{204801}{\rotatebox[origin]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptstyle\mathcal{L}\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{217728}{177495}{\rotatebox[origin]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{L}\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}[\mu_{t}]
preserves the positivity of
\mu_{t}
so long as the diffusion matrix
S_{D}
is non-negative. This is a consequence of the parabolic maximum principle. Although a priori one might only expect solutions
\mu_{t}
to be valued in the space of distributions, the positivity of
\mu_{t}
ensures that in fact
\mu_{t}
remains a measure for all positive times.

Thus, under the assumptions of Theorem
3.1
, we have defined a trajectory
\tilde{\rho}(t)
that is at all times a mixture (
6.1
) of Gaussian states with covariances matrices from the set
\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{NTS}}(\xi)
. We now turn to proving the
bounds (
3.22
) and (
3.21
)

6.2
Bounding
\|\rho-\tilde{\rho}\|_{\mathrm{Tr}}

First observe what may be understood as a version of Duhamel’s principle,

\displaystyle\tilde{\rho}(t)-\rho(t)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{d}s\,e^{(t-s)\hat{\mathcal{L}}}\left(\partial_{s}-\hat{\mathcal{L}}\right)[\tilde{\rho}(s)]

(6.20)

because the anti-derivative of the integrand (with respect to
s
) is
e^{(t-s)\hat{\mathcal{L}}}[\tilde{\rho}(s)]
(and
\tilde{\rho}(0)=\rho(0)
). Then

\displaystyle\left\lVert\tilde{\rho}(t)-\rho(t)\right\rVert_{\mathrm{Tr}}

\displaystyle=\left\lVert\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{d}s\,e^{(t-s)\hat{\mathcal{L}}}\left(\partial_{s}-\hat{\mathcal{L}}\right)[\tilde{\rho}(s)]\right\rVert_{\mathrm{Tr}}

(6.21)

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{d}s\,\left\lVert e^{(t-s)\hat{\mathcal{L}}}\left(\partial_{s}-\hat{\mathcal{L}}\right)[\tilde{\rho}(s)]\right\rVert_{\mathrm{Tr}}

(6.22)

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{d}s\,\left\lVert\left(\partial_{s}-\hat{\mathcal{L}}\right)[\tilde{\rho}(s)]\right\rVert_{\mathrm{Tr}}

(6.23)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{d}s\,\left\lVert\int\left(\hat{\mathcal{L}}^{(\alpha)}-\hat{\mathcal{L}}\right)[\hat{\tau}_{\alpha,\sigma}]\mathrm{d}\mu_{s}(\alpha,\sigma)\right\rVert_{\mathrm{Tr}}

(6.24)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{d}s\,\left\lVert\int\delta\hat{\mathcal{L}}^{(\alpha)}[\hat{\tau}_{\alpha,\sigma}]\mathrm{d}\mu_{s}(\alpha,\sigma)\right\rVert_{\mathrm{Tr}}

(6.25)

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{d}s\,\int\left\lVert\delta\hat{\mathcal{L}}^{(\alpha)}[\hat{\tau}_{\alpha,\sigma}]\mathrm{d}\mu_{s}(\alpha,\sigma)\right\rVert_{\mathrm{Tr}}

(6.26)

\displaystyle\leq\max_{\alpha}\max_{\sigma\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{NTS}}(\xi)}\left\lVert\delta\hat{\mathcal{L}}^{(\alpha)}[\hat{\tau}_{\alpha,\sigma}]\right\rVert_{\mathrm{Tr}}\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{d}s\,\int\mathrm{d}\mu_{s}(\alpha,\sigma)

(6.27)

\displaystyle\leq t\max_{\alpha}\max_{\sigma\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{NTS}}(\xi)}\left\lVert\delta\hat{\mathcal{L}}^{(\alpha)}[\hat{\tau}_{\alpha,\sigma}]\right\rVert_{\mathrm{Tr}}

(6.28)

where (
6.23
) follows from the fact that
e^{(t-s)\hat{\mathcal{L}}}
is a CP map and so cannot increase the trace norm,
(
6.24
) follows from dynamics (
6.2
) for
\tilde{\rho}
, in (
6.25
) we have used
\hat{\mathcal{L}}=\hat{\mathcal{L}}^{(\alpha)}+\delta\hat{\mathcal{L}}^{(\alpha)}
, in (
6.27
) we have used the fact that
\mu_{t}
is supported on
\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{NTS}}(\xi)
(which follows from Lemma
6.1
), and in
(
6.28
) we have used that
\mu_{t}
is a probability measure so that
\int\mathrm{d}\mu_{t}(\alpha,\sigma)=1
. We will now make use of the following lemma on the error introduced by the harmonic approximation, proved in Section
8.2
:

Lemma 6.2

(Error in harmonic approximation to quantum dynamics)
.

The error
\delta\mathcal{L}^{(\alpha)}
in the local harmonic approximation to the quantum dynamics acting on coherent state
\tau_{\alpha,\sigma}
satisfies

\displaystyle\left\lVert\delta\hat{\mathcal{L}}^{(\alpha)}[\hat{\tau}_{\alpha,\sigma}]\right\rVert_{\mathrm{Tr}}\leq

\displaystyle\,C_{d}\frac{\|\sigma\|^{3/2}}{\hbar}\left(B^{\mathrm{anh}}_{\mathrm{q}}[H,L_{k},\hbar]+B^{\mathrm{anh}}_{\mathrm{q}^{\prime}}[L_{k},\hbar,\|\sigma\|^{1/2}]\right)

(6.29)

where
B^{\mathrm{anh}}_{\mathrm{q}}[H,L_{k},\hbar]
and

B^{\mathrm{anh}}_{\mathrm{q}^{\prime}}[L_{k},\hbar,\nu]
are defined in (
3.19
) and (
3.20
).

Applying Lemma
6.2
gives

\displaystyle\left\lVert\delta\hat{\mathcal{L}}^{(\alpha)}[\hat{\tau}_{\alpha,\sigma}]\right\rVert_{\mathrm{Tr}}\,

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\|\sigma\|^{3/2}}{\hbar}\left(B^{\mathrm{anh}}_{\mathrm{q}}[H,L_{k},\hbar]+B^{\mathrm{anh}}_{\mathrm{q}^{\prime}}[L_{k},\hbar,\|\sigma\|^{1/2}]\right)

(6.30)

\displaystyle\leq\hbar^{1/2}g^{3/2}\left(B^{\mathrm{anh}}_{\mathrm{q}}[H,L_{k},\hbar]+B^{\mathrm{anh}}_{\mathrm{q}^{\prime}}[L_{k},\hbar,\|\sigma\|^{1/2}]\right)

(6.31)

for all
\alpha
and
\sigma\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{NTS}}(g/2)
, recalling
\sigma\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{NTS}}(\xi)
satisfy
\left\lVert\sigma\right\rVert\leq(2\xi)^{-1}\hbar
and that
B^{\mathrm{anh}}_{\mathrm{q}^{\prime}}[L_{k},\hbar,\nu]
is a monotonically increasing function of
\nu
, so (
6.28
) implies

\displaystyle\left\lVert\tilde{\rho}(t)-\rho(t)\right\rVert_{\mathrm{Tr}}\leq

\displaystyle\,t\hbar^{1/2}(2\xi)^{-3/2}\left(B^{\mathrm{anh}}_{\mathrm{q}}[H,L_{k},\hbar]+B^{\mathrm{anh}}_{\mathrm{q}^{\prime}}[L_{k},\hbar,(2\xi)^{-1/2}\hbar^{1/2}]\right)

(6.32)

6.3
Bounding
\|\mathcal{W}_{\hbar}[\tilde{\rho}]-f\|_{\mathrm{L^{1}}}

The calculation is similar to the that of the previous subsection. Again we observe a version of Duhamel’s principle,

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}_{\hbar}[\tilde{\rho}(t)]-f(t)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{d}s\,e^{(t-s)\mathcal{L}}\left(\partial_{s}-\mathcal{L}\right)[\mathcal{W}_{\hbar}[\tilde{\rho}](s)]

(6.33)

which follows for a similar reason as (
6.20
).
Then we have

\displaystyle\left\lVert\mathcal{W}_{\hbar}[\tilde{\rho}(t)]-f(t)\right\rVert_{L^{1}}

\displaystyle=\left\lVert\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{d}s\,e^{(t-s)\mathcal{L}}\left(\partial_{s}-\mathcal{L}\right)\mathcal{W}_{\hbar}[\tilde{\rho}(s)]\right\rVert_{L^{1}}

(6.34)

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{d}s\,\left\lVert e^{(t-s)\mathcal{L}}\left(\partial_{s}-\mathcal{L}\right)\mathcal{W}_{\hbar}[\tilde{\rho}(s)]\right\rVert_{L^{1}}

(6.35)

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{d}s\,\left\lVert\left(\partial_{s}-\mathcal{L}\right)\mathcal{W}_{\hbar}[\tilde{\rho}(s)]\right\rVert_{L^{1}}

(6.36)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{d}s\,\left\lVert\int\left(\mathcal{L}^{(\alpha)}-\mathcal{L}\right)[\tau_{\alpha,\sigma}]\mathrm{d}\mu_{s}(\alpha,\sigma)\right\rVert_{L^{1}}

(6.37)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{d}s\,\left\lVert\int\delta\mathcal{L}^{(\alpha)}[\tau_{\alpha,\sigma}]\right\rVert_{L^{1}}\mathrm{d}\mu_{s}(\alpha,\sigma)

(6.38)

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{d}s\,\int\left\lVert\delta\mathcal{L}^{(\alpha)}[\tau_{\alpha,\sigma}]\right\rVert_{L^{1}}\mathrm{d}\mu_{s}(\alpha,\sigma)

(6.39)

\displaystyle\leq\max_{\alpha}\max_{\sigma\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{NTS}}(\xi)}\left\lVert\delta\mathcal{L}^{(\alpha)}[\tau_{\alpha,\sigma}]\right\rVert_{L^{1}}\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{d}s\,\int\mathrm{d}\mu_{s}(\alpha,\sigma)

(6.40)

\displaystyle\leq t\max_{\alpha}\max_{\sigma\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{NTS}}(\xi)}\left\lVert\delta\mathcal{L}^{(\alpha)}[\tau_{\alpha,\sigma}]\right\rVert_{L^{1}}

(6.41)

where (
6.36
) follows from the fact that
e^{(t-s)\mathcal{L}}
(flow and diffusion) does not increase
L^{1}
norm,
(
6.37
) follows from
\mathcal{W}_{\hbar}\circ\hat{\mathcal{L}}^{(\alpha)}=\mathcal{L}^{(\alpha)}\circ\mathcal{W}_{\hbar}
(by Lemma
5.3
),
(
6.38
) follows from
\hat{\mathcal{L}}=\hat{\mathcal{L}}^{(\alpha)}+\delta\hat{\mathcal{L}}^{(\alpha)}
, in (
6.40
) we used that
\mu_{t}

is supported on
\mathbb{R}^{2d}\times\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{NTS}}(\xi)
(by Lemma
6.1
), and (
6.41
) follows from
\int\mathrm{d}\mu_{s}(\alpha,\sigma)=1
.
We will now make use of the following bound on the error in the classical dynamics introduced in the harmonic approximation, proved in Section
8
:

Lemma 6.3

(Error in harmonic approximation to classical dynamics)
.

The error
\delta\mathcal{L}^{(\alpha)}:=\mathcal{L}-\mathcal{L}^{(\alpha)}
in the local harmonic approximation to the classical dynamics acting on coherent state
\tau_{\alpha,\sigma}
satisfies

\left\lVert\delta\mathcal{L}^{(\alpha)}[\tau_{\alpha,\sigma}]\right\rVert_{L^{1}}\leq 14d^{\frac{3}{2}}\frac{1}{\hbar}\left\lVert\sigma\right\rVert^{\frac{3}{2}}B^{\mathrm{anh}}_{\mathrm{c}}[H,L_{k}]

(6.42)

with anharmonicity factor

\displaystyle B^{\mathrm{anh}}_{\mathrm{c}}[H,L_{k}]:=\left(\left|H\right|_{C^{3}}+\left|G\right|_{C^{2}}+\left|\Omega\right|_{C^{1}}\right).

(6.43)

depending only on the classical Hamiltonian and Lindblad functions through
G^{a}=\Imag\sum_{k}L_{k}\partial^{a}L_{k}^{*}
and
\Omega^{ab}=\Real\sum_{k}(\partial^{a}L_{k})(\partial^{b}L_{k}^{*})
.

By Lemma
6.3
we have

\displaystyle\|\delta\mathcal{L}^{(\alpha)}[\tau_{\alpha,\sigma}]\|_{L^{1}}\leq 14d^{\frac{3}{2}}\frac{1}{\hbar}\left\lVert\sigma\right\rVert^{\frac{3}{2}}B^{\mathrm{anh}}_{\mathrm{c}}[H,L_{k}]\leq 14d^{\frac{3}{2}}\hbar^{1/2}(2\xi)^{-3/2}B^{\mathrm{anh}}_{\mathrm{c}}[H,L_{k}]

(6.44)

for all
\alpha
and
\sigma\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{NTS}}(g/2)
, recalling
\sigma\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{NTS}}(\xi)
satisfy
\left\lVert\sigma\right\rVert\leq(2\xi)^{-1}\hbar
, so (
6.41
) implies

\displaystyle\left\lVert\mathcal{W}_{\hbar}[\tilde{\rho}(t)]-f(t)\right\rVert_{L^{1}}\leq 14d^{\frac{3}{2}}\hbar^{1/2}(2\xi)^{-3/2}tB^{\mathrm{anh}}_{\mathrm{c}}[H,L_{k}]

(6.45)

6.4
Concluding the proof

We construct
\tilde{\rho}(t)
as in Section
6.1
. We need to choose our class of NTS states such that the condition sufficiently strong diffusion in Lemma
6.1
is satisfied:

\displaystyle\begin{cases}2\hbar\xi\zeta_{\mathrm{max}}+\xi^{2}\chi_{\mathrm{max}}<1&\text{if}\ G^{a}\neq 0\,\,\,\,\text{(frictionful)}\\
\hbar\xi\zeta_{\mathrm{max}}<1&\text{if}\ G^{a}=0\,\,\,\,\text{(frictionless)}\end{cases}

(6.46)

where we recall

\displaystyle\zeta_{\mathrm{max}}\,

\displaystyle:=\sup_{\alpha}\|F(\alpha)\|\|D^{-1}(\alpha)\|=\hbar^{-1}\left(\inf_{\alpha}\frac{\lambda_{\mathrm{min}}[\Omega(\alpha)]}{\lambda_{\mathrm{max}}[\nabla^{2}H(\alpha)]}\right)^{-1},

(6.47)

\displaystyle\chi_{\mathrm{max}}\,

\displaystyle:=\sup_{\alpha}\|D(\alpha)\|\|D^{-1}(\alpha)\|=\left(\inf_{\alpha}\frac{\lambda_{\mathrm{min}}[D(\alpha)]}{\lambda_{\mathrm{max}}[D(\alpha)]}\right)^{-1}.

(6.48)

Therefore we pick NTS states characterized
\sigma\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{NTS}}(\xi=g/2)
, where we recall:
See
3.2

In other words, we choose

\displaystyle\xi=\frac{g}{2}=\begin{cases}\min\left\{(4\hbar\zeta_{\mathrm{max}})^{-1},\chi_{\mathrm{max}}^{-1/2}\right\},&\text{if}\ G^{a}\neq 0\,\,\text{(frictionful)}\\
\min\left\{(2\hbar\zeta_{\mathrm{max}})^{-1},1\right\},&\text{if}\ G^{a}=0\,\,\text{(frictionless)}\end{cases}

(6.49)

This choice ensures (
6.46
), so we have by Lemma
6.1

that the evolution of
\tilde{\rho}
given by (
6.7
) preserves the property that
\mu_{t}

is always supported on
\mathbb{R}^{2d}\times\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{NTS}}(g/2)
.
Then by (
6.32
) we conclude

\displaystyle\left\lVert\tilde{\rho}(t)-\rho(t)\right\rVert_{\mathrm{Tr}}\leq

\displaystyle\,t\,\hbar^{\frac{1}{2}}g^{-\frac{3}{2}}\left(B^{\mathrm{anh}}_{\mathrm{q}}[H,L_{k},\hbar]+B^{\mathrm{anh}}_{\mathrm{q}^{\prime}}[L_{k},\hbar,\sqrt{\hbar/g}]\right)

(6.50)

Likewise by (
6.45
) we conclude

\displaystyle\left\lVert\mathcal{W}_{\hbar}[\tilde{\rho}(t)]-f(t)\right\rVert_{L^{1}}\leq 14d^{\frac{3}{2}}t\,\hbar^{\frac{1}{2}}g^{-\frac{3}{2}}B^{\mathrm{anh}}_{\mathrm{c}}[H,L_{k}]

(6.51)

This concludes the proof of Theorem
3.1
, our main result. The proof depended on lemmas concerning the preservation of the NTS condition (Lemma
6.1
, proven in Section
7
) and the size of the error from the classical and quantum harmonic approximations (Lemma
6.2
and Lemma
6.3
, proven in Section
8
).

7
NTS Preservation

In this section we prove Lemma
6.1
, which
we now restate.
See
6.1

The proof of Lemma
6.1
relies primarily on Lemma
7.1
below, a
statement about just linear algebra which we use to define the decomposition

S=S_{0}+S_{\mathrm{D}}
. To state our decomposition we recall from Section
4.2
that a matrix
A
is defined to be symplectic, Hamiltonian, or skew-Hamiltonian when it satisfies the respective conditions
A^{\top}\omega A=\omega
,
A^{\top}=-\omega^{\top}A\omega
,
A^{\top}=\omega^{\top}A\omega
. When
A
is symmetric, symplectic, and invertible (as is true for the covariance matrix for all pure Gaussian states) it therefore satisfies
\omega^{\top}A\omega=A^{-1}
.

As the proof of Lemma
7.1
is cumbersome, the reader may prefer to first examine “Step 1” of the simpler analogous proof of Theorem 1 of our shorter companion paper
[
48
]
.

Lemma 7.1

(Decomposition of covariance dynamics)
.

Suppose
F
is a Hamiltonian matrix (
F^{\top}=-\omega^{\top}F\omega
),
\Omega+i\Gamma\omega\geq 0
is a positive semidefinite matrix with real and imaginary parts
\Omega
and
\Gamma\omega
satisfying
\Omega\geq c_{\Omega}\mathds{1}
, and
S
is the function

S(\sigma):=(F+\Gamma)\sigma+\sigma(F+\Gamma)^{\top}+\hbar\Omega

(7.1)

on positive definite matrices
\sigma
.
Suppose moreover that
\xi\in(0,1]
obeys

\displaystyle c_{\Omega}>2\xi\|F\|+\xi^{2}\|\Omega\|.

(7.2)

or, alternatively, that
\Gamma=0
and
\xi
satisfies the weaker condition
c_{\Omega}>\xi\|F\|
.
Then there exists a decomposition

S=S_{0}+S_{\mathrm{D}}

satisfying

•

“Diffusion positivity”:
S_{\mathrm{D}}(\sigma)
is positive semidefinite whenever
\sigma\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{NTS}}(\xi)
;

•

“Purity preservation”:
S_{0}(\sigma)
is symmetric and
\sigma^{-1}S_{0}(\sigma)
is Hamiltonian; and

•

“NTS preservation”:

v^{\top}S_{0}(\sigma)v>0

whenever
v
is an eigenvector of
\sigma

with eigenvalue
\lambda\leq(\hbar/2)\xi
.

Proof.

In the following proof, we will let an overline denote division by
\hbar/2
, so
\bar{\sigma}=\sigma/(\hbar/2)
.
We work with these normalized quantities because
\bar{\sigma}=\omega^{\top}\bar{\sigma}^{-1}\omega>0

is symplectic exactly when
\sigma
is the covariance matrix of a pure Gaussian state. Likewise,
\sigma\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{NTS}}(\xi)
implies
\xi\mathds{1}_{2d}\leq\bar{\sigma}\leq\xi^{-1}\mathds{1}_{2d}
.

The dynamics
S(\sigma)
generate a very general positivity-preserving linear dynamics for
\sigma
, and our goal is to break this up into a piece
S_{0}
that additionally preserves the symplectic property (“purity preservation”) and a remainder
S_{\mathrm{D}}
that is equivalent to diffusion of the state in phase space (“diffusion positivity”). The purity-preservation condition is that
\sigma^{-1}S_{0}(\sigma)=\bar{\sigma}^{-1}\bar{S}_{0}(\sigma)
is Hamiltonian, which is equivalent to the form
\bar{S}_{0}(\sigma)=\mathring{F}(\bar{\sigma})\bar{\sigma}+\bar{\sigma}\mathring{F}(\bar{\sigma})^{\top}
for some Hamiltonian matrix
\mathring{F}(\bar{\sigma})
. Intuitively,
58
58

58

Indeed, if the overall dynamics are pure Hamiltonian (i.e., if all Lindblad terms are zero), then we could just make the choice
\mathring{F}(\bar{\sigma})=F
.
we want
\mathring{F}
to include the Hamiltonian part
F
of the overall dynamics
S
plus an extra piece
Y(\bar{\sigma})
that will fight against any squeezing that risks violating the NTS-preservation condition. Therefore we look for
\mathring{F}(\bar{\sigma})=F+Y(\bar{\sigma})
,

\displaystyle\bar{S}_{0}(\sigma)

\displaystyle=[F+Y(\bar{\sigma})]\bar{\sigma}+\bar{\sigma}[F+Y(\bar{\sigma})]^{\top},

(7.3)

\displaystyle\bar{S}_{\mathrm{D}}(\sigma)

\displaystyle=2\Omega+[\Gamma-Y(\bar{\sigma})]\bar{\sigma}+\bar{\sigma}[\Gamma-Y(\bar{\sigma})]^{\top}.

(7.4)

In the frictionful case (
\Gamma\neq 0
) we make the ansatz

\displaystyle Y(\bar{\sigma})=\frac{1}{2}[{\Omega}\bar{\sigma}^{-1}-\bar{\sigma}\omega^{\top}{\Omega}\omega],

(7.5)

which is the Hamiltonian part of
{\Omega}\bar{\sigma}^{-1}
. Because
\bar{\sigma}
is symplectic,
\omega^{\top}\bar{\sigma}\omega=\bar{\sigma}^{-1}
, so
Y(\bar{\sigma})
is Hamiltonian by construction and hence preserves purity. Furthermore,

\displaystyle\bar{S}_{\mathrm{D}}(\sigma)\,

\displaystyle={\Omega}+\bar{\sigma}\omega^{\top}{\Omega}\omega\bar{\sigma}+\Gamma\bar{\sigma}+\bar{\sigma}\Gamma^{\top}

(7.6)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left[(1+i\omega\bar{\sigma})^{\dagger}({\Omega}+i\Gamma\omega)(1+i\omega\bar{\sigma})+\mathrm{tp.}\right]

(7.7)

is a positive semidefinite because
{\Omega}+i\Gamma\omega
is a positive semidefinite matrix. (Above, “
\mathrm{tp.}
” denotes the transpose of the preceding expression.) This ensures diffusion positivity. Finally, for

v

an eigenvalue of
\bar{\sigma}
with eigenvalue
\bar{\lambda}\leq\xi
, we consider

\displaystyle v^{\top}\bar{S}_{0}(\sigma)v\,

\displaystyle=v^{\top}[F\bar{\sigma}+\bar{\sigma}F^{\top}+{\Omega}-\bar{\sigma}\omega^{\top}{\Omega}\omega\bar{\sigma}]v,

(7.8)

\displaystyle=2\bar{\lambda}v^{\top}Fv+v^{\top}{\Omega}v-\bar{\lambda}^{2}v^{\top}\omega^{\top}{\Omega}\omega v,

(7.9)

\displaystyle\geq-2\bar{\lambda}\|F\|+c_{{\Omega}}-\bar{\lambda}^{2}\|{\Omega}\|

(7.10)

This is guaranteed to be positive, and thus NTS preserving, when (
7.2
) holds because
0<\bar{\lambda}\leq\xi\leq 1
.

Alternatively, in the frictionless case (
\Gamma=0
) we make the same ansatz (
7.5
) except with

\Omega\to 2c_{\Omega}/(1-\xi^{2})
, i.e.,

\displaystyle Y(\bar{\sigma})=\left(\frac{c_{\Omega}}{\xi}\right)\frac{\bar{\sigma}^{-1}-\bar{\sigma}}{\xi^{-1}-\xi}=Y(\bar{\sigma})^{\top}.

(7.11)

Again,
Y(\bar{\sigma})
is a Hamiltonian matrix and so preserves purity. Furthermore,

\displaystyle\bar{S}_{\mathrm{D}}(\sigma)\,

\displaystyle=2\Omega-2c_{\Omega}\left(\frac{\bar{\sigma}}{\xi}\right)\left(\frac{\bar{\sigma}^{-1}-\bar{\sigma}}{\xi^{-1}-\xi}\right)

(7.12)

\displaystyle\geq 2(\Omega-c_{\Omega}\mathds{1}_{2d})

(7.13)

\displaystyle\geq 0,

(7.14)

ensuring diffusion positivity for
\sigma\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{NTS}}(\xi)
because
\xi\mathds{1}\leq\bar{\sigma}\leq\xi^{-1}\mathds{1}
. Lastly, with
v
again an eigenvector of
\bar{\sigma}
with eigenvalue
\bar{\lambda}\leq\xi\leq 1
, we have

\displaystyle v^{\top}\bar{S}_{0}(\sigma)v\,

\displaystyle=v^{\top}[F\bar{\sigma}+\bar{\sigma}F^{\top}+2Y(\bar{\sigma})\bar{\sigma}]v,

(7.15)

\displaystyle=2\bar{\lambda}(v^{\top}Fv)+2c_{\Omega}\left(\frac{\bar{\lambda}}{\xi}\right)\frac{\bar{\lambda}^{-1}-\bar{\lambda}}{\xi^{-1}-\xi}

(7.16)

\displaystyle\geq 2\bar{\lambda}\left(\frac{c_{\Omega}}{\xi}-\|F\|\right)

(7.17)

This is guaranteed to be positive, and thus NTS preserving, when
c_{\Omega}>\xi\|F\|
.
∎

Intuitively, Lemma
7.1
has established that the dynamics (
7.1
) for the covariance matrix of our Gaussian states can always be reinterpreted as diffusion of the center of the Gaussian plus Hamiltonian (i.e., purity-preserving) dynamics that confine the covariance matrix to
\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{NTS}}(\xi)
. We will now make this precise.

Proof of Lemma
6.1
.

First, observe that this Lemma’s assumed form for
S(\alpha,\sigma)
and the constraint on
\xi
(given by (
6.8
) and (
6.9
), respectively) ensures that, for any fixed
\alpha
,
S(\alpha,\sigma)
satisfies the form for
S(\alpha)
and the constraint for
\xi
in Lemma
7.1
(given by (
7.1
) and (
7.2
), respectively) when taking
\Omega=\hbar^{-1}D
as expected. In other words, we can apply Lemma
7.1
freely at all points
\alpha
in phase space.

We will show that for any probability measure
\mu_{t}

evolved by
\mathchoice{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{327682}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\displaystyle\mathcal{L}\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{327682}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\textstyle\mathcal{L}\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{259232}{204801}{\rotatebox[origin]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptstyle\mathcal{L}\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{217728}{177495}{\rotatebox[origin]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{L}\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}
, (
6.1
), the total probability mass of
\mathrm{NTS}
states,

m_{\mathrm{NTS}}(t):=\iint I_{\mathrm{NTS}}(\sigma)\mathrm{d}\mu_{t}(\alpha,\sigma),

(7.18)

is non-decreasing. Here
I_{\mathrm{NTS}}(\sigma)
is the indicator function enforcing the minimum-eigenvalue condition
59
59

59

Note that everywhere we work with covariance matrices of pure states (i.e.,
\frac{\sigma}{\hbar/2}
symplectic and positive definite), so it’s not necessary to enforce those conditions with the indicator function.
for the NTS covariance matrices:

I_{\mathrm{NTS}}(\sigma):=\Theta(\lambda_{\mathrm{min}}[\sigma]-(\hbar/2)\xi)=\begin{cases}1,&\lambda_{\mathrm{min}}[\sigma]\geq(\hbar/2)\xi\\
0,&\lambda_{\mathrm{min}}[\sigma]<(\hbar/2)\xi\end{cases},

(7.19)

where
\Theta
