is the Heaviside step function.
Therefore

\displaystyle\partial_{\sigma}I_{\mathrm{NTS}}(\sigma)=\delta(\lambda_{\mathrm{min}}[\sigma]-(\hbar/2)\xi)\partial_{\sigma}\lambda_{\mathrm{min}}[\sigma].

(7.20)

We use this to
compute the time derivative of

m_{\mathrm{NTS}}(t)
:

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}m_{\mathrm{NTS}}(t)

\displaystyle=\langle I_{\mathrm{NTS}},\mathchoice{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{327682}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\displaystyle\mathcal{L}\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{327682}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\textstyle\mathcal{L}\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{259232}{204801}{\rotatebox[origin]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptstyle\mathcal{L}\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{217728}{177495}{\rotatebox[origin]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{L}\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}[\mu_{t}]\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\mathchoice{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{327682}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\displaystyle\mathcal{L}\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{327682}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\textstyle\mathcal{L}\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{259232}{204801}{\rotatebox[origin]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptstyle\mathcal{L}\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{217728}{177495}{\rotatebox[origin]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{L}\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}^{*}[I_{\mathrm{NTS}}],\mu_{t}\rangle.

To show that
\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}m_{\mathrm{NTS}}(t)
is
nonnegative it therefore suffices
to show that
\mathchoice{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{327682}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\displaystyle\mathcal{L}\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{327682}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\textstyle\mathcal{L}\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{259232}{204801}{\rotatebox[origin]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptstyle\mathcal{L}\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{217728}{177495}{\rotatebox[origin]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{L}\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}^{*}[I_{\mathrm{NTS}}]
is positive.
Here the adjoint of
\mathchoice{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{327682}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\displaystyle\mathcal{L}\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{327682}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\textstyle\mathcal{L}\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{259232}{204801}{\rotatebox[origin]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptstyle\mathcal{L}\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{217728}{177495}{\rotatebox[origin]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{L}\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}
is

\mathchoice{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{327682}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\displaystyle\mathcal{L}\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{327682}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\textstyle\mathcal{L}\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{259232}{204801}{\rotatebox[origin]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptstyle\mathcal{L}\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{217728}{177495}{\rotatebox[origin]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{L}\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}^{*}[f]:=U\partial_{\alpha}f+S_{0}\partial_{\sigma}f+\frac{1}{2}S_{D}\partial_{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}f.

(7.21)

Since
I_{\mathrm{NTS}}
does not depend on
\alpha
, the only term that
remains is the term with
\partial_{\sigma}
. Thus by (
7.20
) we have

\begin{split}\mathchoice{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{327682}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\displaystyle\mathcal{L}\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{327682}{254863}{\rotatebox[origin]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\textstyle\mathcal{L}\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{259232}{204801}{\rotatebox[origin]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptstyle\mathcal{L}\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}{\mathop{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern 0.80002pt$\hss\resizebox{217728}{177495}{\rotatebox[origin]{90.0}{(}}\hss$\crcr\cr$\hss\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{L}\hss$\crcr}}}\limits}^{*}[I_{\mathrm{NTS}}](\alpha,\sigma)&=S_{0}(\alpha,\sigma)\partial_{\sigma}I_{\mathrm{NTS}}(\sigma)\\
&=S_{0}(\alpha,\sigma)\delta(\lambda_{\mathrm{min}}[\sigma]-(\hbar/2)\xi)\partial_{\sigma}\lambda_{\mathrm{min}}[\sigma].\end{split}

(7.22)

To conclude we need to show that

S_{0}(\alpha,\sigma)\partial_{\sigma}\lambda_{\mathrm{min}}[\sigma]\geq 0
. Note that

S_{0}(\alpha,\sigma)\partial_{\sigma}\lambda_{\mathrm{min}}[\sigma]=\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\lambda_{\mathrm{min}}[\sigma+tS_{0}(\alpha,\sigma)]\right|_{t=0}=v^{\top}S_{0}(\alpha,\sigma)v

(7.23)

when
\sigma v=\lambda_{\mathrm{min}}[\sigma]v
for
unit eigenvector
v
.
Using the “NTS preservation condition” of Lemma
7.1
, it follows
that

v^{\top}S_{0}(\alpha,\sigma)v\geq 0

when
\lambda_{\mathrm{min}}[\sigma]=(\hbar/2)\xi
. Therefore

S_{0}(\alpha,\sigma)\partial_{\sigma}\lambda_{\mathrm{min}}[\sigma]\geq 0
,
so it follows that

\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}m_{\mathrm{NTS}}(t)\geq 0.

(7.24)

We note that
\mu_{t}\geq 0
is guaranteed by the “diffusion positivity” condition in Lemma
7.1
(
S_{\mathrm{D}}(\alpha,\sigma)\geq 0
) and that
\int\!\mathrm{d}\mu_{t}=1
is conserved, so we have that
\mu_{t}
is a probability measure and
m_{\mathrm{NTS}}(t)=1
for
t\geq 0
. Combining this with the “purity preservation condition” in Lemma
7.1
, we conclude that
\mu_{t}
is supported on the set
\mathbb{R}^{2d}\times\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{NTS}}(\xi)
for all times
t\geq 0
.

∎

8
Harmonic approximation error

In this section we prove Lemma
6.3
about the error in the harmonic approximation to the classical dynamics and Lemma
6.2
about the error in the harmonic approximation to the quantum dynamics. In both cases we find that the instantaneous error scales as
\frac{1}{\hbar}\left\lVert\sigma\right\rVert^{\frac{3}{2}}
, where
\sigma
is the covariance matrix of the pure state on which the dynamics act.

8.1
Classical case

Consider the classical state
\tau_{\alpha,\sigma}(\alpha+\beta)=\exp(-\beta^{a}\sigma^{-1}_{ab}\beta^{b}/2)/((2\pi)^{d}\sqrt{\det\sigma})
, a Gaussian probability distribution over phase space centered on
\alpha
that’s equal to the Wigner function of the quantum state
\hat{\tau}_{\alpha,\sigma}=|\alpha,\sigma\rangle\langle\alpha,\sigma|
. We want to bound the error

\displaystyle\left\lVert(\mathcal{L}-\mathcal{L}^{(\alpha)})[\tau_{\alpha,\sigma}]\right\rVert_{L^{1}}=\left\lVert\delta\mathcal{L}^{(\alpha)}[\tau_{\alpha,\sigma}]\right\rVert_{L^{1}}

(8.1)

due to approximating the true classical dynamics, generated by

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}[f]=

\displaystyle-\left(\partial^{a}H\right)(\partial_{a}f)-\partial_{a}\left[f\Imag\sum_{k}L_{k}\partial^{a}L_{k}^{*}\right]+\frac{\hbar}{2}\partial_{a}\left[(\partial_{b}f)\Real\sum_{k}(\partial^{a}L_{k})(\partial^{b}L_{k}^{*})\right]

(8.2)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\left(\partial^{a}H\right)(\partial_{a}f)-\partial_{a}\left[fG{}^{a}\right]+\frac{\hbar}{2}\partial_{a}\left[(\partial_{b}f)\Omega^{ab}\right]

(8.3)

acting on the state
\tau_{\alpha,\sigma}
, with the linearization
\mathcal{L}^{(\alpha)}
given by (see Section
5.4
)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{(\alpha)}[f]=

\displaystyle-\partial_{a}\left[f\left(\partial^{a}H^{[\alpha,2]}+G^{[\alpha,1]}{}^{a}\right)\right]+\frac{\hbar}{2}\Omega^{[\alpha,0]ab}\partial_{a}\partial_{b}f

(8.4)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\partial_{a}\left[f\left(\partial^{a}H^{[\alpha,2]}+\Imag\sum_{k}\left(L_{k}(\alpha)\partial^{a}M^{[\alpha,2]}_{k}{}^{*}+M^{[\alpha,1]}_{k}\partial^{a}M^{[\alpha,1]}_{k}{}^{*}\right)\right)\right]

(8.5)

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{\hbar}{2}\left[\Real\sum_{k}(\partial^{a}M^{[\alpha,1]}_{k})(\partial^{b}M^{[\alpha,1]}_{k}{}^{*})\right](\partial_{a}\partial_{b}f)

(8.6)

where we recall
M_{k}=L_{k}-L_{k}(\alpha)
,
G_{a}=\Imag\sum_{k}L_{k}\partial_{a}L_{k}^{*}
, and
\Omega^{ab}=\Real\sum_{k}(\partial^{a}M_{k})^{*}(\partial^{b}M_{k})
.
(As described earlier,
E^{[\alpha,m]}
denotes the
m
-th order Taylor approximation to
E
at
\alpha
.)
The main result of this section will be to prove Lemma
6.3
, which we now re-state:

See
6.3

Proof.

In the proof below, because we are after an explicit constant in (
6.43
), we compute the Gaussian integrals explicitly.

We start by observing the following identity for the derivative of the Gaussian state
\tau_{\alpha,\sigma}
:

\displaystyle\begin{split}(\partial_{c}\tau_{\alpha,\sigma})(\alpha+\beta)&=\frac{\partial}{\partial\beta^{c}}\frac{\exp\left[-\beta^{a}\sigma^{-1}_{ab}\beta^{b}/2\right]}{(2\pi)^{d}\sqrt{\det\sigma}}\\
&=-\sigma^{-1}_{cd}\beta^{d}\frac{\exp\left[-\beta^{a}\sigma^{-1}_{ab}\beta^{b}/2\right]}{(2\pi)^{d}\sqrt{\det\sigma}}\\
&=-m_{c}\tau_{\alpha,\sigma}(\alpha+\beta),\\
\end{split}

(8.7)

where

m_{a}:=\sigma^{-1}_{ab}\beta^{b}
.
Then expanding
\mathcal{L}[f]-\mathcal{L}^{(\alpha)}[f]
using
the expressions in (
8.2
) and (
8.4
) with
f=\tau_{\alpha,\sigma}
, and using the triangle inequality,

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\left\lVert\delta\mathcal{L}^{(\alpha)}[\tau_{\alpha,\sigma}]\right\rVert_{L^{1}}\leq\left\lVert\tau_{\alpha,\sigma}m^{a}(\partial_{a}\delta H^{[\alpha,2]})\right\rVert_{L^{1}}+\left\lVert\tau_{\alpha,\sigma}m^{a}\delta G^{[\alpha,1]}_{a}\right\rVert_{L^{1}}+\left\lVert\tau_{\alpha,\sigma}\partial^{a}\delta G^{[\alpha,1]}_{a}\right\rVert_{L^{1}}\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+\frac{\hbar}{2}\left\lVert\tau_{\alpha,\sigma}m_{a}m_{b}\delta\Omega^{[\alpha,0]ab}\right\rVert_{L^{1}}\end{split}

(8.8)

where
\delta E^{[\alpha,m]}=E-E^{[\alpha,m]}
denotes error on the
m
-th order Taylor approximation to
E
.

Starting with the first term on the right-hand side, we use Taylor’s theorem on the derivative of
\delta H^{(\alpha)}
:

\displaystyle\begin{split}(\partial_{d}\delta H^{[\alpha,2]})(\alpha+\beta)&=(\partial_{d}H)(\alpha+\beta)-(\partial_{d}H^{[\alpha,2]})(\alpha+\beta)\\
&=\frac{1}{2!}\beta^{a}\beta^{b}(\partial_{a}\partial_{b}\partial_{d}H)(\alpha+z\beta)\end{split}

(8.9)

for some (
\beta
-dependent) choice of
z\in[0,1]
.
We can bound this derivative

\displaystyle\begin{split}\left|m^{a}\partial_{a}\delta H^{(\alpha)}(\alpha)\right|^{2}=\frac{1}{4}\left|m^{a}\beta^{b}\beta^{c}(\partial_{a}\partial_{b}\partial_{c}H)(\alpha)\right|^{2}\leq\frac{1}{4}|m|^{2}|\beta|^{4}\left|H\right|_{C^{3}}^{2}.\end{split}

(8.10)

using the
C^{3}
seminorm defined in (
4.8
),

\displaystyle\begin{split}\left|H\right|_{C^{3}}:=\sup_{\alpha}\left\|\nabla^{3}H(\alpha)\right\|=\sup_{\alpha}\sup_{\|\beta_{i}\|=1}|\beta_{1}^{a}\beta_{2}^{b}\beta_{3}^{c}\partial_{a}\partial_{b}\partial_{c}H(\alpha)|\end{split}

(8.11)

which gives a global upper bound on the third derivatives of the classical Hamiltonian function. Likewise norms like

\displaystyle|m|^{2}=m^{a}\mathds{1}_{ab}m^{b}=\beta^{a}(\sigma^{-1})_{a}^{\phantom{a}c}\mathds{1}_{cd}(\sigma^{-1})^{d}_{\phantom{a}b}\beta^{b}=\beta^{a}\sigma^{-2}_{ab}\beta^{b}=(\beta^{\top}\sigma^{-2}\beta)

(8.12)

are computed not with the symplectic form but with the Euclidean inner product.
60
60

60

Note that, physically, this inner product depends on our choice of units. See Appendix
A
.

We can then perform the Gaussian integral

\displaystyle\left\lVert\tau_{\alpha,\sigma}|m||\beta|^{2}\right\rVert_{L^{1}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert\tau_{\alpha,\sigma}^{1/2}\right\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}\left\lVert\tau_{\alpha,\sigma}^{1/2}|m||\beta|^{2}\right\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}

(8.13)

\displaystyle=\int\!\mathrm{d}\beta\,\tau_{\alpha,\sigma}(\alpha+\beta)\left(\beta^{\top}\sigma^{-2}\beta\right)|\beta|^{4}

(8.14)

\displaystyle=\Tr[\sigma^{-1}](\Tr\sigma)^{2}+2\Tr[\sigma^{-1}]\Tr[\sigma^{2}]+4\Tr[\sigma]\Tr[\sigma^{0}]+8\Tr[\sigma]

(8.15)

\displaystyle\leq\hbar^{-2}[2^{5}(d+1)d^{2}]\|\sigma\|^{3}+[2^{4}(d+1)d]\|\sigma\|

(8.16)

\displaystyle\leq\hbar^{-2}[2^{5}(d+2)(d+1)d]\|\sigma\|^{3}

(8.17)

\displaystyle\leq\hbar^{-2}[2^{6}3d^{3}]\|\sigma\|^{3}

(8.18)

where in the first line we have used Cauchy-Schwartz inequality,
61
61

61

More specifically, the Cauchy-Schwartz is
|(v,w)|^{2}\leq(v,v)(w,w)
and we choose
v=\sqrt{\tau_{\alpha,\sigma}}
and
w=\sqrt{\tau_{\alpha,\sigma}}|\beta|
so
\|vw\|^{2}_{L^{1}}=[\int\!\mathrm{d}\beta\,|v(\beta)w(\beta)|]^{2}=|(v,w)|^{2}\leq(v,v)(w,w)=\int\!\mathrm{d}\beta\,|w(\beta)|^{2}
because
(v,v)=\int\!\mathrm{d}\beta\,\tau_{\alpha,\sigma}(\beta)=1
.

and in the last two lines
we have used
d\geq 1
and the fact that, by the uncertainty principle,
\|\sigma\|\geq\hbar/2
because
\sigma
is the covariance matrix of a pure Gaussian state.
(The Gaussian integrals we use in this section are recalled in Appendix
B.2
.)
Thus we can bound the first term on the right-hand side of (
8.8
):

\displaystyle\left\lVert\tau_{\alpha,\sigma}m^{a}(\partial_{a}\delta H^{[\alpha,2]})\right\rVert_{L^{1}}

\displaystyle\leq\hbar^{-1}\left|H\right|_{C^{3}}[2^{4}3d^{3}]^{1/2}\|\sigma\|^{3/2}

(8.19)

Now we bound the rest of the terms in (
8.8
) in basically the same way. Using Taylor’s theorem again, we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}\delta G^{[\alpha,1]}_{a}(\alpha+\beta)&=\frac{1}{2!}\beta^{b}\beta^{c}\partial_{b}\partial_{c}G_{a}(\alpha+z\beta)\\
\partial^{a}\delta G^{[\alpha,1]}_{a}(\alpha+\beta)&=\delta(\partial^{a}G_{a})^{[\alpha,0]}(\alpha+\beta)=\beta^{c}\partial_{c}\partial^{a}G_{a}(\alpha+z\beta)\\
\delta\Omega^{[\alpha,0]ab}(\alpha+\beta)&=\beta^{c}\partial_{c}\Omega^{ab}(\alpha+z\beta)\end{split}

(8.20)

where on each line
z\in[0,1]
depends separately on
\beta
on that line.
Then applying the
C^{k}
seminorms as before

\displaystyle\begin{split}|m^{a}\delta G^{[\alpha,1]}_{a}(\alpha+\beta)|&\leq|m||\beta|^{2}\left|G\right|_{C^{2}}/2!\\
|\partial^{a}\delta G^{[\alpha,1]}_{a}(\alpha+\beta)|&\leq|\beta|\left|G\right|_{C^{2}}\\
|m_{a}m_{b}\delta\Omega^{[\alpha,0]ab}(\alpha+\beta)|&\leq|m|^{2}|\beta|\left|\Omega\right|_{C^{1}}\end{split}

(8.21)

where
analogously to
\left|H\right|_{C^{3}}
in (
8.11
) we defined

\displaystyle\left|G\right|_{C^{2}}:=

\displaystyle\sup_{\alpha}\left\|\nabla^{2}G(\alpha)\right\|=\sup_{\alpha}\sup_{\|\beta_{i}\|=1}|\beta_{1}^{a}\beta_{2}^{b}\beta_{3}^{c}\partial_{a}\partial_{b}G_{c}|

(8.22)

\displaystyle\begin{split}=&\sup_{\alpha}\sup_{\|\beta_{i}\|=1}\Bigg|\beta_{1}^{a}\beta_{2}^{b}\beta_{3}^{c}\Imag\sum_{k}\Big[(\partial_{a}\partial_{b}L_{k})^{*}(\alpha)(\partial_{c}L_{k})(\alpha)+2(\partial_{a}L_{k})^{*}(\partial_{b}\partial_{c}L_{k})(\alpha)\\
&\hskip 220.00034pt+(L_{k})^{*}(\alpha)(\partial_{a}\partial_{b}\partial_{c}L_{k})(\alpha)\Big]\Bigg|,\end{split}

(8.23)

\displaystyle\left|\Omega\right|_{C^{1}}:=

\displaystyle\sup_{\alpha}\left\|\nabla^{1}\Omega(\alpha)\right\|=\sup_{\alpha}\sup_{\|\beta_{i}\|=1}|\beta_{1}^{a}\beta_{2}^{b}\beta_{3}^{c}\partial_{a}\Omega_{bc}|

(8.24)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sup_{\alpha}\sup_{\|\beta_{i}\|=1}\left|\beta_{1}^{a}\beta_{2}^{b}\beta_{3}^{c}\Real\sum_{k}\Big[(\partial_{a}\partial_{b}L_{k})^{*}(\partial_{c}L_{k})+(\partial_{b}L_{k})^{*}(\alpha)(\partial_{a}\partial_{c}L_{k})(\alpha)\Big]\right|.

(8.25)

(Seminorms are reviewed in Section
4.3
.)

We again use Cauchy-Schwartz (see footnote
61
) to get

\displaystyle\begin{split}\left\lVert\tau_{\alpha,\sigma}|\beta|\right\rVert_{L^{1}}^{2}&\leq\int\!\mathrm{d}\beta\,\tau_{\alpha,\sigma}(\alpha+\beta)|\beta|^{2}=\Tr[\sigma]\leq(2d)\|\sigma\|\leq\hbar^{-2}(8d)\|\sigma\|^{3}\\
\left\lVert\tau_{\alpha,\sigma}|m|^{2}|\beta|\right\rVert_{L^{1}}^{2}&\leq\int\!\mathrm{d}\beta\,\tau_{\alpha,\sigma}(\alpha+\beta)|m|^{4}|\beta|^{2}\\
&=(\Tr[\sigma^{-1}])^{2}\Tr[\sigma]+4\Tr[\sigma^{-1}]\Tr[\sigma^{0}]+\Tr[\sigma]\Tr[\sigma^{-2}]+8\Tr[\sigma^{-1}]\\
&\leq\hbar^{-2}[2^{4}(2d^{2}+5d+4)d]\|\sigma\|^{3}\\
&\leq\hbar^{-2}[2^{4}11d^{3}]\|\sigma\|^{3}\end{split}

(8.26)

where the relevant Gaussian integrals are recalled in Appendix
B.2
.

Pulling this all together we can now bound the error (
8.8
) on the classical harmonic approximation for a Gaussian state:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\left\lVert\delta\mathcal{L}^{(\alpha)}[\tau_{\alpha,\sigma}]\right\rVert_{L^{1}}&\leq\frac{\|\sigma\|^{3/2}}{\hbar}\Big[(\left|H\right|_{C^{3}}+\left|G\right|_{C^{2}})\left[2^{4}3d^{3}\right]^{1/2}+\left|G\right|_{C^{2}}\left[8d\right]^{1/2}+(\hbar/2)\left|\Omega\right|_{C^{1}}\left[2^{4}11d^{3}\right]^{1/2}\Big]\\
&\leq\frac{\|\sigma\|^{3/2}}{\hbar}B^{\mathrm{anh}}_{\mathrm{c}}[H,L_{k}]\end{split}

(8.27)

where

\displaystyle B^{\mathrm{anh}}_{\mathrm{c}}[H,L_{k}]=14d^{3/2}\left(\left|H\right|_{C^{3}}+\left|G\right|_{C^{2}}+\left|\Omega\right|_{C^{1}}\right).

(8.28)

is a measure of the anharmonicity of the classical Hamiltonian and Lindblad functions
H
and
L_{k}
(and in particular does not depent on
\hbar
or
\sigma
).
It may seem unusual that
B^{\mathrm{anh}}_{\mathrm{c}}
depends on
\left|G\right|_{C^{2}}
, which in turn can diverge if the Lindblad functions
L_{k}
becomes arbitrarily large without its third derivative vanishing (see the last term in (
8.23
)). However, this may be expected due to the fact that the overall dynamics are
not
invariant under
L_{k}(\alpha)\to L_{k}(\alpha)+L_{0}
(in contrast to the case of the Hamiltonian shift
H(\alpha)\to H(\alpha)+H_{0}
, which does preserve the dynamics).

That concludes the proof of Lemma
6.3
.
∎

8.2
Quantum case

We recall from (
5.54
) that, for any
\alpha
, we can express the exact quantum dynamics with
\hat{M}_{k}:=\hat{L}_{k}-L_{k}(\alpha)
as

\displaystyle\hat{\mathcal{L}}[\rho]

\displaystyle=-\frac{i}{\hbar}\left[\hat{H}+\Imag\sum_{k}L_{k}(\alpha)\hat{M}_{k}^{\dagger},\rho\right]+\frac{1}{\hbar}\sum_{k}\left(\hat{M}_{k}\rho\hat{M}_{k}^{\dagger}-\frac{1}{2}\left\{\hat{M}_{k}^{\dagger}\hat{M}_{k},\rho\right\}\right)

(8.29)

We emphasize that although we express
\hat{\mathcal{L}}
above in terms of
\alpha
, the object is independent of
\alpha
. As discussed in Section
5.4
, the linearized dynamics
\hat{\mathcal{L}}^{(\alpha)}
at
\alpha
, which of course
do
depend on
\alpha
, are

\displaystyle\hat{\mathcal{L}}^{(\alpha)}[\rho]

\displaystyle=-\frac{i}{\hbar}\left[\hat{H}^{[\alpha,2]}+\Imag\sum_{k}L_{k}(\alpha)\hat{M}^{[\alpha,2]}_{k}{}^{\dagger},\rho\right]+\frac{1}{\hbar}\sum_{k}\left(\hat{M}^{[\alpha,1]}_{k}\rho\hat{M}^{[\alpha,1]}_{k}{}^{\dagger}-\frac{1}{2}\left\{\hat{M}^{[\alpha,1]}_{k}{}^{\dagger}\hat{M}^{[\alpha,1]}_{k},\rho\right\}\right)

(8.30)

We want a global (independent of
\alpha
) bound on the error

\displaystyle\left\lVert(\hat{\mathcal{L}}-\hat{\mathcal{L}}^{(\alpha)})[\hat{\tau}_{\alpha,\sigma}]\right\rVert_{\mathrm{Tr}}=\left\lVert\delta\hat{\mathcal{L}}^{(\alpha)}[\hat{\tau}_{\alpha,\sigma}]\right\rVert_{\mathrm{Tr}}.

(8.31)

for the Gaussian state
\hat{\tau}_{\alpha,\sigma}=|\alpha,\sigma\rangle\langle\alpha,\sigma|
with covariance matrix
\sigma
located at
\alpha
. We can now re-state the lemma concerning the harmonic approximation which we prove in this subsection:

See
6.2

Our proof of Lemma
6.2
is more
more involved than the classical case (Lemma
6.3
) in the previous subsection. We use many variations of the same basic trick, and we expect (for reasons related to the above discussion) that a more abstract understanding of how Lindbladians and Liouvillians are Taylor approximated would make tighter, simpler bounds possible.

In the proof of Lemma
6.2
we will not keep track of the constant
C_{d}
, choosing instead to use the notation
A{\,\lesssim\,}B
to mean that
A\leq CB
for some constant
C
depending only on dimension. The implicit constant can change from line to line.

Proof of Lemma
6.2
.

We have

\displaystyle\begin{split}\delta\hat{\mathcal{L}}^{(\alpha)}[\hat{\tau}_{\alpha,\sigma}]&=\hat{\mathcal{L}}[\hat{\tau}_{\alpha,\sigma}]-\hat{\mathcal{L}}^{(\alpha)}[\hat{\tau}_{\alpha,\sigma}]\\
&=\frac{-i}{\hbar}\left[\delta\hat{H}^{(\alpha)}+\Imag\sum_{k}L_{k}(\alpha)\delta\hat{M}^{[\alpha,2]}_{k},|\alpha,\sigma\rangle\langle\alpha,\sigma|\right]\\
&\qquad+\frac{1}{\hbar}\sum_{k}\Bigg[\delta\hat{M}^{[\alpha,1]}_{k}\mathinner{|{\alpha,\sigma}\rangle}\mathinner{\langle{\alpha,\sigma}|}\hat{M}^{[\alpha,1]}_{k}{}^{\dagger}+\hat{M}^{[\alpha,1]}_{k}\mathinner{|{\alpha,\sigma}\rangle}\mathinner{\langle{\alpha,\sigma}|}\delta\hat{M}^{[\alpha,1]}_{k}{}^{\dagger}\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+\delta\hat{M}^{[\alpha,1]}_{k}\mathinner{|{\alpha,\sigma}\rangle}\mathinner{\langle{\alpha,\sigma}|}\delta\hat{M}^{[\alpha,1]}_{k}{}^{\dagger}-\frac{1}{2}\left\{\delta\hat{M}^{[\alpha,1]}_{k}{}^{\dagger}\hat{M}^{[\alpha,1]}_{k},\mathinner{|{\alpha,\sigma}\rangle}\mathinner{\langle{\alpha,\sigma}|}\right\}\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad-\frac{1}{2}\left\{\hat{M}^{[\alpha,1]}_{k}{}^{\dagger}\delta\hat{M}^{[\alpha,1]}_{k},\mathinner{|{\alpha,\sigma}\rangle}\mathinner{\langle{\alpha,\sigma}|}\right\}-\frac{1}{2}\left\{\delta\hat{M}^{[\alpha,1]}_{k}{}^{\dagger}\delta\hat{M}^{[\alpha,1]}_{k},\mathinner{|{\alpha,\sigma}\rangle}\mathinner{\langle{\alpha,\sigma}|}\right\}\Bigg]\end{split}

(8.32)

For any state vectors
\mathinner{|{\psi}\rangle}
and
\mathinner{|{\phi}\rangle}
, we have
\left\lVert\mathinner{|{\psi}\rangle}\mathinner{\langle{\phi}|}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{Tr}}^{2}=\Tr[(\mathinner{|{\psi}\rangle}\!\langle\phi|\phi\rangle\!\mathinner{\langle{\psi}|})^{1/2}]^{2}=\langle\psi|\psi\rangle\langle\phi|\phi\rangle=\|\psi\|^{2}\|\phi\|^{2}
, where the unlabeled norms denote Hilbert space norm.
Therefore,
62
62

62

For a tighter bound, but only by a constant, we can compute more exactly.
Note
\left\lVert|v\rangle\langle w|-|w\rangle\langle v|\right\rVert=\left\lVert v\right\rVert\left\lVert w\right\rVert(1-|\langle v|w\rangle|^{2}/(\left\lVert v\right\rVert\left\lVert w\right\rVert))^{1/2}
, so
\|[\hat{H},\hat{\tau}_{\alpha,\sigma}]\|=\|\hat{H}|\alpha\rangle\langle\alpha|-|\alpha\rangle\langle\alpha|\hat{H}\|=\|\hat{H}|\alpha\rangle\|(1-\langle\alpha|\hat{H}|\alpha\rangle^{2}/\langle\alpha|\hat{H}^{2}|\alpha\rangle)^{1/2}=(\langle\alpha|\hat{H}^{2}|\alpha\rangle-\langle\alpha|\hat{H}|\alpha\rangle^{2})^{1/2}=(\textrm{Var}(\hat{H})_{|\alpha\rangle})^{1/2}
. And also
\||v\rangle\langle w|-|w\rangle\langle v|\|_{1}=2\||v\rangle\langle w|-|w\rangle\langle v|\|
.

\displaystyle\begin{split}\left\lVert\delta\hat{\mathcal{L}}^{(\alpha)}[\hat{\tau}_{\alpha,\sigma}]\right\rVert_{\mathrm{Tr}}\leq&\frac{2}{\hbar}\left\lVert\delta\hat{H}^{[\alpha,2]}|\alpha,\sigma\rangle\right\rVert+\frac{2}{\hbar}\sum_{k}L_{k}(\alpha)\left\lVert\delta\hat{M}^{[\alpha,2]}_{k}|\alpha,\sigma\rangle\right\rVert\\
&\quad+\frac{1}{\hbar}\sum_{k}\Bigg[2\left\lVert\delta\hat{M}^{[\alpha,1]}_{k}|\alpha,\sigma\rangle\right\rVert\left\lVert\hat{M}^{[\alpha,1]}_{k}|\alpha,\sigma\rangle\right\rVert+\left\lVert\delta\hat{M}^{[\alpha,1]}_{k}|\alpha,\sigma\rangle\right\rVert^{2}+\left\lVert\delta\hat{M}^{[\alpha,1]}_{k}{}^{\dagger}\hat{M}^{[\alpha,1]}_{k}|\alpha,\sigma\rangle\right\rVert\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad+\left\lVert\hat{M}^{[\alpha,1]}_{k}{}^{\dagger}\delta\hat{M}^{[\alpha,1]}_{k}|\alpha,\sigma\rangle\right\rVert+\left\lVert\delta\hat{M}^{[\alpha,1]}_{k}{}^{\dagger}\delta\hat{M}^{[\alpha,1]}_{k}|\alpha,\sigma\rangle\right\rVert\Bigg]\end{split}

(8.33)

These terms all become small in the classical limit for essentially the same reason: a Gaussian state of scale
\hbar
cannot easily “see” the third order corrections to the harmonic approximation. However, since our current techniques are not strong enough show this rigorously in one fell swoop, we will bound each of these terms individually.
To simplify the bounds we will use the quantities

\mathcal{Q}^{q,r}_{\hbar}[E]
and

\mathcal{N}^{q,r}_{\hbar;s,\nu}[E]
defined in (
3.16
) and (
3.17
) and recalled below for convenience:

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}^{q,r}_{\hbar}[E]

\displaystyle:=\sum_{j=q}^{r}\hbar^{(j-q)/2}\left|E\right|_{C^{j}}

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}^{q,r}_{\hbar;s,\nu}[E](\alpha)

\displaystyle:=\sum_{j=q}^{r}\hbar^{(j-q)/2}\sup_{\beta}\frac{\left\|\nabla^{j}E(\alpha+\beta)\right\|}{(1+\nu^{-1}|\beta|)^{s}}.

With this notation we will show that we can bound the terms of (
8.33
) by

\displaystyle\|\delta\hat{H}^{[\alpha,2]}|\alpha,\sigma\rangle\|

\displaystyle{\,\lesssim\,}\|\sigma\|^{3/2}\mathcal{Q}^{3,2d+4}_{\hbar}[H]

(8.34)

\displaystyle|L_{k}(\alpha)|\left\lVert\delta\hat{M}^{[\alpha,2]}_{k}|\alpha,\sigma\rangle\right\rVert

\displaystyle{\,\lesssim\,}\|\sigma\|^{3/2}\sup_{\beta}|L_{k}(\beta)|\mathcal{N}^{3,2d+6}_{\hbar;1,\|\sigma\|^{1/2}}[L_{k}](\beta)

(8.35)

\displaystyle\left\lVert\delta\hat{M}^{[\alpha,1]}_{k}|\alpha,\sigma\rangle\right\rVert

\displaystyle{\,\lesssim\,}\|\sigma\|\mathcal{Q}^{2,2d+3}_{\hbar}[L_{k}]

(8.36)

\displaystyle\left\lVert\hat{M}^{[\alpha,1]}_{k}|\alpha,\sigma\rangle\right\rVert

\displaystyle{\,\lesssim\,}\|\sigma\|^{1/2}\mathcal{Q}^{1,1}[L_{k}]

(8.37)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\left\lVert\delta\hat{M}^{[\alpha,1]}_{k}{}^{\dagger}\hat{M}^{[\alpha,1]}_{k}|\alpha,\sigma\rangle\right\rVert&{\,\lesssim\,}\|\sigma\|^{3/2}\mathcal{Q}^{1,1}[L_{k}]\mathcal{Q}^{2,2d+3}_{\hbar}[L_{k}]\end{split}

(8.38)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\left\lVert\hat{M}^{[\alpha,1]}_{k}{}^{\dagger}\delta\hat{M}^{[\alpha,1]}_{k}|\alpha,\sigma\rangle\right\rVert&{\,\lesssim\,}\|\sigma\|^{3/2}\mathcal{Q}^{1,1}[L_{k}]\mathcal{Q}^{2d+3}_{\hbar}[L_{k}]\end{split}

(8.39)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\left\lVert\delta\hat{M}^{[\alpha,1]}_{k}{}^{\dagger}\delta\hat{M}^{[\alpha,1]}_{k}|\alpha,\sigma\rangle\right\rVert&{\,\lesssim\,}\|\sigma\|^{2}\big(\mathcal{Q}^{2,4d+6}_{\hbar}[L_{k}]\big)^{2}\end{split}

(8.40)

Assuming these bounds,
the error (
8.33
) on the quantum harmonic approximation for a Gaussian state becomes

\displaystyle\begin{split}\left\lVert\delta\hat{\mathcal{L}}^{(\alpha)}[\hat{\tau}_{\alpha,\sigma}]\right\rVert_{\mathrm{Tr}}&{\,\lesssim\,}\frac{\|\sigma\|^{3/2}}{\hbar}\Bigg[\mathcal{Q}^{3,2d+4}_{0;\hbar}[H]+\sum_{k}\bigg(\mathcal{Q}^{1,1}[L_{k}]\mathcal{Q}^{2,2d+3}_{\hbar}[L_{k}]+\sup_{\beta}|L_{k}(\beta)|\mathcal{N}^{3,2d+6}_{\hbar;1,\|\sigma\|^{1/2}}[L_{k}](\beta)\\
&\hskip 260.0004pt+(\mathcal{Q}^{2,4d+6}_{\hbar}[L_{k}])^{2}\|\sigma\|^{1/2}\bigg)\Bigg]\\
&{\,\lesssim\,}\frac{\|\sigma\|^{3/2}}{\hbar}\left(B^{\mathrm{anh}}_{\mathrm{q}}[H,L_{k},\hbar]+B^{\mathrm{anh}}_{\mathrm{q}^{\prime}}[L_{k},\hbar,\|\sigma\|^{1/2}]\right)\end{split}

(8.41)

for the anharmonicity factors
B^{\mathrm{anh}}_{\mathrm{q}}[H,L_{k},\hbar]
and
B^{\mathrm{anh}}_{\mathrm{q}^{\prime}}[L_{k},\hbar,\nu]
defined by (
3.19
) and (
3.20
).

Thus, to complete the proof of Lemma
6.2
, all we have to do is demonstrate the bounds (
8.34
–
8.40
).
To do this we will use the trace formula
63
63

63

Recall that
\hat{E}:=\OpX_{\hbar}[E]
denotes the Weyl quantization and
\star
denotes the Moyal product.

\|\hat{E}\mathinner{|{\alpha,\sigma}\rangle}\|^{2}=\Tr[\hat{E}^{2}\hat{\tau}_{\alpha,\sigma}]=\int(E\star E)(\beta)\tau_{\alpha,\sigma}(\beta)\mathrm{d}\beta,

(8.42)

which holds for any polynomially bounded operator
\hat{E}
by Lemma
5.1
. To bound this Moyal product in turn we use the following proposition,
which is technically involved and is proved separately in Section
9
. The point of this proposition
is to show that if
A
vanishes to order
m
at
\alpha
(meaning in
particular that
|A(\alpha+\beta)|{\,\lesssim\,}|\beta|^{m+1}
) then

|A^{*}\star A(\alpha+\beta)|{\,\lesssim\,}|\beta|^{2m+2}+\hbar^{m+1}
. The reason we cannot simply use the standard
series approximation to the Moyal product with
O(\hbar^{m+1})

remainder is that we are dealing with symbols that may grow
at infinity, so we need a way of suppressing the dependence
on the symbol far from
\alpha
. This is taken care
of by the quantity
\mathcal{N}_{\hbar;s,\nu}^{q,r}[A](\alpha)

in the statement of the lemma below. The parameter
s
controls how much growth in the symbol
A
we want to allow.

Proposition 8.1

(Moyal Product Bound)
.

Let
\delta E^{[\alpha,m]}=E-E^{[\alpha,m]}
be the remainder to the
m
-th Taylor approximation

E^{[\alpha,m]}(\alpha+\beta)=\sum_{k=0}^{m}\beta^{a_{1}}\cdots\beta^{a_{k}}(\partial_{a_{1}}\cdots\partial_{a_{k}}E)(\alpha)/k!

at
\alpha
of some function
E
over
2d
-dimensional phase space.
Then, for any non-negative integer
s
and phase-space length scale
\nu>0
,

\begin{split}|\delta E^{[\alpha,m]}{}^{*}\star\delta E^{[\alpha,m]}(\alpha+\beta)|&{\,\lesssim\,}_{m}(1+\nu^{-2s}|\beta|^{2s})\\
&\qquad(|\beta|^{2m+2}+\hbar^{m+1})[\mathcal{N}_{\hbar;s,\nu}^{m+1,2d+2+m+s}[E](\alpha)]^{2}\end{split}

(8.43)

where
\mathcal{N}_{\hbar;s,\nu}^{q,r}[E](\alpha)
, defined in (
3.17
),
is an upper bound on the
q
-th through
r
-th derivatives
of
E
near
\alpha
, weighted by an
s
-th order polynomial decay in the distance
\beta
from
\alpha

(and is particular is bounded for symbols
in
S((1+|\alpha|)^{s})
as defined in (
3.18
))
Likewise for non-negative integer
s
we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}|E^{[\alpha,m]}{}^{*}\star E^{[\alpha,m]}\star E^{[\alpha,m]}{}^{*}\star E^{[\alpha,m]}(\alpha+\beta)|&{\,\lesssim\,}_{m}(1+\nu^{-4s}|\beta|^{4s})\\
&\qquad(|\beta|^{4m+4}+\hbar^{2(m+1)})[\mathcal{N}_{\hbar;2s,\nu}^{m+1,2(2d+2+m+s)}[E](\alpha)]^{4}\end{split}

(8.44)

for the thrice iterated Moyal product.

We will predominantly make use of Proposition
8.1
in the special case
64
64

64

Which, anyway, is the easy case to prove directly from the asymptotic series for the Moyal product.
of
s=0
, in which case the right hand side simplifies to

\displaystyle|\delta E^{[\alpha,m]}{}^{*}\star\delta E^{[\alpha,m]}(\alpha+\beta)|{\,\lesssim\,}(|\beta|^{2(m+1)}+\hbar^{m+1})(\mathcal{Q}^{m+1,2d+m+2}_{\hbar}[E])^{2}

(8.45)

and

\displaystyle\begin{split}&|E^{[\alpha,m]}{}^{*}\star E^{[\alpha,m]}\star E^{[\alpha,m]}{}^{*}\star E^{[\alpha,m]}(\alpha+\beta)|{\,\lesssim\,}(|\beta|^{4(m+1)}+\hbar^{2(m+1)})(\mathcal{Q}^{m+1,2(2d+m+2)}_{\hbar}[E])^{4}.\end{split}

(8.46)

We break up the rest of the proof into parts corresponding to Eqs. (
8.34
–
8.40
), and we apply Proposition
8.1
in all but one.
For the purposes of this proof, we introduce the shorthand
\ell_{a}=\partial_{a}L_{k}(\alpha)
since we consider just one Lindblad operator at a time and
\alpha
is just a fixed point we are expanding around.

Also, because we are not carefully computing the constants involved as we did in the classical case, we will mostly simply use the
following bound for the Gaussian integral:

\int\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\beta\tau_{\alpha,\sigma}(\alpha+\beta)|\beta|^{2k}{\,\lesssim\,}\|\sigma\|^{k}.

(8.47)

Proof of (
8.34
)
, (
8.36
), and (
8.40
):

Using (
8.42
),

\displaystyle\begin{split}\left\lVert\delta\hat{H}^{[\alpha,2]}|\alpha,\sigma\rangle\right\rVert^{2}&=\langle\alpha,\sigma|(\delta\hat{H}^{(\alpha)})^{2}|\alpha,\sigma\rangle=\Tr\left[\hat{\tau}_{\alpha,\sigma}(\delta\hat{H}^{[\alpha,2]})^{2}\right]\\
&=\int\!\mathrm{d}\beta\,\tau_{\alpha,\sigma}(\alpha+\beta)\left(\delta H^{[\alpha,2]}\star\delta H^{[\alpha,2]}\right)(\alpha+\beta)\end{split}

(8.48)

where
\tau_{\alpha,\sigma}(\alpha+\beta)=\exp(-\beta^{a}\sigma^{-1}_{ab}\beta^{b}/2)/(2\pi\sqrt{\det\sigma})
, a positive-valued function on phase space,
is the Wigner function of the pure Gaussian state
|\alpha,\sigma\rangle
. (Note that
\delta\hat{H}^{(\alpha)}
on the left-hand side is an operator while
\delta H^{(\alpha)}
on the right-hand side is just a classical scalar function of the phase-space location
\beta
.)
Applying Proposition
8.1
with
m=2
gives

\displaystyle\begin{split}\left\lVert\delta\hat{H}^{[\alpha,2]}|\alpha,\sigma\rangle\right\rVert^{2}&{\,\lesssim\,}(\mathcal{Q}^{3,2d+4}_{\hbar}[E])^{2}\int\!\mathrm{d}\beta\,\tau_{\alpha,\sigma}(\alpha+\beta)(|\beta|^{6}+\hbar^{3})\\
&{\,\lesssim\,}(\mathcal{Q}^{3,2d+4}_{\hbar}[H])^{2}\|\sigma\|^{3}.\end{split}

(8.49)

The proof of (
8.36
) follows the same strategy, first applying (
8.42
), then Proposition
8.1
with
m=1
, then performing the Gaussian integral, and finally applying elementary inequalities:

\displaystyle\left\lVert\delta\hat{M}^{[\alpha,1]}_{k}|\alpha,\sigma\rangle\right\rVert^{2}

\displaystyle=\int\!\mathrm{d}\beta\,\tau_{\alpha,\sigma}(\alpha+\beta)\left(\delta M^{[\alpha,1]}_{k}\star\delta M^{[\alpha,1]}_{k}\right)(\alpha+\beta)

(8.50)

\displaystyle{\,\lesssim\,}(\mathcal{Q}^{2,2d+3}_{\hbar}[L_{k}])^{2}\int\!\mathrm{d}\beta\,\tau_{\alpha,\sigma}(\alpha+\beta)(|\beta|^{4}+\hbar^{2})

(8.51)

\displaystyle{\,\lesssim\,}(\mathcal{Q}^{2,2d+3}_{\hbar}[L_{k}])^{2}\|\sigma\|^{2}

(8.52)

For (
8.40
), we deploy the triple-Moyal-product part of Proposition
8.1
with
m=1
, giving

\displaystyle\left\lVert\delta\hat{M}^{[\alpha,1]}_{k}{}^{\dagger}\delta\hat{M}^{[\alpha,1]}_{k}|\alpha,\sigma\rangle\right\rVert^{2}

\displaystyle=\langle\alpha,\sigma|\delta\hat{M}^{[\alpha,1]}_{k}{}^{\dagger}\delta\hat{M}^{[\alpha,1]}_{k}\delta\hat{M}^{[\alpha,1]}_{k}{}^{\dagger}\delta\hat{M}^{[\alpha,1]}_{k}|\alpha,\sigma\rangle

(8.53)

\displaystyle=\int\!\mathrm{d}\beta\,\tau_{\alpha,\sigma}(\alpha+\beta)\left(\delta M^{[\alpha,1]}_{k}{}^{*}\star\delta M^{[\alpha,1]}_{k}\star\delta M^{[\alpha,1]}_{k}{}^{*}\star\delta M^{[\alpha,1]}_{k}\right)(\alpha+\beta)

(8.54)

\displaystyle{\,\lesssim\,}(\mathcal{Q}^{2,4d+6}_{\hbar}[L_{k}])^{4}\int\!\mathrm{d}\beta\,\tau_{\alpha,\sigma}(\alpha+\beta)\left(\hbar^{4}+|\beta|^{8}\right)

(8.55)

\displaystyle{\,\lesssim\,}(\mathcal{Q}^{2,4d+6}_{\hbar}[L_{k}])^{4}\|\sigma\|^{4}

(8.56)

Proof of (
8.35
)
:

The presence of leading term
L_{k}(\alpha)
on the left-hand side of (
8.35
) introduces a complication for our goal of bounding that side with a constant independent of
\alpha
. In particular, we want our bound to hold in the special case of linear Lindblad operators,
L_{k}(\alpha)=\ell_{k;a}\alpha^{a}
, so we cannot bound
|L_{k}(\alpha)|
and
\|\delta\hat{M}^{[\alpha,2]}_{k}|\alpha,\sigma\rangle\|
separately. It is for this term, and this term only, that we will use the form of Proposition
8.1
with
s=1
rather than
s=0
,

\displaystyle\left\lVert\delta\hat{M}^{[\alpha,2]}_{k}|\alpha,\sigma\rangle\right\rVert^{2}

\displaystyle=\int\!\mathrm{d}\beta\,\tau_{\alpha,\sigma}(\alpha+\beta)\left(\delta M^{[\alpha,2]}_{k}{}^{*}\star\delta M^{[\alpha,2]}_{k}\right)(\alpha+\beta)

(8.57)

\displaystyle{\,\lesssim\,}(\mathcal{N}^{3,2d+6}_{\hbar;1,\nu}[L_{k}](\alpha))^{2}\int\!\mathrm{d}\beta\,\tau_{\alpha,\sigma}(\alpha+\beta)(|\beta|^{6}+\hbar^{3}+\nu^{-2}|\beta|^{8}+\nu^{-2}\hbar^{3}|\beta|^{2})

(8.58)

\displaystyle{\,\lesssim\,}(\mathcal{N}^{3,2d+6}_{\hbar;1,\nu}[L_{k}](\alpha))^{2}\left[\|\sigma\|^{3}+\nu^{-2}\|\sigma\|^{4}\right]

(8.59)

Then

\displaystyle|L_{k}(\alpha)|\left\lVert\delta\hat{M}^{[\alpha,2]}_{k}|\alpha,\sigma\rangle\right\rVert

\displaystyle{\,\lesssim\,}|L_{k}(\alpha)|\mathcal{N}^{3,2d+6}_{\hbar;1,\nu}[L_{k}](\alpha)\left[\|\sigma\|^{3}+\nu^{-2}\|\sigma\|^{4}\right]^{1/2}

(8.60)

\displaystyle{\,\lesssim\,}|L_{k}(\alpha)|\mathcal{N}^{3,2d+6}_{\hbar;1,\nu}[L_{k}](\alpha)\left[\|\sigma\|^{3/2}+\nu^{-1}\|\sigma\|^{2}\right]

(8.61)

Choosing
\nu=\|\sigma\|^{1/2}
we
obtain (
8.35
).

Proof of (
8.37
)
:

For this we do not actually need Proposition
8.1

because
M^{[\alpha,1]}_{k}(\beta)=\ell_{a}(\beta^{a}-\alpha^{a})
is just linear so, by the explicit Moyal product (
4.18
),

\displaystyle M^{[\alpha,1]}_{k}{}^{*}\star M^{[\alpha,1]}_{k}(\alpha+\beta)

\displaystyle=\left|M^{[\alpha,1]}_{k}(\alpha+\beta)\right|^{2}+\frac{i\hbar}{2}(\partial_{a}M^{[\alpha,1]}_{k}{}^{*})(\alpha+\beta)(\partial^{a}M^{[\alpha,1]}_{k})(\alpha+\beta)

(8.62)

\displaystyle=|\ell_{a}\beta^{a}|^{2}+\frac{i\hbar}{2}\ell_{a}^{*}\ell^{a}

(8.63)

\displaystyle\leq|\ell|^{2}(|\beta|^{2}+\hbar/2)

(8.64)

\displaystyle\leq(\mathcal{Q}^{1,1}[L_{k}])^{2}(|\beta|^{2}+\hbar/2)

(8.65)

where we have used the Cauchy-Schwartz inequality and
|\ell|=|\partial L_{k}(\alpha)|\leq C^{1,1}_{L_{k};0}=\sup_{\beta}[|\partial_{\mathrm{x}}L_{k}(\beta)|+|\partial_{\mathrm{p}}L_{k}(\beta)|]
. Therefore

\displaystyle\left\lVert\hat{M}^{[\alpha,1]}_{k}|\alpha,\sigma\rangle\right\rVert^{2}

\displaystyle=\int\!\mathrm{d}\beta\,\tau_{\alpha,\sigma}(\beta)\left(M^{[\alpha,1]}_{k}{}^{*}\star M^{[\alpha,1]}_{k}\right)(\alpha+\beta)

(8.66)

\displaystyle\leq(\mathcal{Q}^{1,1}[L_{k}])^{2}\int\!\mathrm{d}\beta\,\tau_{\alpha,\sigma}(\alpha+\beta)(|\beta|^{2}+\hbar/2)

(8.67)

\displaystyle=(\mathcal{Q}^{1,1}[L_{k}])^{2}(\Tr[\sigma]+\hbar/2)

(8.68)

\displaystyle{\,\lesssim\,}(\mathcal{Q}^{1,1}[L_{k}])^{2}\|\sigma\|

(8.69)

as desired.

Proof of (
8.38
)
and (
8.39
):

We note that the left hand sides of (
8.39
)
and (
8.38
) are related by

\displaystyle\|\hat{M}^{[\alpha,1]}_{k}{}^{\dagger}\delta\hat{M}^{[\alpha,1]}_{k}\mathinner{|{\alpha,\sigma}\rangle}\!\|\leq\|\delta\hat{M}^{[\alpha,1]}_{k}\hat{M}^{[\alpha,1]}_{k}{}^{\dagger}\mathinner{|{\alpha,\sigma}\rangle}\!\|+\|\big[\hat{M}^{[\alpha,1]}_{k}{}^{\dagger},\delta\hat{M}^{[\alpha,1]}_{k}\big]\mathinner{|{\alpha,\sigma}\rangle}\!\|.

(8.70)

Lets start by expanding the first part of the right-hand side:

\|\delta\hat{M}^{[\alpha,1]}_{k}\hat{M}^{[\alpha,1]}_{k}{}^{\dagger}\mathinner{|{\alpha,\sigma}\rangle}\!\|^{2}=\int\left(\delta M^{[\alpha,1]}_{k}{}^{*}\star\delta M^{[\alpha,1]}_{k}\right)(\alpha+\beta)\left(M^{[\alpha,1]}_{k}{}^{*}\star\tau_{\alpha,\sigma}\star M^{[\alpha,1]}_{k}\right)(\alpha+\beta)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\beta,

(8.71)

The first term inside the integrand of (
8.71
) can be bounded with Proposition
8.1
.

\left(\delta M^{[\alpha,1]}_{k}{}^{*}\star\delta M^{[\alpha,1]}_{k}\right)(\alpha+\beta){\,\lesssim\,}(\mathcal{Q}^{2,2d+3}_{\hbar}[L_{k}])^{2}(|\beta|^{4}+\hbar^{2}).

(8.72)

For the second term in the integrand of (
8.71
), we note that since

M^{[\alpha,1]}_{k}
is linear,
M^{[\alpha,1]}_{k}{}^{*}\star\tau_{\alpha,\sigma}
can be computed explicitly with the Moyal product (
4.18
) as

M^{[\alpha,1]}_{k}{}^{*}\star\tau_{\alpha,\sigma}(\alpha+\beta)=\ell_{a}^{*}\beta^{a}\tau_{\alpha,\sigma}(\alpha+\beta)+\frac{i\hbar}{2}\ell_{a}^{*}\partial^{a}\tau_{\alpha,\sigma}(\alpha+\beta).

(8.73)

Now we recall (
8.7
),

\displaystyle(\partial_{c}\tau_{\alpha,\sigma})(\alpha+\beta)

\displaystyle=-m_{c}\tau_{\alpha,\sigma}(\alpha+\beta)

(8.74)

where
m^{a}:=(\sigma^{-1})^{a}_{\phantom{a}b}\beta^{b}=\omega^{ac}\sigma^{-1}_{cb}\beta^{b}
, so

\displaystyle M^{[\alpha,1]}_{k}{}^{*}\star\tau_{\alpha,\sigma}(\alpha+\beta)

\displaystyle=\ell_{a}^{*}\left[\beta^{a}-\frac{i\hbar}{2}m^{a}\right]\tau_{\alpha,\sigma}(\alpha+\beta)

(8.75)

\displaystyle=\ell_{a}^{*}\left[\delta^{a}_{\phantom{a}b}-\frac{i\hbar}{2}(\sigma^{-1})^{a}_{\phantom{a}b}\right]\beta^{b}\tau_{\alpha,\sigma}(\alpha+\beta).

(8.76)

Then we can apply the Moyal product with
M^{[\alpha,1]}_{k}
on the right to get

\displaystyle\begin{split}&M^{[\alpha,1]}_{k}{}^{*}\star\tau_{\alpha,\sigma}\star M^{[\alpha,1]}_{k}(\alpha+\beta)\\
&\qquad=\tau_{\alpha,\sigma}(\alpha+\beta)\left[|\ell_{a}\beta^{a}|^{2}-\frac{i\hbar}{2}(\ell_{a}^{*}(\sigma^{-1})^{a}_{\phantom{a}b}\beta^{b})(\ell_{c}\beta^{c})\right]-\frac{i\hbar}{2}\ell_{c}\left[\partial^{c}(M^{[\alpha,1]}_{k}{}^{*}\star\tau_{\alpha,\sigma})\right](\alpha+\beta).\end{split}

(8.77)

We can compute

\displaystyle\left[\partial_{c}(M^{[\alpha,1]}_{k}{}^{*}\star\tau_{\alpha,\sigma})\right](\alpha+\beta)=\left[\ell_{c}^{*}-\frac{i\hbar}{2}\ell_{a}^{*}(\sigma^{-1})^{a}_{\phantom{a}c}-\left(\ell_{a}^{*}\beta^{a}-\frac{i\hbar}{2}\ell_{a}^{*}(\sigma^{-1})^{a}_{\phantom{a}b}\beta^{b}\right)\sigma^{-1}_{cd}\beta^{d}\right]\tau_{\alpha,\sigma}(\alpha+\beta)

(8.78)

so now inserting this into (
8.77
) we have

\begin{split}M^{[\alpha,1]}_{k}{}^{*}\star\tau_{\alpha,\sigma}\star M^{[\alpha,1]}_{k}(\alpha+\beta)&=\tau_{\alpha,\sigma}(\alpha+\beta)\Big[|\ell_{a}\beta^{a}|^{2}+i\frac{\hbar}{2}\big(\ell^{c}\ell^{*}_{c}-\ell^{*}_{a}\beta^{a}\ell^{c}\sigma^{-1}_{cb}\beta^{b}+\ell^{a}{}^{*}\sigma^{-1}_{ab}\beta^{b}\ell_{c}\beta^{c}\big)\\
&\qquad\qquad+\frac{\hbar^{2}}{4}(\ell^{a}{}^{*}\sigma^{-1}_{ab}\beta^{b}\ell^{c}\sigma^{-1}_{cd}\beta^{d}-\ell^{c}{}^{*}(\sigma^{-1})_{ca}\ell^{a})\Big]\\
&=\tau_{\alpha,\sigma}(\alpha+\beta)\Big[|\ell_{a}\beta^{a}|^{2}+\hbar\Imag\left(\ell^{*}_{a}\beta^{a}\ell^{c}\sigma^{-1}_{cb}\beta^{b}-\ell^{c}\ell^{*}_{c}/2\right)\\
&\qquad\qquad+\frac{\hbar^{2}}{4}\left(|\ell^{a}\sigma^{-1}_{ab}\beta^{b}|^{2}-\ell^{c}{}^{*}\sigma^{-1}_{ca}\ell^{a}\right)\Big]\\
&=\tau_{\alpha,\sigma}(\alpha+\beta)\left[{\ell^{a}}\left(\frac{i\hbar}{2}\omega_{ab}+\frac{\hbar^{2}}{4}\sigma^{-1}_{ab}\right)\ell^{b}{}^{*}+\left|\ell^{a}\left(\omega_{ab}-\frac{i\hbar}{2}\sigma^{-1}_{ab}\right)\beta^{a}\right|^{2}\right]\\
&=\tau_{\alpha,\sigma}(\alpha+\beta)\left[v_{0}+w_{a}^{*}w_{b}\beta^{a}\beta^{b}\right]\end{split}

(8.79)

where

\displaystyle w_{b}:=

\displaystyle\ell^{a}\left(\omega_{ab}-\frac{i\hbar}{2}\sigma^{-1}_{ab}\right)\in\mathbb{C}^{2d},

(8.80)

\displaystyle v_{0}:=

\displaystyle\frac{i\hbar}{2}{\ell^{a}}\left(\omega_{ab}-\frac{i\hbar}{2}\sigma^{-1}_{ab}\right)\ell^{b}{}^{*}=\frac{i\hbar}{2}w_{b}\ell^{b}{}^{*}\in\mathbb{R}

(8.81)

which obey

\displaystyle|v_{0}|\leq

\displaystyle\,(\hbar/2)|\ell|^{2}\left(1+(\hbar/2)\|\sigma^{-1}\|\right)\leq(\mathcal{Q}^{1,1}[L_{k}])^{2}\left(\hbar/2+\|\sigma\|\right)\leq 2(\mathcal{Q}^{1,1}[L_{k}])^{2}\|\sigma\|

(8.82)

\displaystyle(w^{\dagger}\sigma^{n}w)=

\displaystyle w_{a}^{*}(\sigma^{n})^{ab}w_{b}=(\ell^{\dagger}\sigma^{n}\ell)+\frac{\hbar^{2}}{4}(\ell^{\dagger}\sigma^{n-2}\ell)\leq(\mathcal{Q}^{1,1}[L_{k}])^{2}\left(\|\sigma^{n}\|+\hbar^{2}\|\sigma^{n-2}\|/4\right)\leq 2(\mathcal{Q}^{1,1}[L_{k}])^{2}\|\sigma^{n}\|

(8.83)

If we insert (
8.79
) and (
8.72
) into (
8.71
) we get

\displaystyle\begin{split}\|\delta\hat{M}^{[\alpha,1]}_{k}{}^{\dagger}\hat{M}^{[\alpha,1]}_{k}\mathinner{|{\alpha,\sigma}\rangle}\|^{2}&\leq[C^{2,2d+3}_{L_{k};0;\hbar}]^{2}\int\tau_{\alpha,\sigma}(\alpha+\beta)\left[\hbar^{2}v_{0}+\hbar^{2}w_{a}^{*}w_{b}\beta^{a}\beta^{b}+v_{0}|\beta|^{4}+w_{a}^{*}w_{b}\beta^{a}\beta^{b}|\beta|^{4}\right]\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\beta\end{split}

(8.84)

and then perform the Gaussian integral

\displaystyle\begin{split}\frac{\|\delta\hat{M}^{[\alpha,1]}_{k}{}^{\dagger}\hat{M}^{[\alpha,1]}_{k}\mathinner{|{\alpha,\sigma}\rangle}\|^{2}}{(\mathcal{Q}^{2,2d+3}_{\hbar}[L_{k}])^{2}}&\leq\int\tau_{\alpha,\sigma}(\alpha+\beta)\left[\hbar^{2}v_{0}+\hbar^{2}w_{a}^{*}w_{b}\beta^{a}\beta^{b}+v_{0}|\beta|^{4}+w_{a}^{*}w_{b}\beta^{a}\beta^{b}|\beta|^{4}\right]\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\beta\\
&=v_{0}\hbar^{2}+\hbar^{2}(w^{\dagger}\sigma w)+v_{0}\left((\Tr\sigma)^{2}+2\Tr[\sigma^{2}]\right)\\
&\qquad\qquad+\left((w^{\dagger}\sigma w)(\Tr\sigma)^{2}+2(w^{\dagger}\sigma w)\Tr[\sigma^{2}]+4\Tr[\sigma](w^{\dagger}\sigma^{2}w)+8(w^{\dagger}\sigma^{3}w)\right)\\
&=(\mathcal{Q}^{1,1}[L_{k}])^{2}4\left[\hbar^{2}\|\sigma\|+8(d+1)^{2}\|\sigma\|^{3}\right]\\
&{\,\lesssim\,}(\mathcal{Q}^{1,1}[L_{k}])^{2}\|\sigma\|^{3}.\end{split}

(8.85)

which proves (
8.38
).

To prove (
8.39
),
we need to handle the commutator in (
8.70
).
Note that
\hat{M}^{[\alpha,1]}_{k}=\ell_{a}(\hat{r}^{a}-\alpha^{a})
is linear in the phase space variables
\hat{r}=(\hat{x},\hat{p})
, so the Wigner transform of (i.e., symbol for) the commutator
[\hat{M}^{[\alpha,1]}_{k}{}^{\dagger},\delta\hat{M}^{[\alpha,1]}_{k}]
can be computed directly with the Moyal product (
4.18
) to be
i\hbar\ell_{a}^{*}\partial^{a}\delta M^{[\alpha,1]}_{k}
. This is just the remainder from the zeroth order Taylor approximation to the function
i\hbar\ell_{a}^{*}\partial^{a}L_{k}=i\hbar\ell_{a}^{*}\partial^{a}M_{k}
:

\displaystyle\ell_{a}^{*}\partial^{a}\delta M^{[\alpha,1]}_{k}=\ell_{a}^{*}\partial^{a}\delta L^{[\alpha,1]}_{k}=\delta(\ell_{a}^{*}\partial^{a}L_{k})^{[\alpha,0]},

(8.86)

so that it satisfies the prerequisites of Proposition
8.1
with
m=0
, which we can apply to get

\displaystyle\left\lVert[\hat{M}^{[\alpha,1]}_{k}{}^{\dagger},\delta\hat{M}^{[\alpha,1]}_{k}]|\alpha,\sigma\rangle\right\rVert^{2}

\displaystyle=\hbar^{2}\int\!\mathrm{d}\beta\,\tau_{\alpha,\sigma}(\alpha+\beta)\left(\delta(\ell_{a}^{*}\partial^{a}L_{k})^{[\alpha,0]}{}^{*}\star\delta(\ell_{a}^{*}\partial^{a}L_{k})^{[\alpha,0]}\right)(\alpha+\beta)

(8.87)

\displaystyle{\,\lesssim\,}\hbar^{2}\left(\mathcal{Q}^{1,2d+2}_{\hbar}[\ell_{a}^{*}\partial^{a}L_{k}]\right)^{2}\int\!\mathrm{d}\beta\,\tau_{\alpha,\sigma}(\alpha+\beta)(|\beta|^{2}+\hbar^{1})

(8.88)

\displaystyle{\,\lesssim\,}\hbar^{2}(\mathcal{Q}^{1,1}[L_{k}]\mathcal{Q}^{2,2d+3}_{\hbar}[L_{k}])^{2}\left(\Tr[\sigma]+\hbar^{1}\right)

(8.89)

\displaystyle{\,\lesssim\,}\hbar^{2}(\mathcal{Q}^{1,1}[L_{k}]\mathcal{Q}^{2,2d+3}_{\hbar}[L_{k}])^{2}\|\sigma\|

(8.90)

\displaystyle{\,\lesssim\,}(\mathcal{Q}^{1,1}[L_{k}]\mathcal{Q}^{2,2d+3}_{\hbar}[L_{k}])^{2}\|\sigma\|^{3}

(8.91)

where we have used

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}^{q,r}_{\hbar}[\ell_{a}^{*}\partial_{a}L_{k}]\leq|\ell|\mathcal{Q}^{q+1,r+1}_{\hbar}[L_{k}]\leq\mathcal{Q}^{1,1}[L_{k}]\mathcal{Q}^{q+1,r+1}_{\hbar}[L_{k}]

(8.92)

Taking the square roots of (
8.85
) and (
8.91
) and inserting into (
8.70
) gives (
8.39
).

Having now demonstrated all the bounds (
8.34
)–(
8.39
) with the help of Proposition
8.1
, Lemma
6.2
is proved.
∎

The only remaining task to complete the demonstration of our main result is to justify Proposition
8.1
, which is addressed in the next section.

9
Moyal product bound

In this section
65
65

65

Note that, due to the regrettably finite size of alphabets, in this section we have re-used variables previously defined for other purposes elsewhere in the paper. This section should be considered self-contained.
we prove Proposition
8.1
, whose statement we now recall:

See
8.1

We use the following integral formulation of the Moyal product:

E\star G(\alpha)=(2\pi\hbar)^{-d}\int e^{i\beta_{a}\gamma^{a}/(2\hbar)}E(\alpha+\beta/2)G(\alpha+\gamma/2)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\beta\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\gamma.

(9.1)

The proof of Proposition
8.1
splits into two main parts. First, in Section
9.1
we
state Lemma
9.1
giving a bound for
F\star F(\alpha)
in terms of a convolution of derivatives of
F
.
Then in Section
9.2
we show how Lemma
9.1
implies Proposition
8.1
.
The proof of Lemma
9.1
is deferred to Section
9.3
.

9.1
Main lemma

In the following lemma
\rho_{K}
is the convolution kernel

\rho_{K}(\alpha):=\hbar^{-d}(\hbar^{-1/2}|\alpha|+1)^{-K}.

Note that when
K>2d

\displaystyle\int\rho_{K}(\alpha)|\alpha|^{j}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\alpha

\displaystyle=\hbar^{-d}\int(\hbar^{-1/2}|\alpha|+1)^{-K}|\alpha|^{j}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\alpha

\displaystyle=\hbar^{j/2}\int(|\alpha|+1)^{-K}|\alpha|^{j}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\alpha

\displaystyle=\hbar^{j/2}C_{d,K,j}

Thus when
K>2d
the convolution
\rho_{K}\ast E
is well-defined:

\rho_{K}\ast E(\alpha):=\int\rho_{K}(\beta)E(\alpha-\beta)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\beta.

(9.2)

The main bound we need to prove Proposition
8.1
is a “localized” pointwise
bound for the Moyal product of two symbols. In particular,
we need to bound
F\star G(\alpha)
in a way that ideally
only depends on the values of
F
and
G
near
\alpha
. The parameters
K_{F}
and
K_{G}
determine how much our bound depends
on the values of
F
and
G
far from
\alpha
. In order
to make the bound more local in
F
we require higher order
derivative bounds on
G
, and vice-versa.

Lemma 9.1
.

Let
F,G\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})
be smooth functions,
and let
K_{F},K_{G}>2d
be nonnegative integers. Then

\begin{split}|F\star G(\alpha)|&{\,\lesssim\,}\left(\sum_{k=0}^{K_{F}}\big(\rho_{K_{G}}\ast\left\|\hbar^{k/2}\nabla^{k}F\right\|\big)(\alpha)\right)\left(\sum_{k=0}^{K_{G}}\big(\rho_{K_{F}}\ast\left\|\hbar^{k/2}\nabla^{k}G\right\|\big)(\alpha)\right).\end{split}

(9.3)

where the implicit constant hidden by
{\,\lesssim\,}
depends on
K_{F}
and
K_{G}
.

Corollary 9.1

(Iterated Moyal bound)
.

Let
F\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})
be a smooth function and let
K
be a nonnegative integer.
Then

|F\star F\star F\star F(\alpha)|{\,\lesssim\,}\left[\rho_{K}\ast\left(\sum_{m=0}^{2K}\rho_{K}\ast\left\|\hbar^{m/2}\nabla^{m}F\right\|\right)^{2}\right]^{2}(\alpha)

(9.4)

Proof using Lemma
9.1
.

Using Lemma
9.1
we have

|F\star F\star F\star F(\alpha)|{\,\lesssim\,}\left(\sum_{k=0}^{K}(\rho_{K}\ast\left\|\hbar^{k/2}\nabla^{k}(F\star F)\right\|)(\alpha)\right)^{2}.

To bound
\nabla^{k}(F\star F)
we use the following product rule for the
partial derivative
\partial^{\vec{n}}
, defined as

\partial_{1}^{n_{1}}\partial_{2}^{n_{2}}\cdots\partial_{2d}^{n_{2d}}

\partial^{\vec{n}}(F\star F)=\sum_{\vec{m}\leq\vec{n}}\prod_{j=1}^{2d}\binom{n_{j}}{m_{j}}\partial^{\vec{m}}F\star(\partial^{\vec{n}-\vec{m}}F).

Applying Lemma
9.1
to the terms in the right hand side we obtain

\begin{split}&\hbar^{k/2}\left\|(\nabla^{j}F)\star(\nabla^{k-j}F)(\alpha)\right\|\\
&\qquad\qquad{\,\lesssim\,}\left(\sum_{m=0}^{K}\rho_{K}\ast\left\|\hbar^{(m+j)/2}\nabla^{m+j}F\right\|(\alpha)\right)\left(\sum_{m=0}^{K}\rho_{K}\ast\left\|\hbar^{(m+k-j)/2}\nabla^{m+k-j}F\right\|(\alpha)\right)\\
&\qquad\qquad{\,\lesssim\,}\left(\sum_{m=0}^{2K}\rho_{K}\ast\left\|\hbar^{m/2}\nabla^{m/2}F\right\|(\alpha)\right)^{2}.\end{split}

(9.5)

∎

9.2
Proof of Proposition
8.1
from Lemma
9.1

First we prove a simple estimate for convolutions of functions against the kernel
\rho_{K}

in terms of the following weighted supremum

M_{\hbar}^{q}[F](\alpha):=\sup_{\beta}(1+\hbar^{-1/2}|\beta-\alpha|)^{-q}|F(\beta)|.

(9.6)

Lemma 9.2
.

If
K\geq 2d+1
then

(\rho_{K}\ast F)(\alpha)\leq CM_{\hbar}^{K-2d-1}[F](\alpha)

(9.7)

for some absolute constant
C
.

Proof.

Set
q=K-2d-1
. Then

\begin{split}|\rho_{K}\ast F(\alpha)|&\leq\hbar^{-d}\int(1+\hbar^{-1/2}|\beta-\alpha|)^{-K}|F(\beta)|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\beta\\
&\leq\hbar^{-d}M_{\hbar}^{q}[F](\alpha)\int(1+\hbar^{-1/2}|\beta-\alpha|)^{2d-1}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\beta\\
&\leq CM_{\hbar}^{q}[F](\alpha).\end{split}

(9.8)

∎

We also note two quick facts about the weighted supremum. The first is the weighted supremum of a monomial

m_{k}(\alpha)=|\alpha|^{k}
, using that
(a+b)^{k}\leq 2^{k}(a^{k}+b^{k})
. For
q\geq k
we have

\begin{split}M_{\hbar}^{q}[m_{k}](\alpha)&=\sup_{\beta}(1+\hbar^{-1/2}|\beta-\alpha|)^{-q}|\beta|^{k}\\
&\leq 2^{k}(|\alpha|^{k}+\sup_{\beta}(1+\hbar^{-1/2}|\beta-\alpha|)^{-q}|\alpha-\beta|^{k})\\
&\leq 4^{k}(|\alpha|^{k}+\hbar^{k/2}),\end{split}

(9.9)

where in the last step we used that

\sup_{t}(1+\hbar^{-1/2}t)^{-q}t^{k}\leq 2^{k}

when
q\geq k
.

The second quick fact that we need is the following product rule for the weighted supremum:

M_{\hbar}^{q_{1}+q_{2}}[FG]\leq M_{\hbar}^{q_{1}}[F]M_{\hbar}^{q_{2}}[G].

(9.10)

We will also use
M_{\hbar}^{q}[A]
to refer
to
M_{\hbar}^{q}[\left\|A\right\|]
when
A
is a tensor-valued
or vector-valued quantity.

Finally, we introduce the weighted supremum at
\nu
-scale,

M_{\nu}^{q}[F](\alpha):=\sup_{\beta}(1+\nu^{-1}|\beta-\alpha|)^{-q}|F(\beta)|.

(9.11)

Now we are ready to prove Proposition
8.1
.

Proof of Proposition
8.1
from Lemma
9.1
.

The first step is to prove (
8.43
).
First we need a bound for
\delta E^{[\alpha,k]}
that follows from the Taylor remainder formula,

f(t)=\sum_{j=0}^{k_{0}}\frac{t^{j}}{j!}f^{(j)}(0)+\frac{1}{k_{0}!}\int_{0}^{t}(t-s)^{k_{0}}f^{(k_{0}+1)}(s)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s,

(9.12)

which holds for functions in
C^{k_{0}+1}(\mathbb{R})
.
Applying this with
f(t)=E(t\alpha)
and evaluating at
t=1
we obtain

E(\alpha)=E^{[0,k]}(\alpha)+\frac{1}{k!}\int_{0}^{1}(1-s)^{k}\alpha^{\otimes({k+1})}\cdot\nabla^{k+1}E(s\alpha)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s.

(9.13)

Thus we obtain the formula

\delta E^{[0,k]}(\alpha)=\frac{1}{k!}\int_{0}^{1}(1-s)^{k}\alpha^{\otimes(k+1)}\cdot\nabla^{k+1}E(s\alpha)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s.

Above
\alpha^{\otimes(k+1)}\nabla^{k+1}E
is shorthand for

\alpha^{a_{1}}\cdots\alpha^{a_{k}}\partial_{a_{1}}\cdots\partial_{a_{k}}E
with an implicit
sum over all indices.

Simply using the triangle inequality and estimating naively
, this produces the bound

|\delta E^{[0,k]}(\alpha)|\leq\sup_{|\beta|\leq|\alpha|}|\nabla^{k+1}E(\beta)||\alpha|^{k+1}\leq M_{\nu}^{s}[\nabla^{k+1}E](0)(1+\nu^{-1}|\alpha|)^{s}|\alpha|^{k+1}.

We also need bounds for the derivatives with respect to
\alpha
. When
0\leq j\leq k
we have

\begin{split}\left\|\nabla^{j}\delta E^{[0,k]}(\alpha)\right\|&\leq\sum_{j^{\prime}=0}^{j}\sup_{|\beta|\leq|\alpha|}\left\|\nabla^{k+1+j^{\prime}}E(\beta)\right\|\|\alpha\|^{k+1+j^{\prime}-j}\\
&\leq\sum_{j^{\prime}=0}^{j}M_{\nu}^{s}[\nabla^{k+1+j^{\prime}}E](0)(1+\nu^{-1}|\alpha|)^{s}|\alpha|^{k+1+j^{\prime}-j}.\end{split}

(9.14)

For higher order derivatives we note that
\nabla^{k+1}E^{[0,k]}=0
and therefore

\nabla^{k+1}\delta E^{[0,k]}=\nabla^{k+1}E
, so that for
j\geq k+1
we have

\left\|\nabla^{j}\delta E^{[0,k]}(\alpha)\right\|\leq\max_{|\beta|\leq|\alpha|}\left\|\nabla^{j}E(\beta)\right\|\leq M_{\nu}^{s}[\nabla^{j}E](0)(1+\nu^{-1}|\alpha|)^{s}.

(9.15)

We will estimate
\rho_{K}\ast|\hbar^{j/2}\nabla^{j}\delta E^{[0,k]}|(\alpha)
in terms of the
quantity

Q_{k_{0},s}[E]:=\sum_{j^{\prime}=k_{0}+1}^{2d+k_{0}+s+2}\hbar^{(j^{\prime}-k-1)/2}M_{\nu}^{s}[\nabla^{j^{\prime}}E](0).

We first work with
j\leq k
. We combine
Combining (
9.14
)
and Lemma
9.2
along with (
9.9
) and
the product rule (
9.10
)
to see that for
K\geq 2d+k+s+1
and
j\leq k_{0}
we have

\begin{split}\rho_{K}\ast\left\|\hbar^{j/2}\nabla^{j}\delta E^{[0,k]}\right\|(\alpha)&\leq C\hbar^{j/2}M_{\hbar}^{K-2d-1}[\nabla^{j}\delta E^{[0,k]}](\alpha)\\
&\leq C\hbar^{j/2}\sum_{j^{\prime}=0}^{j}M_{\nu}^{s}[\nabla^{k+1+j^{\prime}}E](0)M_{\hbar}^{k+s+1}[(1+\nu^{-1}|\alpha|)^{s}|\alpha|^{k+1+j^{\prime}-j}]\\
&\leq C\hbar^{j/2}\sum_{j^{\prime}=0}^{j}M_{\nu}^{s}[\nabla^{k+1+j^{\prime}}E](0)M_{\hbar}^{s}[(1+\nu^{-1}|\alpha|)^{s}]M_{\hbar}^{k+1}[|\alpha|^{k+1+j^{\prime}-j}]\\
&\leq C(1+\nu^{-1}|\alpha|)^{s}\sum_{j^{\prime}=0}^{j}\hbar^{j/2}M_{\nu}^{s}[\nabla^{k+1+j^{\prime}}E](0)\left(|\alpha|^{k+1+j^{\prime}-j}+\hbar^{(k+1+j^{\prime}-j)/2}\right)\\
&\leq C(1+\nu^{-1}|\alpha|)^{s}Q_{k_{0},s}[E]\sum_{j^{\prime}=0}^{J}\left(\hbar^{(j-j^{\prime})/2}|\alpha|^{j-j^{\prime}}|\alpha|^{k+1}+\hbar^{(k+1)/2}\right)\end{split}

(9.16)

for some constant
C
.
In the final line, note that either
\hbar^{1/2}>|\alpha|
, in which case the term
\hbar^{(k_{0}+1)/2}
dominates,
or else
\hbar^{1/2}<|\alpha|
, in which case
(\hbar^{1/2}|\alpha|^{-1})^{(j-j^{\prime})}<1
so that the first
term in the sum is bounded by
|\alpha|^{k+1}
. Therefore we can simplify the above bound to

\rho_{K}\ast|\hbar^{j/2}\nabla^{j}\delta E^{[0,k]}|(\alpha)\leq CQ_{k_{0},s}[E](1+\nu^{-1}|\alpha|)^{s}(|\alpha|^{k+1}+\hbar^{(k+1)/2})

(9.17)

For larger derivatives
j>k
, we simply use (
9.15
) to see that

\rho_{K}\ast|\hbar^{j/2}\nabla^{j}\delta E^{[0,k]}|(\alpha)\leq\hbar^{j/2}M_{\nu}^{s}[\nabla^{j}E](1+\nu^{-1}|\alpha|)^{s}\leq\hbar^{(k+1)/2}Q_{k_{0},s}[E](1+\nu^{-1}|\alpha|)^{s}.

Taking
K=2d+k_{0}+s+2
we have

\sum_{j=0}^{K}(\rho_{K}\ast|\hbar^{j/2}\nabla^{j}\delta E^{[0,k]}|)(\alpha)\leq CQ_{k_{0},s}[E](1+\nu^{-1}|\alpha|)^{s})(|\alpha|^{k_{0}+1}+\hbar^{(k_{0}+1)/2}).

(9.18)

The proof of (
8.43
) now follows from an application of Lemma
9.1
.

To prove the bound on the triple Moyal product (
8.44
),
we use the stronger quantity

Q^{\prime}_{k_{0},s}[E]:=\sum_{j=k_{0}+1}^{4d+2k_{0}+2s+4}\hbar^{(j-k_{0}-1)/2}M_{\nu}^{s}[\nabla^{j^{\prime}}E](0).

Then taking (
9.18
) with
K^{\prime}=4d+2k_{0}+2s+4
, squaring both sides, and convolving with
\rho_{K^{\prime}}
, we have

\rho_{K^{\prime}}\ast\Bigg(\sum_{j=0}^{K^{\prime}}\rho_{K^{\prime}}\ast\left\|\hbar^{j/2}\nabla^{j}\delta E^{[\alpha,k_{0}]}\right\|\Bigg)^{2}\leq C(Q^{\prime}_{k_{0},s}[E])^{2}(1+\nu^{-2s}|\alpha|^{2s})(|\alpha|^{2k_{0}+2}+\hbar^{k_{0}+1}).

(9.19)

Then (
8.44
) follows from Corollary
9.1
.
∎

9.3
Proof of the Moyal product bound

Proof of Lemma
9.1
.

The main idea is to use the following identity to integrate by parts in the

\gamma
variables in order to obtain decay in the
\beta
variables:

e^{i\beta_{a}\gamma^{a}/(2\hbar)}=-2i\hbar(\beta_{a})^{-1}\partial_{\gamma^{a}}e^{i\beta_{a}\gamma^{a}/(2\hbar)}.

(9.20)

Symmetrically, we can integrate by parts in the
\beta
variables to obtain decay in
the
\gamma
variables.

To do this we introduce a partition of unity

1=\chi_{0}(t)+\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\chi_{j}(t),

where
\chi_{0}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
is a smooth function supported in
[-\hbar^{1/2},\hbar^{1/2}]

and
\chi_{j}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
are supported in
\{t\in\mathbb{R}\mid 2^{j-1}\hbar^{1/2}\leq|t|\leq 2^{j+2}\hbar^{1/2}\}
. We choose this partition of unity so that it satisfies the bounds

\sup_{t}\left|\frac{\mathrm{d}^{k}}{\mathrm{d}t^{k}}\chi_{j}(t)\right|\leq C_{k}2^{-jk}\hbar^{-k/2}.

(9.21)

For such
\chi_{j}
, we also have

\sup_{t}\left|\frac{\mathrm{d}^{k}}{\mathrm{d}t^{k}}(t^{-a}\chi_{j}(t))\right|\leq C_{a,k}2^{-j(k+a)}\hbar^{-(k+a)/2}.

(9.22)

Applying this partition of unity to each variable we we obtain the identity

1=\prod_{a=1}^{2d}\left(\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\chi_{j}(\beta_{a})\right)\prod_{b=1}^{2d}\left(\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\chi_{j}(\gamma^{a})\right)=\sum_{{\mathbf{j}}^{\beta},{\mathbf{j}}^{\gamma}:[2d]\to{\mathbb{N}}}\prod_{a=1}^{2d}\chi_{j^{\beta}_{a}}(\beta_{a}),\prod_{a=1}^{2d}\chi_{j^{\gamma}_{a}}(\gamma^{a}).

(9.23)

The latter sum is over pairs of tuples

{\mathbf{j}}^{\beta}=(j^{\beta}_{1},j^{\beta}_{2},\cdots,j^{\beta}_{2d})
,

{\mathbf{j}}^{\gamma}=(j^{\gamma}_{1},j^{\gamma}_{2},\cdots,j^{\gamma}_{2d})
.

We use this to split the moyal product
F\star G
into terms indexed by
{\mathbf{j}}^{\beta}
and
{\mathbf{j}}^{\gamma}
,

F\star G=\sum_{{\mathbf{j}}^{\beta},{\mathbf{j}}^{\gamma}}(F\star G)_{{\mathbf{j}}^{\beta},{\mathbf{j}}^{\gamma}},

(9.24)

where

(F\star G)_{{\mathbf{j}}^{\beta},{\mathbf{j}}^{\alpha}}(\alpha):=(2\pi\hbar)^{-2d}\int e^{i\beta_{a}\gamma^{a}/(2\hbar)}F(\alpha+\beta/2)G(\alpha+\gamma/2)\prod_{a=1}^{2d}\chi_{j^{\beta}_{a}}(\beta_{a})\prod_{a=1}^{2d}\chi_{j^{\gamma}_{a}}(\gamma_{a})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\beta_{a}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\gamma^{a}.

(9.25)

We estimate the quantity
(F\star G)_{{\mathbf{j}}^{\beta},{\mathbf{j}}^{\gamma}}
differently depending
on whether
{\mathbf{j}}^{\beta}=0
and/or
{\mathbf{j}}^{\gamma}=0
. We thus split into four terms:

F\star G=(F\star G)_{{\mathbf{0}},{\mathbf{0}}}+\sum_{{\mathbf{j}}^{\beta}\not=0}(F\star G)_{{\mathbf{j}}^{\beta},{\mathbf{0}}}+\sum_{{\mathbf{j}}^{\gamma}\not=0}(F\star G)_{{\mathbf{0}},{\mathbf{j}}^{\gamma}}+\sum_{{\mathbf{j}}^{\beta},{\mathbf{j}}^{\gamma}}(F\star G)_{{\mathbf{j}}^{\beta},{\mathbf{j}}^{\gamma}}.

The first term, with
{\mathbf{j}}^{\beta}={\mathbf{j}}^{\gamma}={\mathbf{0}}
being all zeros, can be bounded simply using the
triangle inequality:

\begin{split}|(F\star G)_{{\mathbf{0}},{\mathbf{0}}}(\alpha)|&\leq\hbar^{-2d}\int|F(\alpha+\beta/2)||G(\alpha+\gamma/2)|\prod\chi_{0}(B_{a})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}B\\
&\leq\big(\hbar^{-d}\int_{|\alpha^{\prime}-\alpha|\leq\hbar^{1/2}}|F(\alpha^{\prime})|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\alpha^{\prime}\big)\big(\hbar^{-d}\int_{|\alpha^{\prime}-\alpha|\leq\hbar^{1/2}}|G(\alpha^{\prime})|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\alpha^{\prime}\big)\\
&\leq 2^{K_{G}+K_{F}}(\rho_{K_{G}}\ast|F|(\alpha))(\rho_{K_{F}}\ast|G|(\alpha)).\end{split}

(9.26)

In the last line the factor

2^{K_{G}+K_{F}}
appears from the use of the
fact that

2^{K}\rho_{K}(\alpha)\geq 1
when
|\alpha|\leq\hbar^{1/2}
.

For the remaining terms we integrate by parts using (
9.20
). We will assume for this part that

{\mathbf{j}}^{\beta}\not={\mathbf{0}}
and
{\mathbf{j}}^{\gamma}\not={\mathbf{0}}
(this being the most technical case to handle).
Let
a_{0}=\argmax{\mathbf{j}}^{\beta}
and
b_{0}=\argmax{\mathbf{j}}^{\gamma}
be the indices for which
J^{\beta}:=j^{\beta}_{a_{0}}

and
J^{\gamma}:=j^{\gamma}_{b_{0}}
are maximized.

We integrate by parts first
K_{G}
times in the
\gamma^{a_{0}}
variable to obtain decay in
\beta_{a_{0}}
, obtaining

\begin{split}(F\star G)_{{\mathbf{j}}^{\beta},{\mathbf{j}}^{\alpha}}(\alpha)=(2\pi\hbar)^{-2d}(2i\hbar)^{K_{G}}&\int e^{i\beta_{a}\gamma^{a}/(2\hbar)}\beta_{a_{0}}^{-K_{G}}F(\alpha+\beta/2)\partial_{\gamma^{a_{0}}}^{K_{G}}\big(\chi_{j^{\gamma}_{a_{0}}}(\gamma^{a_{0}})G(\alpha+\gamma/2)\big)\\
&\qquad\qquad\times\prod_{a=1}^{2d}\chi_{j^{\beta}_{a}}(\beta_{a})\prod_{b\not=a_{0}}\chi_{j^{\gamma}_{b}}(\gamma^{b})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\beta_{a}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\gamma^{a}.\end{split}

(9.27)

Next we integrate by parts
K_{F}
times in
\beta_{b_{0}}
to obtain decay in
\gamma^{b_{0}}
, then
use the product rule to split up the derivatives

\begin{split}(F\star G)_{{\mathbf{j}}^{\beta},{\mathbf{j}}^{\alpha}}(\alpha)&=(2\pi\hbar)^{-2d}(2i\hbar)^{K_{G}}(2i\hbar)^{K_{F}}\int e^{i\beta_{a}\gamma^{a}/(2\hbar)}\partial_{\beta_{b_{0}}}^{K_{F}}\big(\beta_{a_{0}}^{-K_{G}}\chi_{j^{\beta}_{b_{0}}}(\beta_{b_{0}})F(\alpha+\beta/2)\big)\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\times(\gamma^{b_{0}})^{-K_{F}}\partial_{\gamma^{a_{0}}}^{K_{G}}\big(\chi_{j^{\gamma}_{a_{0}}}(\gamma^{a_{0}})G(\alpha+\gamma/2)\big)\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\times\prod_{a\not=b_{0}}\chi_{j^{\beta}_{a}}(\beta_{a})\prod_{b\not=a_{0}}\chi_{j^{\gamma}_{b}}(\gamma^{b})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\beta_{a}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\gamma^{a}\\
&=(2\pi\hbar)^{-2d}(2i\hbar)^{K_{G}}(2i\hbar)^{K_{F}}\int e^{i\beta_{a}\gamma^{a}/(2\hbar)}\sum_{k=0}^{K_{F}}\binom{K_{F}}{k}\partial_{\beta_{b_{0}}}^{K_{F}-k}\big(\beta_{a_{0}}^{-K_{G}}\chi_{j^{\beta}_{b_{0}}}(\beta_{b_{0}})\big)\partial_{\beta_{b_{0}}}^{k}F(\alpha+\beta/2)\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\times(\gamma^{b_{0}})^{-K_{F}}\sum_{k^{\prime}=0}^{K_{G}}\binom{K_{G}}{k^{\prime}}\partial_{\gamma^{a_{0}}}^{K_{G}-k^{\prime}}\chi_{j^{\gamma}_{a_{0}}}(\gamma^{a_{0}})\partial_{\gamma^{a_{0}}}^{k^{\prime}}G(\alpha+\gamma/2)\big)\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\times\prod_{a\not=b_{0}}\chi_{j^{\beta}_{a}}(\beta_{a})\prod_{b\not=a_{0}}\chi_{j^{\gamma}_{b}}(\gamma^{b})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\beta_{a}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\gamma^{a}.\end{split}

(9.28)

In the quantity

\partial_{\beta_{b_{0}}}^{K_{F}-k}(\beta_{a_{0}}^{-K_{G}}\chi_{j^{\beta}_{b_{0}}}(\beta_{b_{0}}))

there are two cases to consider. If

a_{0}=b_{0}
then we use (
9.22
),
and if
a_{0}\not=b_{0}
then the derivative
only falls on
\chi
and we use (
9.21
). Regardless we have the bound
(using
\beta_{a_{0}}\geq 2^{J^{\beta}}\hbar^{1/2}
)

|\partial_{\beta_{b_{0}}}^{K_{F}-k}(\beta_{a_{0}}^{-K_{G}}\chi_{j^{\beta}_{b_{0}}}(\beta_{b_{0}}))|\leq C_{K}2^{-J^{\beta}K_{G}}\hbar^{-(K_{F}+K_{G}-k)/2}.

(9.29)

Now we use the estimates (
9.22
) and (
9.21
)
(using that
\gamma^{b_{0}}\sim 2^{J^{\gamma}}\hbar^{1/2}
and
\beta_{a_{0}}\sim 2^{J^{\beta}}\hbar^{1/2}
) to
bound the derivatives hitting
\chi
, and then apply the triangle inequality

\begin{split}|(F\star G)_{{\mathbf{j}}^{\beta},{\mathbf{j}}^{\alpha}}(\alpha)|&\leq C_{K}\hbar^{K_{G}+K_{F}-2d}\int\sum_{k=0}^{K_{F}}2^{-J^{\beta}K_{G}}\hbar^{-(K_{F}-K_{G}-k)/2}|\partial_{\beta_{b_{0}}}^{k}F(\alpha+\beta/2)|\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\times 2^{-J^{\gamma}K_{F}}\hbar^{-K_{F}/2}\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\times\sum_{k^{\prime}=0}^{K_{G}}\hbar^{-(K_{G}-k^{\prime})/2}|\partial_{\gamma^{a_{0}}}^{k^{\prime}}G(\alpha+\gamma/2)\big|\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\times\prod_{a}{\widetilde{\chi}}_{j^{\beta}_{a}}(\beta_{a})\prod_{b}{\widetilde{\chi}}_{j^{\gamma}_{b}}(\gamma^{b})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\beta_{a}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\gamma^{a}.\end{split}

(9.30)

Above
{\widetilde{\chi}}_{a}
is the indicator
function for the support of
\chi_{a}
.
Collecting the constants, using

\binom{K}{k}\leq 2^{K}
and combining factors
of
2
and
\hbar
we arrive at

\begin{split}|(F\star G)_{{\mathbf{j}}^{\beta},{\mathbf{j}}^{\alpha}}(\alpha)|&\leq\sum_{k=0}^{K_{F}}\sum_{k^{\prime}=0}^{K_{G}}\hbar^{-2d}\int 2^{-J^{\beta}K_{G}}\hbar^{k/2}|\partial_{\beta_{b_{0}}}^{k}F(\alpha+\beta/2)|\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\times 2^{-J^{\gamma}K_{F}}\hbar^{k^{\prime}/2}|\partial_{\gamma^{a_{0}}}^{k^{\prime}}G(\alpha+\gamma/2)\big|\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\times\prod_{a}{\widetilde{\chi}}_{j^{\beta}_{a}}(\beta_{a})\prod_{b}{\widetilde{\chi}}_{j^{\gamma}_{b}}(\gamma^{b})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\beta_{a}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\gamma^{a}.\end{split}

(9.31)

Since
J^{\beta}>j^{\beta}_{a}
for any other index
a
and on the support of the integrand above
\beta_{a}\sim\hbar^{1/2}2^{j^{\beta}_{a}}

(and similarly for
\gamma
, it holds that

2^{-KJ^{\beta}}\leq C^{K}(\hbar^{-1/2}|\beta|+1)^{-K}\leq\hbar^{d}\rho_{K}(\beta)

and similarly

2^{-KJ^{\gamma}}\leq C^{K}(\hbar^{-1/2}|\gamma|+1)^{-K}\leq\hbar^{d}\rho_{K}(\gamma).

Recalling that
\rho_{K}(\beta)=\hbar^{-d}(\hbar^{1/2}|\beta|+1)^{-K}
,
and then noting that we can sum over all multi-indices
{\mathbf{j}}^{\beta}

and
{\mathbf{j}}^{\gamma}
using

\sum_{{\mathbf{j}}^{\beta}}\prod_{a}{\widetilde{\chi}}_{j^{\beta}_{a}}(\beta_{a})\leq 4^{d}

we can simplify the above bound to

\begin{split}|(F\star G)(\alpha)|&\leq\hbar^{-2d}\sum_{{\mathbf{j}}^{\beta},{\mathbf{j}}^{\alpha}}\int 2^{-J^{\beta}K_{G}}\hbar^{k/2}|\partial_{\beta_{b_{0}}}^{k}F(\alpha+\beta/2)|\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\times 2^{-J^{\gamma}K_{F}}\hbar^{k^{\prime}/2}|\partial^{k^{\prime}}_{\gamma^{a_{0}}}G(\alpha+\gamma/2)|\prod_{a}{\widetilde{\chi}}_{j^{\beta}_{a}}(\beta_{a})\prod_{a}{\widetilde{\chi}}_{j^{\gamma}_{b}}(\gamma^{b})\mathrm{d}\beta_{a}\mathrm{d}\gamma^{a}\\
&{\,\lesssim\,}\int\rho_{K_{G}}(\beta)\hbar^{k/2}(\sum_{k=0}^{K_{F}}\left\|\nabla^{k}F(\alpha+\beta/2)\right\|)\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\quad\times\rho_{K_{F}}(\gamma)\hbar^{k^{\prime}/2}(\sum_{k^{\prime}=0}^{K_{G}}\left\|\nabla^{k^{\prime}}G(\alpha+\gamma/2)\right\|)\sum_{{\mathbf{j}}^{\beta},{\mathbf{j}}^{\alpha}}(\prod_{a}{\widetilde{\chi}}_{j^{\beta}_{a}}(\beta_{a})\prod_{b}{\widetilde{\chi}}_{j^{\gamma}_{b}}(\gamma^{b}))\mathrm{d}\beta_{a}\mathrm{d}\gamma^{a}\\
&{\,\lesssim\,}\int\rho_{K_{G}}(\beta)\hbar^{k/2}(\sum_{k=0}^{K_{F}}\left\|\nabla^{k}F(\alpha+\beta/2)\right\|)\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\quad\times\rho_{K_{F}}(\gamma)\hbar^{k^{\prime}/2}(\sum_{k^{\prime}=0}^{K_{G}}\left\|\nabla^{k^{\prime}}G(\alpha+\gamma/2)\right\|)\mathrm{d}\beta_{a}\mathrm{d}\gamma^{a}\\
&{\,\lesssim\,}\sum_{k=0}^{K_{F}}\sum_{k^{\prime}=0}^{K_{G}}\big(\rho_{K_{G}}\ast\left\|\hbar^{k/2}\nabla^{k}F\right\|(\alpha)\big)\big(\rho_{K_{F}}\ast\left\|\hbar^{k^{\prime}/2}\nabla^{k^{\prime}}G\right\|(\alpha)\big),\end{split}

(9.32)

as desired. The remaining terms
(with
{\mathbf{j}}^{\beta}\not=0
and

{\mathbf{j}}^{\gamma}=0
or vice versa)
are handled similarly.
∎

Appendix A
Physical units, symplectic covariance, and a corollary

In this section we offer some informal discussion of symplectic symmetry and the relationship to units. To illustrate this, we then define some preferred choices of units and use them to state and prove Corollary
A.1
of Theorem
3.1
. This corollary generalizes the main result from our companion paper
[
48
]
to Hamiltonians not restricted to the form
\hat{H}=\hat{p}^{2}/2m+V(\hat{x})
at the expense of introducing the uncomputed constant
C_{d}
.

A.1
Symplectic transformations of the main result

For a symplectic matrix
\mathcal{Z}
(
\mathcal{Z}^{\top}\omega\mathcal{Z}=\omega
) representing a linear symplectic transformation, we will use
\mathcal{Z}
as a superscript on scalar functions over phase space to denote the composition equivalent to the change of coordinates associated with the matrix:
E^{\mathcal{Z}}(\alpha):=(E\circ\mathcal{Z})(\alpha)=E(\mathcal{Z}\alpha)
. The same notation is used for tensor functions, except we must additionally transform the indices, e.g.,
(E^{\mathcal{Z}})^{a}_{\phantom{a}b}(\alpha):=(\mathcal{Z}^{-1})^{a}_{\phantom{a}c}\mathcal{Z}^{d}_{\phantom{a}b}E^{c}_{\phantom{a}d}(\mathcal{Z}\alpha)
.

The general Fokker-Planck equation for a classical open system,

\partial_{t}f=-\partial_{a}[f(\partial^{a}H+G^{a})]+\frac{1}{2}\partial_{a}(D^{ab}\partial_{b}f),

(A.1)

and the Wigner representation of the Lindblad equation

\displaystyle\partial_{t}W_{\rho}=

\displaystyle\,-\frac{i}{\hbar}(H\star W_{\rho}-W_{\rho}\star H)+\frac{1}{\hbar}\sum_{k}\left(L_{k}\star W_{\rho}\star L_{k}^{*}-\frac{1}{2}L_{k}^{*}\star L_{k}\star W_{\rho}-\frac{1}{2}W_{\rho}\star L_{k}^{*}\star L_{k}\right)

(A.2)

are both covariant under linear symplectic transformations.
This means (
A.1
) is unchanged under
H\to H^{\mathcal{Z}}
,
G\to G^{\mathcal{Z}}
,
D\to D^{\mathcal{Z}}
, and
f\to f^{\mathcal{Z}}
because
\partial_{a}f^{\mathcal{Z}}=\mathcal{Z}^{b}_{\phantom{a}a}\partial_{a}f
.
Likewise (
A.2
) is unchanged under under
H\to H^{\mathcal{Z}}
,
L_{k}\to L_{k}^{\mathcal{Z}}
, and
W_{\rho}\to W_{\rho}^{\mathcal{Z}}
because
A^{\mathcal{Z}}\star B^{\mathcal{Z}}=(A\star B)^{\mathcal{Z}}
, a basic property of the Moyal product.

If the data
(H,\{L_{k}\}_{k=1}^{K})
are admissible under Assumption
3.1
, then the transformed data
(H^{\mathcal{Z}},\{L_{k}^{\mathcal{Z}}\}_{k=1}^{K})
are also admissible. Now suppose the initial state can expressed as a mixture of NTS states (
\sigma\geq\frac{g}{2}\sigma_{*}
)
and also
as a mixture of
\mathcal{Z}
-transformed NTS states (
\sigma\geq\frac{g_{\mathcal{Z}}}{2}\mathcal{Z}^{-1}\sigma_{*}\mathcal{Z}^{-\top}
), where
g
and
g_{\mathcal{Z}}
are the relative diffusion strengths (
3.10
) computed with the original and transformed data. Then we can apply Theorem
3.1
to the original and transformed data and
both
sets of bounds will hold. These will generally be distinct bounds because the anharmonicity measures
B^{\mathrm{anh}}_{\mathrm{c}}
,
B^{\mathrm{anh}}_{\mathrm{q}}
, and
B^{\mathrm{anh}}_{\mathrm{q}^{\prime}}
are not invariant under linear symplectic transformations of the data.
66
66

66

Note that
H^{\mathcal{Z}}
is quadratic if and only if
H
is quadratic, and
L_{k}^{\mathcal{Z}}
is linear if and only if
L_{k}
is linear, so whether the anharmonicity measures vanish will generally be invariant under linear symplectic transformations.

A.2
Units

Given a phase-space vector with mixed units like
\tilde{\alpha}=(x_{0}\,\mathrm{m},p_{0}\,\mathrm{kg}\!\cdot\!\mathrm{m}/\mathrm{s})
, where
x_{0}
and
p_{0}
are dimensionless numbers, we can transform this to a vector with uniform units using a symplectic matrix like
\mathcal{Z}^{-1}=\diag(\eta,\eta^{-1})
with
\eta=\sqrt{(\mathrm{kg}\!\cdot\!\mathrm{m}/\mathrm{s})/(\mathrm{m})}=\sqrt{\mathrm{kg}/\mathrm{s}}
. Specifically,
\mathcal{Z}^{-1}\tilde{\alpha}=(x_{0},p_{0})[\mathrm{m}\sqrt{\mathrm{kg}/\mathrm{s}}]
. Given such a choice of
\mathcal{Z}
and a real, physical Hamiltonian
H(x,p)
taking
as input dimensionful positions
x
and momenta
p
, we can then apply Theorem
3.1
to the transformed functions

H^{\mathcal{Z}}(\alpha):=H(\mathcal{Z}\alpha)
and
L_{k}^{\mathcal{Z}}(\alpha):=L_{k}(\mathcal{Z}\alpha)
, which will accept vectors with uniform units.
Now, there is generally no symplectic matrix that can make an arbitrary unitful vector unitless. Still, a choice like
\mathcal{Z}
above is sufficient to ensure that all of the manipulations in this paper (such as taking the Euclidean
67
67

67

An alternative way to think about this is that the choice
\mathcal{Z}
defines an inner product:
\langle\tilde{\alpha},\tilde{\beta}\rangle_{\mathcal{Z}}:={\tilde{\alpha}}^{\top}(\mathcal{Z}\mathcal{Z}^{\top})^{-1}\tilde{\beta}=\alpha^{\top}\cdot\beta
for uniform-unit vectors
\alpha=\mathcal{Z}^{-1}\tilde{\alpha}
,
\beta=\mathcal{Z}^{-1}\tilde{\beta}
.

norm of
\mathcal{Z}\alpha
for mixed-unit vector
\alpha
) are physically meaningful once such a choice of units has been made.

The symplectic matrices are closed under multiplication (being a group) and so the choice of units does not exhaust the freedom to choose
\mathcal{Z}
. For instance, the skewing matrix
\mathcal{Z}=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&1\\
0&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)
is symplectic and mixes position and momentum in a way that does not correspond to a choice of units.
68
68

68

In order that an arbitrary symplectic matrix
\mathcal{Z}^{-1}
correctly makes all units uniform, the entries
\mathcal{Z}_{\mathrm{x}_{i},\mathrm{x}_{j}}
and
\mathcal{Z}_{\mathrm{p}_{i},\mathrm{x}_{j}}
must have units of [length/momentum]
1/2
and the entries
\mathcal{Z}_{\mathrm{x}_{i},\mathrm{p}_{j}}
and
\mathcal{Z}_{\mathrm{p}_{i},\mathrm{p}_{j}}
must have units of [momentum/length]
1/2
. This ensures that all elements of the vector
\mathcal{Z}\alpha
have units of [length
\cdot
momentum]
1/2
, where
\alpha
is a physical phase-space vector with units of [length] in the first
d
elements and [momentum] in the other
d
elements.

Thus, the fact that multiple bounds can be derived using Theorem
3.1
by applying difference choices of
\mathcal{Z}
is not removed by a choice of units.
Although the trajectory
\tilde{\rho}(t)
constructed in different cases will generally be different, note that the existence of such a trajectory is a units-independent statement; the bounds (
3.21
), (
3.22
) are on unitless norms, which in turn constrains the maximum (unitless) difference in outcome probability for any measurement.

Ideally the choice of
\mathcal{Z}
could be optimized for the best bound
(since coherent states
\hat{\tau}_{\alpha}
would correspond to covariance matrices satisfying

\sigma=\frac{\hbar}{2}\mathcal{Z}^{\top}\mathcal{Z}
). Alternatively, to name a concrete choice, one might for example choose

\begin{split}\mathcal{Z}{}_{0}=\left(\begin{matrix}r\mathds{1}_{d}&0\\
0&r^{-1}\mathds{1}_{d}\end{matrix}\right)\end{split}

(A.3)

where
r=\sqrt{r_{x}/r_{p}}
for characteristic scales
r_{x},r_{p}
defined by
r_{x}^{-2}=\sup_{\alpha}\sup_{w\in\mathbb{R}^{d},\left\lVert w\right\rVert=1}(w_{a}\partial_{x_{a}})^{2}H(\alpha)
and
r_{p}^{-2}=\sup_{\alpha}\sup_{w\in\mathbb{R}^{d},\left\lVert w\right\rVert=1}(w_{a}\partial_{p_{a}})^{2}H(\alpha)
, which quantify the maximum second derivatives of
H
with respect to position and momentum, respectively.

A.3
Corollary

To illustrate the above informal discussion, we will apply Theorem
3.1
to a special case with linear Lindblad operators (diffusion matrix homogeneous over phase space) and no friction. We will ask: given a physical system with (unitful) Hamiltonian
H
, how much environmental noise must we add to ensure that the quantum and classical dynamics cannot be distinguished up to some tolerable error? We will first identify the relevant characteristic timescales and action scales of the Hamiltonian. They will be constructed from the Hamiltonian’s derivatives, and in particular we recall from Eq. (
3.16
) the seminorm

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}^{q,r}_{s}[E]

\displaystyle:=\sum_{j=q}^{r}s^{(j-q)/2}\left|E\right|_{C^{j}}=\sum_{j=q}^{r}s^{(j-q)/2}\sup_{\alpha}\sup_{\|\beta_{\ell}\|=1}\left|\beta_{1}^{a_{1}}\cdots\beta_{j}^{a_{j}}\partial_{a_{1}}\cdots\partial_{a_{j}}E(\alpha)\right|.

(A.4)

which bounds the
q
-th through
r
-th derivatives. For our main result, we used
s=\hbar
to get the tightest bound, but here it will be instructive to use a (macroscopic) action scale
s\geq\hbar
.

Definition A.1

(Characteristic classical scales)
.

For any classical Hamiltonian
H
with bounded partial derivatives of degree
k=2,\ldots,2d+4

and any symplectic matrix
\mathcal{Z}
, we define the

harmonic time

\tau_{\mathrm{H}}
as the inverse of the maximum operator norm taken by the Hessian of
H^{\mathcal{Z}}
over phase space,
69
69

69

For linear Hamiltonians,
\tau_{\mathrm{H}}=\infty
by convention. This indicates that there is no natural time scale because the (real or imaginary) frequency of the local harmonic dynamics is zero everywhere in phase space.

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\tau_{\mathrm{H}}}:=|H^{\mathcal{Z}}|_{C^{2}}=\mathcal{Q}^{2,2}[H^{\mathcal{Z}}]=\sup_{\alpha}\sup_{\|\beta\|=\|\xi\|=1}\left|\beta^{a}\xi^{b}\partial_{a}\partial_{b}H^{\mathcal{Z}}(\alpha)\right|.

(A.5)

The
anharmonic action

s_{\mathrm{A}}
of
H
is given by the ratio of the largest second and third directional derivative:

\displaystyle s_{\mathrm{A}}:=\left(\tau_{\mathrm{H}}|H^{\mathcal{Z}}|_{C^{3}}\right)^{-2}=\left(\frac{|H^{\mathcal{Z}}|_{C^{2}}}{|H^{\mathcal{Z}}|_{C^{3}}}\right)^{2}=\left(\frac{\mathcal{Q}^{2,2}[H^{\mathcal{Z}}]}{\mathcal{Q}^{3,3}[H^{\mathcal{Z}}]}\right)^{2},

(A.6)

We use the anharmonic action

s_{\mathrm{A}}
to define the
modified anharmonic action
70
70

70

For quadratic Hamiltonians,
s_{\mathrm{A}}=\tilde{s}_{\mathrm{A}}=\infty
by convention. This is the case of harmonic dynamics where quantum and classical evolution is identical on phase space.
of
H

\displaystyle\tilde{s}_{\mathrm{A}}:=\left(\tau_{\mathrm{H}}\mathcal{Q}^{3,2d+4}_{s_{\mathrm{A}}}[H^{\mathcal{Z}}]\right)^{-2}=\left(\frac{\mathcal{Q}^{2,2}[H^{\mathcal{Z}}]}{\mathcal{Q}^{3,2d+4}_{s_{\mathrm{A}}}[H^{\mathcal{Z}}]}\right)^{2}.

(A.7)

Given
\tau_{\mathrm{H}}
,
\mathcal{Z}
, and
\tilde{s}_{\mathrm{A}}
, we furthermore have a natural choice of
characteristic diffusion matrix
given by
D_{\mathrm{C}}=(\tilde{s}_{\mathrm{A}}/\tau_{\mathrm{H}})\mathcal{Z}\mathcal{Z}^{\top}
.

Intuitively, the harmonic time
\tau_{\mathrm{H}}
is the
shortest
timescale associated with the local harmonic approximation at any point in phase space.
When the harmonic time is long, the classical dynamics are slow compared to the quantum scale set by
\hbar
, and we expect they well approximate the quantum dynamics they correspond to.

The anharmonic action
s_{\mathrm{A}}
and modified anharmonic action
\tilde{s}_{\mathrm{A}}
are
not
measures of the accessible phase space. Rather, they measure the phase-space scale on which the anharmonicity of the potential is important over the harmonic time
\tau_{\mathrm{H}}
. For cubic potentials,
\tilde{s}_{\mathrm{A}}=s_{\mathrm{A}}
because the higher-order seminorms in (
A.7
) vanish. Introducing the higher order terms increases the denominator in
\tilde{s}_{\mathrm{A}}
, so
\tilde{s}_{\mathrm{A}}\leq s_{\mathrm{A}}
always holds.

We now prove a corollary of Theorem
3.1
making use of the scales defined in Definition
A.1
. The seminorm
\mathcal{Q}^{3,2d+4}_{\hbar}[H^{\mathcal{Z}}]
naturally arises in Theorem
3.1
, but through
\tilde{s}_{\mathrm{A}}
we will upper bound it with the factor of
\mathcal{Q}^{3,2d+4}_{s_{\mathrm{A}}}[H^{\mathcal{Z}}]\geq\mathcal{Q}^{3,2d+4}_{\hbar}[H^{\mathcal{Z}}
(assuming
s_{\mathrm{A}}\geq\hbar
). This loosens the bound, essentially throwing out the detailed information about how the anharmonic factors depend on higher powers of the action scale, but has the benefit of isolating the leading
\hbar^{4/3}
dependence, with everything else expressed in terms of (
\hbar
-independent) macroscopic properties of the classical Hamiltonian.

Corollary A.1

(Minimum diffusion for correspondence)
.

For
d
degrees of freedom, let
\hat{H}
be a quantum Hamiltonian function with bounded partial derivatives of degree
j=2,\ldots,2d+4
and corresponding classical Hamiltonian
H=\OpX_{\hbar}^{-1}[\hat{H}]
. Let
\mathcal{Z}
a symplectic matrix
and let

\tau_{\mathrm{H}}=1/\mathcal{Q}^{2,2}[H^{\mathcal{Z}}]
,

s_{\mathrm{A}}=(\mathcal{Q}^{2,2}[H^{\mathcal{Z}}]/\mathcal{Q}^{3,3}[H^{\mathcal{Z}}])^{2}
,

\tilde{s}_{\mathrm{A}}=(\mathcal{Q}^{2,2}[H^{\mathcal{Z}}]/\mathcal{Q}^{3,2d+4}_{s_{\mathrm{A}}}[H^{\mathcal{Z}}])^{2}
,

D_{\mathrm{C}}=(\tilde{s}_{\mathrm{A}}/\tau_{\mathrm{H}})\mathcal{Z}\mathcal{Z}^{\top}

be the harmonic time, anharmonic action, modified anharmonic action, and characteristic diffusion matrix of
H
from Definition
A.1
.
Assume
s_{\mathrm{A}}\geq\hbar
.
