Assume an initial state quantum state
\rho(t\mathord{=}0)
given as a mixture of Gaussian states with covariance matrix
\sigma=(\hbar/2)\mathcal{Z}\mathcal{Z}^{\top}
.
Finally, let
\rho(t)
and
f(t)
be the corresponding quantum and classical trajectory for the frictionless dynamics specified by
H
and a homogenous (i.e, constant over phase space) diffusion matrix
D
.
Then for any tolerable error growth rate
r
satisfying

r\geq\frac{C_{d}}{\tau_{\mathrm{H}}}\sqrt{\frac{\hbar}{\tilde{s}_{\mathrm{A}}}},

(A.8)

the diffusion strength constraint

\displaystyle D\geq\left(\frac{C_{d}}{\tau_{\mathrm{H}}r}\right)^{\frac{2}{3}}\left(\frac{\hbar}{\tilde{s}_{\mathrm{A}}}\right)^{\frac{4}{3}}D_{\mathrm{C}}

(A.9)

guarantees there exists a quantum trajectory
\tilde{\rho}(t)
with strictly positive Wigner function
\mathcal{W}_{\hbar}[\tilde{\rho}(t)]
such that

a.

\tilde{\rho}(t)
cannot be distinguished from
\rho(t)
with probability greater than
rt
by
any
quantum measurement; and

b.

\mathcal{W}_{\hbar}[\tilde{\rho}(t)]
cannot be distinguished from
f(t)
with probability greater than
rt
by
any
classical variable.

Above,
C_{d}
is the same universal dimensionless constant depending only on
d
from Theorem
3.1
.

Proof.

We can consult the definitions of the anharmonic factors in Eqs. (
3.15
), (
3.19
), and (
3.20
) to see that in the special case of linear Lindblad operators (
L_{k}=\ell_{k,a}\alpha^{a}
,
D_{ab}=\hbar\Omega_{ab}=\hbar\Real\sum_{k}{\ell_{k,a}^{*}}{\ell_{k,b}^{\phantom{*}}}
) they reduce to
just

\displaystyle B^{\mathrm{anh}}_{\mathrm{c}}[H^{\mathcal{Z}},\ell_{k,a}\alpha^{a}]

\displaystyle=\mathcal{Q}^{3,3}[H^{\mathcal{Z}}]

(A.10)

\displaystyle B^{\mathrm{anh}}_{\mathrm{q}}[H^{\mathcal{Z}},\ell_{k,a}\alpha^{a},\hbar]

\displaystyle=\mathcal{Q}^{3,2d+4}_{\hbar}[H^{\mathcal{Z}}]

(A.11)

\displaystyle B^{\mathrm{anh}}_{\mathrm{q}^{\prime}}[\ell_{k,a}\alpha^{a},\hbar,\nu]

\displaystyle=0

(A.12)

The dynamics are taken to be frictionless, so we will apply Theorem
3.1
with
Eq. (
3.11
) from Definition
3.2
for the relative diffusion strength. The key quantity is

\displaystyle\inf_{\alpha}\frac{\lambda_{\mathrm{min}}[\Omega^{\mathcal{Z}}(\alpha)]}{\lambda_{\mathrm{max}}[(\nabla^{2}H)^{\mathcal{Z}}(\alpha)]}

\displaystyle\geq\tau_{\mathrm{H}}\hbar^{-1}\lambda_{\mathrm{min}}[D^{\mathcal{Z}}(\alpha)]

(A.13)

\displaystyle\geq\tau_{\mathrm{H}}\hbar^{-1}\left(\frac{C_{d}}{\tau_{\mathrm{H}}r}\right)^{2/3}\left(\frac{\hbar}{\tilde{s}_{\mathrm{A}}}\right)^{4/3}\lambda_{\mathrm{min}}[D_{\mathrm{C}}^{\mathcal{Z}}]

(A.14)

\displaystyle\geq\left(\frac{C_{d}^{2}\hbar}{r^{2}\tau_{\mathrm{H}}^{2}\tilde{s}_{\mathrm{A}}}\right)^{1/3}

(A.15)

\displaystyle\geq 1

(A.16)

where we have sequentially applied the definitions

\tau_{\mathrm{H}}=\mathcal{Q}^{2,2}[H^{\mathcal{Z}}]
and
D=\hbar\Omega
,
the diffusion constraint (
A.9
), the fact that
\lambda_{\mathrm{min}}[D_{\mathrm{C}}^{\mathcal{Z}}]=\lambda_{\mathrm{min}}[(\tilde{s}_{\mathrm{A}}/\tau_{\mathrm{H}})\mathds{1}]=\tilde{s}_{\mathrm{A}}/\tau_{\mathrm{H}}
, and the error rate constraint (
A.8
). This means that the relative diffusion strength
g=1
so that, by Eqs. (
3.21
) and (
3.22
) of Theorem
3.1
we have that
\left\lVert\tilde{\rho}(t)-\rho(t)\right\rVert_{\mathrm{Tr}}
and
\left\lVert\mathcal{W}_{\hbar}[\tilde{\rho}(t)]-f(t)\right\rVert_{L^{1}}
are both upper bounded by

\displaystyle C_{d}\,t\,\hbar^{\frac{1}{2}}\mathcal{Q}^{3,2+4d}_{\hbar}[H^{\mathcal{Z}}]\leq r\,t

(A.17)

because
\tilde{s}_{\mathrm{A}}=(\tau_{\mathrm{H}}\mathcal{Q}^{3,2+4d}_{s_{\mathrm{A}}}[H^{\mathcal{Z}}])^{-2}
and
\mathcal{Q}^{q,r}_{\tilde{s}_{\mathrm{A}}}
is an increasing function of an action
\tilde{s}_{\mathrm{A}}\geq\hbar
.
∎

This corollary is more general than the result in our companion paper
[
48
]
because here we do not restrict to Hamiltonians of the form
H=p^{2}/2m+V(x)
, but it is weaker in that special case because it involves an unknown constant
C_{d}
.

Appendix B
Gaussian derivatives and integrals

B.1
Gaussian derivatives

The Gaussian probability distribution with mean
\alpha
and covariance matrix
\sigma
is

\displaystyle\tau_{\alpha,\sigma}(\alpha+\beta)=\frac{e^{-\beta^{\top}\sigma^{-1}\beta/2}}{(2\pi)^{d}\sqrt{\det\sigma}}=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{d}\sqrt{\det\sigma}}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2}\beta^{a}\sigma^{-1}_{ab}\beta^{b}\right)

(B.1)

Let us consider this a real-valued function of any vector
\beta
and any invertible matrix
\sigma
, including non-symmetric ones, so that
\sigma_{ab}
and
\sigma_{ba}
are independent variables for the purposes of partial derivatives. However, at the end we will evaluate these derivatives on the subspace where
\sigma
is symmetric.
Recalling our notation
\partial_{c}=\partial/\partial\beta^{c}
so
\partial_{c}\beta^{a}=\delta_{c}^{\phantom{a}a}
,
we have

\displaystyle\partial_{d}\left(\beta^{a}\sigma^{-1}_{ab}\beta^{b}\right)\,

\displaystyle=\sigma^{-1}_{db}\beta^{b}+\beta^{a}\sigma^{-1}_{ad}

(B.2)

\displaystyle\partial_{c}\partial_{d}\left(\beta^{a}\sigma^{-1}_{ab}\beta^{b}\right)\,

\displaystyle=\sigma^{-1}_{dc}+\sigma^{-1}_{cd}.

(B.3)

so that

\displaystyle\partial_{a}\tau_{\alpha,\sigma}(\alpha+\beta)=-\sigma^{-1}_{ab}\beta^{b}\tau_{\alpha,\sigma}(\alpha+\beta)

(B.4)

when
\sigma
is symmetric.
We also deploy the standard
[
114
]
matrix derivative identities
71
71

71

Some sources express will express this for a not-necessarily invertible matrix
Z
using the matrix adjugate
\mathrm{adj}(Z)
. A property of the adjugate is that
Z\,\mathrm{adj}(Z)=(\det Z)\mathds{1}
so that, when
Z
is invertible,
\mathrm{adj}(Z)=(\det Z)Z^{-1}.

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\det Z}{\partial y}

\displaystyle=(\det Z)\Tr\left[Z^{-1}\frac{\partial Z}{\partial y}\right],

(B.5)

\displaystyle\frac{\partial Z^{-1}}{\partial y}

\displaystyle=-Z^{-1}\frac{\partial Z}{\partial y}Z^{-1}

(B.6)

for an invertible matrix
Z
, so in particular

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\det Z}{\partial Z^{ab}}

\displaystyle=(\det Z)Z^{-1}_{ba},

(B.7)

\displaystyle\frac{\partial Z^{-1}_{cd}}{\partial Z^{ab}}

\displaystyle=-Z^{-1}_{ca}Z^{-1}_{bd}.

(B.8)

Combining these we get

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\partial_{a}\partial_{b}\tau_{\alpha,\sigma}(\alpha+\beta)=\frac{1}{2}(\sigma^{-1}_{ac}\beta^{c}\sigma^{-1}_{bd}\beta^{d}-\sigma^{-1}_{ab})\tau_{\alpha,\sigma}(\alpha+\beta)=\frac{\partial}{\partial\sigma^{ab}}\tau_{\alpha,\sigma}(\alpha+\beta),

(B.9)

when evaluated for symmetric
\sigma
. (As expected, this is singular when
\sigma
is non-invertible.) Weyl quantizing both sides with
\mathcal{W}_{\hbar}^{-1}=(2\pi\hbar)^{d}\OpX_{\hbar}
gives the corresponding quantum expression
\partial_{a}\partial_{b}\hat{\tau}_{\alpha,\sigma}=2\frac{\partial}{\partial\sigma_{ab}}\hat{\tau}_{\alpha,\sigma}
.

B.2
Gaussian integrals

Here we recall the evaluation of some Gaussian integrals, as can be done with Wick’s theorem. We define the shorthand:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\langle(\beta^{\top}A\beta)\rangle_{\sigma}:=&\int\!\mathrm{d}\beta\,\tau_{0,\sigma}(\beta)(\beta^{\top}A\beta)\\
=&\int\!\mathrm{d}\beta\,\tau_{\alpha,\sigma}(\alpha+\beta)(\beta^{\top}A\beta)\\
=&A_{ab}\int\!\mathrm{d}\beta\,\tau_{\alpha,\sigma}(\alpha+\beta)\beta^{a}\beta^{b}\\
=&A_{ab}\sigma^{ab}\\
=&\Tr[\sigma A].\end{split}

(B.10)

for any positive semidefinite matrix
A
. (
\sigma
is also positive, of course.) Likewise, for
B
,
C
, and
D
also positive semidefinite, we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}\langle(\beta^{\top}A\beta)(\beta^{\top}B\beta)\rangle_{\sigma}:=&\int\!\mathrm{d}\beta\,\tau_{\alpha,\sigma}(\alpha+\beta)(\beta^{\top}A\beta)(\beta^{\top}B\beta)\\
=&A_{ab}B_{cd}\left[\sigma^{ab}\sigma^{cd}+2\sigma^{ad}\sigma^{bc}\right]\\
=&\Tr[\sigma A]\Tr[\sigma B]+2\Tr[\sigma A\sigma B]\end{split}

(B.11)

and

\displaystyle\begin{split}\langle(\beta^{\top}A\beta)(\beta^{\top}B\beta)(\beta^{\top}C\beta)\rangle_{\sigma}:=&\int\!\mathrm{d}\beta\,\tau_{\alpha,\sigma}(\alpha+\beta)(\beta^{\top}A\beta)(\beta^{\top}B\beta)(\beta^{\top}C\beta)\\
=&A_{ab}B_{cd}C_{ef}\Big[\sigma^{ab}\sigma^{cd}\sigma^{ef}+2\left(\sigma^{ab}\sigma^{cf}\sigma^{de}+\sigma^{af}\sigma^{cd}\sigma^{be}+\sigma^{ad}\sigma^{bc}\sigma^{ef}\right)\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad+4\left(\sigma^{ad}\sigma^{be}\sigma^{cf}+\sigma^{af}\sigma^{bc}\sigma^{de}\right)\Big]\\
=&\Tr[\sigma A]\Tr[\sigma B]\Tr[\sigma C]+2\Tr[\sigma A]\Tr[\sigma B\sigma C]+2\Tr[\sigma B]\Tr[\sigma C\sigma A]\\
&\qquad+2\Tr[\sigma C]\Tr[\sigma B\sigma A]+8\Tr[\sigma A\sigma B\sigma C].\end{split}

(B.12)

and

\displaystyle\begin{split}\langle(\beta^{\top}A\beta)&(\beta^{\top}B\beta)(\beta^{\top}C\beta)(\beta^{\top}D\beta)\rangle_{\sigma}\\
:=&\int\!\mathrm{d}\beta\,\tau_{\alpha,\sigma}(\alpha+\beta)(\beta^{\top}A\beta)(\beta^{\top}B\beta)(\beta^{\top}C\beta)(\beta^{\top}D\beta)\\
=&A_{ab}B_{cd}C_{ef}D_{gh}\Big[\sigma^{ab}\sigma^{cd}\sigma^{ef}\sigma^{gh}\\
&+2\big(\sigma^{ab}\sigma^{cd}\sigma^{eg}\sigma^{fh}+\sigma^{ab}\sigma^{ce}\sigma^{df}\sigma^{gh}+\sigma^{ac}\sigma^{bd}\sigma^{ef}\sigma^{gh}+\\
&\qquad\sigma^{ab}\sigma^{cg}\sigma^{ef}\sigma^{dh}+\sigma^{ae}\sigma^{cd}\sigma^{bf}\sigma^{gh}+\sigma^{ag}\sigma^{cd}\sigma^{ef}\sigma^{bh}\big)\\
&+4\big(\sigma^{ac}\sigma^{bd}\sigma^{eg}\sigma^{fh}+\sigma^{ae}\sigma^{cg}\sigma^{bf}\sigma^{dh}+\sigma^{ag}\sigma^{ce}\sigma^{df}\sigma^{bh}\big)\\
&+8\big(\sigma^{ac}\sigma^{de}\sigma^{fb}\sigma^{gh}+\sigma^{ac}\sigma^{dg}\sigma^{ef}\sigma^{gb}+\sigma^{ae}\sigma^{cd}\sigma^{fg}\sigma^{hb}+\sigma^{ab}\sigma^{ce}\sigma^{fg}\sigma^{hd}\big)\\
&+16\big(\sigma^{ac}\sigma^{de}\sigma^{fg}\sigma^{hb}+\sigma^{ae}\sigma^{fc}\sigma^{dg}\sigma^{hb}+\sigma^{ac}\sigma^{dg}\sigma^{he}\sigma^{fb}\big)\Big]\\
=&\Tr[\sigma A]\Tr[\sigma B]\Tr[\sigma C]\Tr[\sigma D]+\\
&+2\big(\Tr[\sigma A]\Tr[\sigma B]\Tr[\sigma C\sigma D]+\Tr[\sigma A]\Tr[\sigma D]\Tr[\sigma B\sigma C]+\Tr[\sigma C]\Tr[\sigma D]\Tr[\sigma A\sigma B]+\\
&\qquad\Tr[\sigma A]\Tr[\sigma C]\Tr[\sigma B\sigma D]+\Tr[\sigma B]\Tr[\sigma D]\Tr[\sigma A\sigma C]+\Tr[\sigma B]\Tr[\sigma C]\Tr[\sigma A\sigma D]\big)\\
&+4\big(\Tr[\sigma A\sigma B]\Tr[\sigma C\sigma D]+\Tr[\sigma A\sigma C]\Tr[\sigma B\sigma D]+\Tr[\sigma A\sigma D]\Tr[\sigma B\sigma C]\big)\\
&+8\big(\Tr[\sigma A\sigma B\sigma C]\Tr[\sigma D]+\Tr[\sigma A\sigma B\sigma D]\Tr[\sigma C]+\Tr[\sigma A\sigma C\sigma D]\Tr[\sigma B]+\Tr[\sigma B\sigma C\sigma D]\Tr[\sigma A]\big)\\
&+16\big(\Tr[\sigma A\sigma B\sigma C\sigma D]+\Tr[\sigma A\sigma C\sigma B\sigma D]+\Tr[\sigma A\sigma B\sigma D\sigma C]\big)\Big].\end{split}

(B.13)
